Theta operators on Hodge type Shimura varieties
Martin Ortiz

Abstract
. We construct a new family of mod p p weight shifting differential operators on Hodge type Shimura varieties at hyperspecial level. First we construct basic theta operators, labelled by positive roots, that generalize Katz’s theta operator for modular forms. Secondly we construct theta linkage maps, these are operators between automorphic vector bundles with linked weights, which can be thought of as generalizations of the classical theta cycle of Tate–Jochnowitz. In particular, there exist such maps within the p p -restricted region, whose weight shifts are directly related to the conjectures of Herzig on the weight part of Serre’s conjecture. We explain the relation between the two operators, and we prove some properties about them, e.g. the injectivity of some of them in a generic locus of the p p -restricted region. As an application, we produce an example of a generic entailment of Serre weights for the groups GL 4 , ℚ p \textnormal{GL}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}} and U ​ ( 4 ) ℚ p U(4)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}} , by combining the method of [ 61 ] with our stronger results about theta operators.



1.
Introduction

In this article we construct a general family of theta operators on the special fibers of Hodge type Shimura varieties. Let us first
recall the original case of mod
p
modular forms, as it will serve us as a template to generalize. Let
p
be a prime
and let
X_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
be the compactified modular curve at level prime to
p
. Let
\omega
be the Hodge bundle over
X_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The first mod
p
theta operator was defined by Katz
[
42
]
for mod
p
modular forms as a map of sheaves

\theta:\omega^{k}\to\omega^{k+p+1}
over
X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. In the same paper he proved some of its important properties.

Proposition 1.0.1
.

(1)

The map
\theta
is Hecke equivariant up to a twist, i.e.
T_{l}\theta=l\theta T_{l}
for
l\neq p
,
where
T_{l}
are the classical
Hecke operators.

(2)

Let
H
be the Hasse invariant. Then
\theta(H)=0
, so that
\theta
is Hasse-linear, i.e. for any form
f\in\omega^{k}
,
\theta(Hf)=H\theta(f)
.

(3)

Let
\theta^{p}
denote the
p
-fold iterate of
\theta
, then
\theta^{p}=H^{p+1}\theta
.

(4)

Let
f\in\omega^{k}
be a local section over
X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
which is not divisible by
H
, i.e.
there does not exist a local section
g\in\omega^{k-p+1}
such that

f=Hg
. Then
H\mid\theta(f)
if and only
if
p\mid k
.

(5)

The map
\theta:\textnormal{H}^{0}(X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}},\omega^{k})\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}},\omega^{k+p+1})

is injective for
k<p-1
.

The relevance of point
(5)
is that one can think of
\textnormal{H}^{0}(X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}},\omega^{k})
as spaces of mod
p
modular forms,
and the theta operator is producing congruences between forms of different weights. Both
(2)
and
(4)
are used to prove

(5)
. Moreover, using
(3)
and
(4)
Jochnowitz
[
38
]
and Tate computed the theta cycle for modular forms of low weight. That is,
one repeatedly applies
\theta
to an eigenform, studying if at some point the form becomes
divisible by the Hasse invariant. By the relation
\theta^{p}=H^{p+1}\theta
this must eventually occur.
Part of their result can be rephrased as follows.

Proposition 1.0.2
.

(The theta cycle) Let
1\leq k\leq p
. The map
\theta^{p-k+1}:\omega^{k}\to\omega^{k+(p-k+1)(p+1)}
factors through
H^{p-k+1}:\omega^{2p-k+2}\to\omega^{k+(p-k+1)(p+1)}
via a map
\Theta:\omega^{k}\to\omega^{2p-k+2}
.

For us, although this is not
standard terminology, we will say that
\Theta
is
the
theta cycle map
.
Moreover, in the case that
f
is an eigenform of weight
k\leq p
which is non-ordinary, Edixhoven
[
16
]

proved that
H\mid\Theta(f)
, thus obtaining a form of weight
p-k+3
. This partially proved the weight part of Serre’s conjecture
for modular forms, and we see it as one of the benchmarks for applications of mod
p
theta operators.
In this article we will generalize the two propositions above. We will denote the generalizations of
\theta

as
basic theta operators
and the generalizations of
\Theta
as
theta linkage maps
.
We will work with Hodge type Shimura varieties.

These are those for which the Shimura datum
(G,X)

embeds into the one for the Siegel Shimura variety. Let
K\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})

be a neat level which is hyperspecial at
p
. That is, there exists a reductive model
\mathcal{G}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
such that

K=K^{p}K_{p}
with
K_{p}=\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. From now on we suppress
\mathcal{G}
from
the notation. Given
G
, the existence of some
K
hyperspecial at
p

is equivalent to
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
being unramified.
By work of Kisin
[
44
]
there exists
a smooth integral model
Sh
for the Shimura variety attached to
(G,X,K)
, defined
over the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
of a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Let
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
be its geometric special fiber, and
\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}
a choice of toroidal compactification.

Let
\mu
be the Hodge cocharacter and
P
a choice of
parabolic of
G
corresponding to
\mu
. Let
M
be the Levi of
P
. We fix a choice
T\subseteq B\subset P\subseteq G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
of
maximal torus and Borel. Let
\omega=e^{*}\Omega^{1}_{A/\textnormal{Sh}}
and
let
\omega\to\mathcal{H}:=\textnormal{H}^{1}_{\textnormal{dR}}(A/\textnormal{Sh})\to\omega^{\vee}_{A^{\vee}}

be the Hodge filtration.
Kisin
[
44
, Cor 2.3.9]
proves the existence of some Hodge tensors

s_{\textnormal{dR}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes}
in such a way that the sheaf
G_{\textnormal{dR}}
consisting of
trivializations of
\mathcal{H}
respecting
s_{\textnormal{dR}}
(
2.2.8
) becomes a
G
-torsor over
Sh
.
Then the Hodge filtration defines a
P
-subtorsor
P_{\textnormal{dR}}\subseteq G_{\textnormal{dR}}
over
Sh
.
For
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
let
W(\lambda)\in\textnormal{Rep}(M)

be the dual Weyl module for
M
of highest weight
\lambda
. We define
\omega(\lambda)=P_{\textnormal{dR}}\times^{P}W(\lambda)

as the vector bundle
on
\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}
associated to
W(\lambda)
via
P_{\textnormal{dR}}
.

As in
[
24
]
we will work with the flag Shimura variety
\pi:\mathcal{F}l:=[B\backslash P_{\textnormal{dR}}]\to\textnormal{Sh}
(
2.2.9
), with the property that its fibers
are isomorphic to the partial flag variety
P/B
.
Given
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
there exists an automorphic line bundle
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)

over
\mathcal{F}l
such that
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\omega(w_{0,M}\lambda)
, where
w_{0,M}\in W_{M}
is the longest element. We will construct our operators on
\mathcal{F}l
, which
then can be pushed forward to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

1.1.
Main results

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum of Hodge type, and
K=K^{p}K_{p}
a neat hyperspecial level at
p
.
We assume that
(G,X)
satisfies Deligne’s axioms (SV1,2,3)
[
58
, §6]

[
14
]
.
Assuming SV3 is harmless for our purposes. Namely, a
\mathbb{Q}
-simple
factor of compact type of
G^{\text{ad}}
does not contribute to the geometry of the Shimura variety.
We will also use
G
to denote the associated reductive model over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
We will always consider
\mathcal{F}l
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in this section.
We construct two kinds of theta operators, basic theta operators and theta linkage maps. Since we will repeatedly use
this concept in this introduction,
let us record our definition of
generic weights
.

Definition 1.1.1
.

Let
G/\mathbb{Q}
be a reductive group. We fix
a maximal torus and a Borel
T\subseteq B\subseteq G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

(1)

Let
\epsilon\geq 0
. We say that
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
is
\epsilon
-generic with respect to a prime
p
if
for each
\gamma\in\Phi
, there exists an integer
n_{\gamma}
such that

n_{\gamma}p+\epsilon<\langle\lambda,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle<(n_{\gamma}+1)p-\epsilon.

(2)

Fix some Shimura datum of Hodge type
(G,X)
.
Let
X=\{X_{p,K}\}_{p,K}
be a family of statements ranging over all rational primes
p
and levels
K\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})

which are hyperspecial at
p
, concerning

\textnormal{Sh}_{K,\mathcal{O}}
(where
\mathcal{O}
lives over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
) and some weights
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
.
We say that
X
holds for
generic

\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
if there exists
\epsilon>0
only depending on

G_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that each statement
X_{p,K}
holds for weights
\lambda
which are
\epsilon
-generic with respect to
p
.

(3)

Similarly, fix
H
a choice of a reductive root datum with a finite cyclic group
\Gamma
acting on it.
For each prime
p
let
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
be the reductive
group over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
given by
(H,\Gamma)
. Choose some maximal torus
T\subset G
.
Let
X=\{X_{p}\}_{p}
be a family of statements about each
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
and some weights
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
.
We say that
X
holds for generic
\lambda
if there exists an
\epsilon
depending only on
(H,\Gamma)
such that
each
X_{p}
holds for weights
\lambda
which are
\epsilon
-generic with respect to
p
.

We refer to
Section

2.1
for all the representation theoretic notation.

1.1.1.
Basic theta operators

The basic theta operators are the natural generalization of Katz’s theta operator, as well as of all other mod

p
theta operators previously defined in the literature.
For each simple root
\alpha\in\Delta
there exists a Hasse invariant
H_{\alpha}
on
\mathcal{F}l

[
34
]
. We note that

G^{\textnormal{der}}
is always simply connected for Shimura varieties of PEL type A or C.

Theorem 1.1.2
.

Let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
be a positive root. For each
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)

there exists a basic theta operator

\theta_{\gamma}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\mu_{\gamma})
, which is a differential operator
over
\mathcal{F}l_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
, and
\mu_{\gamma}
is an explicit weight depending on
\gamma
. They extend to toroidal
compactifications.
They satisfy the following properties.

(1)

They are Hecke equivariant away from
p
.

(2)

For each
\alpha\in\Delta
,
\theta_{\gamma}(H_{\alpha})=0
.

(3)

For
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Phi^{+}
we have the commutation relations

[\theta_{\gamma_{1}},\theta_{\gamma_{2}}]=\theta_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}
up to multiplying the right-hand side
by an explicit collection of Hasse invariants and a small explicit constant. If
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}\notin\Phi^{+}
,
the right-hand side is zero by convention.

(4)

Assume that
G^{\textnormal{der}}
is simply connected. Let
\{G_{i}\}
be the almost-simple factors of

G^{\textnormal{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
, and fix such an
i
.
The Frobenius in
\textnormal{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p})

maps
G_{i}
isomorphically to
G_{j}
for some
j
.
Suppose that
there exists
\alpha\in\Delta_{G_{i}}
not contained in

\textnormal{Lie}(M)
, and let
\delta
be the longest root of
G_{j}
. Then if
G_{i}
has rank at least
2

\theta^{p}_{\alpha}=H^{p}_{\alpha}\theta_{\delta}.

(5)

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
, and assume that
H_{\alpha}
has a simple zero
(this holds
if
G^{\textnormal{der}}
is simply connected).
Let
f\in\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
be a local section, then

H_{\alpha}\mid\theta_{\alpha}(f)
if and only if
p\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
or
H_{\alpha}\mid f
.

(6)

Assume that
G^{\textnormal{der}}
is simply connected, and that
2.8.2
(2) holds.
Then the map

\theta_{\gamma}:\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda))\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda+w_{0,M}\mu_{\gamma}))

is injective for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
.

Here
2.8.2
(2) concerns the vanishing of
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda))

for weights
\lambda

which are generically away from the dominant cone.
It is implied in many cases by the cone conjecture of Goldring–Koskivirta

[
25
]
, which has been proved in some low rank examples, as well as in the Siegel case

[
46
]
. This theorem is the amalgamation of

4.2.7
,
Theorem

4.3.2
,
Theorem

4.4.2
, and
Theorem

4.4.7

in that order,
where we give more precise statements. See
4.2.4
and
Lemma

4.1.1
for how to compute

\mu_{\gamma}
.
We would like to draw parallels between
the case of the modular curve in
1.0.1
and this theorem. Point
(3)
above
does not appear in the case of the modular curve since it is only relevant
for groups of higher rank.
We also note that some of the operators become linear
after pushing them forward to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
, these are precisely
\theta_{\gamma}

for
\gamma\in\textnormal{Lie}(U_{B})\backslash\textnormal{Lie}(U_{P})
.

Remark 1.1.3
.

To prove points
(5)
and
(6)
we in fact prove a more precise result about the restriction of basic theta operators
to codimension
1
strata, e.g.
4.4.4
. This could be seen as a first step towards the
study of differential operators on EO strata.

We illustrate the weight shifts of the basic theta operators and Hasse invariants in the case of
G=\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
.
Weights are given by a tuple
(k,l,w)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
with
w=k+l\bmod{2}
, and then for
k\geq l

we have
\omega(k,l,w)=\textnormal{Sym}^{k-l}\omega\otimes\det^{l}\omega\otimes\delta^{(w-k-l)/2}
, where

\delta:=\omega(0,0,2)
. It is a non-canonically trivial line bundle which twists the Hecke action, and

\delta^{p-1}
is canonically trivial over
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.
The simple roots are
\alpha=(1,-1,0)
and
\beta=(0,2,0)
, and the basic theta operators are

•

\pi_{*}\theta_{\beta}:\omega(\lambda)\to\omega(\lambda+(p+1,p-1,0))

•

\pi_{*}\theta_{\alpha}:\omega(\lambda)\to\omega(\lambda+(p-1,0,p-1))

•

\pi_{*}\theta_{\alpha+\beta}:\omega(\lambda)\to\omega(\lambda+(2p,p-1,p-1))

•

\pi_{*}\theta_{2\alpha+\beta}:\omega(\lambda)\to\omega(\lambda+(2p,0,2p-2))
.

Note that only the weight shift of
\pi_{*}\theta_{\alpha}
does not introduce a twist by
\delta
.
The arrows in
Figure

1
illustrate these weight shifts, where
we are suppressing the central character. The large parallelogram
roughly corresponds to the

p
-restricted weights. It will be a phenomenon that the operators only produce congruences between

p
-restricted weights for non-generic weights, i.e. those very close to the boundary of the
p
-restricted region.
Here
\pi_{*}\theta_{\beta}
was already defined by
[
75
]
and
[
17
]
, but the others
are new. In the setup of
[
17
]
,
\pi_{*}\theta_{\alpha}
appearing in point
(4)
agrees
with their theta operator.

\bullet
k
l
0
(p,0)
(p,p)
(2p,p)

H_{\alpha}
\theta_{\beta}
\theta_{\alpha}
\theta_{2\alpha+\beta}
\theta_{\alpha+\beta}

1.1.2.
The theta linkage maps

In order to get maps between
p
-restricted weights we can use
theta linkage maps
. On one hand, these generalize
the theta cycle map
\Theta:\omega^{k}\to\omega^{2p-k+2}

from the modular curve case, and on the other hand they pair nicely with the modular representation theory appearing
in the setup of mod
p
algebraic automorphic forms.
Let
\rho
be the half sum of positive roots for
G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
.
We can divide weight space
X^{*}(T)\otimes\mathbb{R}
into

\rho
-shifted alcoves, defined as the connected components of weight space after dividing it by the
hyperplanes
H_{\gamma,n}:\{\lambda:np=\langle\lambda+\rho,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

and
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
. The affine Weyl group
W_{\text{aff}}=p\mathbb{Z}\Phi^{+}\rtimes W

acts transitively on the alcoves, and
it is generated by reflections
s_{\gamma,n}:\lambda\mapsto\lambda-(\langle\lambda+\rho,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle-pn)\gamma
. We say that
\lambda\uparrow\mu
(
\mu
is linked
to
\lambda
) if there is a sequence
\lambda=\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{k}=\mu
such that

\lambda_{i+1}=s_{\gamma_{i},n_{i}}\cdot\lambda_{i}
for some
\gamma_{i}
and
n_{i}
.
We say that
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu
if
\mu=s_{\gamma,n}\cdot\lambda
such that
n
is the smallest integer
for which
\lambda\leq s_{\gamma,n}\cdot\lambda
. In this case
\mu
is a reflection across
the closest
\gamma
-hyperplane to
\lambda
, such that it moves in the positive root direction.

Theorem 1.1.4

(
5.1.3
,
5.1.6
)
.

Assume that
G^{\text{der}}
is simply connected.
Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)
such that
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu
. Then there exists a differential
operator

\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}:\mathcal{L}(-\mu)\to\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)
on
\mathcal{F}l_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. It is Hecke
equivariant away from
p
, and after post-composing
\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
with
some number of Hasse invariants it is identified with a combination of basic theta operators.

We denote these operators by
theta linkage maps
. For general
\lambda\uparrow\mu
,
by composing the maps above we can construct a map
\mathcal{L}(-\mu)\to\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)
, but
we don’t know if this is independent of the chain of reflections going from
\lambda
to
\mu
.
Even in the setup of the theorem we cannot prove in general that these operators are non-zero.
The last sentence in the statement means
that there is an element
f\in U\mathfrak{u}_{B}
, which can be thought of as a
non-commutative polynomial on the positive roots
\{x_{\gamma}\}
,
such that when evaluating it on the basic theta operators
\{\theta_{\gamma}\}
we get

f([\theta_{\gamma}]_{\gamma\in\Phi^{+}})=\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta}H^{n_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}

for some

n_{\alpha}\geq 0
. This clearly generalizes the behaviour of the theta cycle map
\Theta=\theta^{k-1}/H^{k-1}:\omega^{k}\to\omega^{2p-k+2}

for the modular curve,
but in general multiple
basic theta operators are required in this combination. Only in the case that
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu
and

\gamma
is a simple root,
then
\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
will be a power of a single basic theta operator. We call
these
simple theta linkage maps
.
Figure

2
illustrates the
3
linkage maps within
the
p
-restricted region for
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
. Because of the twist
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\omega(w_{0,M}\lambda)
we get
maps
\omega(\lambda_{i}+\eta)\to\omega(\lambda_{i+1}+\eta)
, where
\eta=(3,3)
.
The one going from
\lambda_{1}
to
\lambda_{2}

is the pushforward of a simple theta linkage map (
w_{0,M}
corresponds to the
reflection across the
x=y
axis up to a shift by
\rho
).

\bullet
-\rho
\beta
\alpha
\scriptstyle\lambda_{0}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\scriptstyle\lambda_{1}
\scriptstyle\lambda_{2}
\scriptstyle\lambda_{3}

Figure 2.

In general theta linkage maps are more difficult to study than
basic theta operators. For instance, they are of higher degree as differential operators,
and they don’t necessarily commute with Hasse invariants
in any reasonable way, so it does not make sense to restrict them to codimension
1
strata.
In the case of simple theta linkage maps we can say a bit more, thanks to
point
(4)
in
Theorem

1.1.2
. We will use a relabelling of the theta linkage maps which is more
suitable for applications.

Theorem 1.1.5

(
Theorem

5.1.11
)
.

Assume that
G^{\textnormal{der}}
is simply connected and that
2.8.2
(2) holds.
Let
G_{i}
be an almost-simple factor of
G^{\textnormal{der}}

and suppose that there exists

\alpha\in\Delta_{G_{i}}
which is not in
\textnormal{Lie}(M)
. Let
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
and
\mu\in X^{*}(T)

such that
\lambda\uparrow_{w_{0,M}\alpha}\mu
.
Then

\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}:=\theta^{-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta\uparrow-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta}:\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\mu+\eta))

is injective for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
. Here
\eta
is the weight of the canonical bundle of
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

In the setup from above, such an
\alpha
is unique.
For the other simple roots
\beta\in\Delta_{G_{i}}
, we have that
\theta^{p}_{\beta}=0
and

\pi_{*}\theta^{-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta\uparrow-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta}
is a linear map between automorphic vector bundles
coming from a map of
P
-representations,
so those are not as interesting.

1.1.3.
(Generic) entailments

As an application of all of the previous machinery we prove some examples of entailments of Serre weights, generalizing
[
61
]
. We remark that these
could not have been proved using only the techniques of
[
61
]
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbb{T}
be a mod
p
Hecke eigensystem appearing in the étale cohomology
of
\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
. We define its set of modular Serre weights to be

W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}):=\{F(\lambda):\textnormal{H}^{\bullet}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\underline{F}(\lambda))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\}.

An entailment of Serre weights occurs when
there exist
p
-restricted weights
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}\in X_{1}(T)
such that for all
\mathfrak{m}
we have

F(\lambda)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(\lambda^{\prime})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
. We will
relax the definition by allowing ourselves to restrict to sufficiently generic
\mathfrak{m}
.
We will consider generic non-Eisenstein
\mathfrak{m}
as defined below.

Definition 1.1.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be a maximal ideal. Assume that all the almost simple factors of
G^{\textnormal{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
are of type
A
or
C_{2}

and that we are in the setup of
Section

7.1
.
We say that
\mathfrak{m}
is non-Eisenstein if its associated mod
p
global Galois representation

\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}:\textnormal{Gal}_{F}\to\check{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
is irreducible. In the case that
\check{G}=\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
we say that

\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is irreducible if it is so as a
\textnormal{GL}_{4}
-valued representation.
We say that

\mathfrak{m}
is generic if there exists an auxiliary prime
l\neq p
such that the restriction of
\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}

to the decomposition groups of primes above
l
is generic in the sense of
[
28
, Def 1.1]
.

If for some
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)

there exists a set
\{\lambda_{i}\}\subseteq X_{1}(T)
not containing
\lambda
such that
for all generic non-Eisenstein
\mathfrak{m}
we have that

F(\lambda)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(\lambda_{i})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})

for some
i
(which may depend on
\mathfrak{m}
), we say
that it is a
weak entailment
. Finally, we will say a (weak) entailment is generic if
the implication
F(\lambda)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(\lambda_{i})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})

holds for
generic

\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
as in
1.1.1
.
We prove some examples of generic weak entailments using theta linkage maps.

In
[
61
]
we proved a generic weak entailment for
\textnormal{GSp}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
In the next theorem, let
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
be a compact unitary Shimura variety such that
G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}=\textnormal{GL}_{4}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
When working with unitary Shimura varieties we will often identify the representation theory of
G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
with
the one of
\textnormal{GL}_{n}
, suppressing the twist coming from the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
In this case there are
6

p
-restricted alcoves
C_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq 5
. Given
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
,
let
\lambda_{i}\in C_{i}
be the corresponding affine Weyl translates.

Theorem 1.1.7
.

[
7.2.6
]
Let
\textnormal{Sh}/\mathcal{O}
be a compact unitary Shimura variety of signature
(3,1)

with
p
split in the quadratic imaginary field.
Let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be a generic non-Eisenstein maximal ideal.
Then the statement

F(\lambda_{0})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(\mu)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})

for some
F(\mu)\in\textnormal{JH}[V(\lambda_{5})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]
holds for
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
generic.
Moreover,

F(\mu)\neq F(\lambda_{0})
.
The same result holds for
p
inert if
2.8.2
(1) holds.

In the case that
p
is inert
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=GU(4)
with respect to
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}

is a non-split unitary group, which have been much less studied
than split groups. A result like this might suggest that the phenomenon of entailments
is more related to the representation theory of
G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
as opposed to the one of
G(\mathbb{F}_{p})
.
For
GU(4)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}

2.8.2
(1) is not known, but it is nevertheless
within reach with the current techniques
1
1

1

In fact
2.8.2
(1) is implied
by the long-announced work of
[
10
]
.
.

Remark 1.1.8
.

Assuming the weight part of Serre’s conjecture as in
[
21
, Conj 3.2.7]
these entailments are
related to certain Breuil–Mezard cycles containing more than one irreducible component
of the reduced Emerton–Gee stack. Then
Theorem

1.1.7
would conjecturally imply that for generic
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
we have a containment
of Breuil–Mezard cycles

\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{0})}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{V(\lambda_{5})}.

Based on
[
19
]
, in work in progress
[
53
]
prove that if the
Breuil–Mezard conjecture is
true, then for generic
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
(see
1.1.1
(3)) one must have

\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{0})}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{5})}
for
\textnormal{GL}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
Moreover, their results would imply that this is the only generic entailment for
\textnormal{GL}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
,
the generic (weak) entailment for
\textnormal{GSp}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
proved in
[
61
]
is the only one,
and no generic entailment exists for
\textnormal{GL}_{3}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Thus, we are able to detect the generic (weak) entailments for the two unramified examples of lowest rank.

We can also produce non-generic (weak) entailments,
using the linkage maps, basic theta operators,
and Hasse invariants. Here a non-generic entailment is defined as an entailment that holds
for a class of weights
which is not generic. Informally, they only hold for a particular class of weights close to the boundary
of the alcoves.
Moreover, we can construct examples of interesting congruences between forms with non-regular
weights and forms with regular weights. We give examples of these for
\textnormal{GL}_{3}
in

Theorem

7.3.3
.

1.1.4.
The de Rham realization functor

As an outcome of the way we construct the theta linkage maps, in
Section

8
we will also construct
exact functors

(1.1.1)

\Psi:D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{P,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\xrightarrow{f}D^{b}((\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})_{\textnormal{crys}})\xrightarrow{g}D^{b}(C_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}).

Here
\mathcal{O}_{P,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
is a category of
(U\mathfrak{g},P)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
-modules modelled after the classical complex category
\mathcal{O}
,

(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})_{\textnormal{crys}}
is the crystalline topos over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, and
C_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
is the category
of
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
-modules with
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-linear maps. The image of
f
lands in the subcategory
of complexes of crystals on

(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})_{\textnormal{crys}}
.
The map
g
in (
8.1.3
) is the pushforward
from the crystalline topos
to the Zariski topos. Moreover,
\Psi
extends to toroidal compactifications.
For
W\in\textnormal{Rep}(P)
let
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(W)=U\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{U\mathfrak{p}}W
be a parabolic Verma module.
The functor satisfies that for V a
G
-representation
\Psi([V])=\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
is the de Rham complex of the associated
vector bundle with connection
\mathcal{V}:=G^{\textnormal{dR}}\times^{G}V
. Thus,
\Psi
computes de Rham cohomology.
It also satisfies that
\Psi(\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(W)[0])=\mathcal{W}^{\vee}[0]
where
\mathcal{W}:=P_{\textnormal{dR}}\times^{P}W
is the
vector bundle associated to
W
. Moreover,
maps between parabolic Verma modules are sent by
\Psi
to
differential operators (these are often theta linkage maps, see
Section

1.2.1
).
We will use this functor in a companion paper
[
63
]
to compute de Rham cohomology of some low dimensional Shimura varieties
in terms of generalized mod
p
BGG decompositions.

1.2.
The methods

1.2.1.
Verma modules and differential operators

The construction of all the operators becomes a simple representation/group theoretic problem
once we establish
the following relation between Verma modules and differential operators on the (flag) Shimura variety.
For a smooth scheme
X/S
such that
p^{n}S=0
let
P_{X/S}
be the sheaf of PD principal parts. It is the structure sheaf
of the PD envelope of
\Delta:X\to X\times_{S}X
. It is a natural
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-bimodule.
Let
P^{n}_{X/S}
be the
(n-1)
th formal neighbourhood of the diagonal. It is defined for any
S
.
Then (crystalline) differential operators between
two
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
V_{1},V_{2}

are defined as
\textnormal{DiffOp}_{X/S}(V_{1},V_{2}):=\cup\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(P^{n}_{X/S}\otimes V_{1},V_{2})
, where
P^{n}_{X/S}\otimes V_{1}

carries its left
\mathcal{O}_{X}
structure. Write
D_{X}:=\textnormal{DiffOp}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
There is a map
\textnormal{DiffOp}_{X/S}(V_{1},V_{2})\to\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}(V_{1},V_{2})
which generally fails to be injective.
We then prove the following. Let
\textnormal{Sh}/\mathcal{O}
with residue field
k
, and let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},k\}
.
Let
F_{B}:\text{Mod}_{R}(B)\to\textnormal{QCoh}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}})
be the map defined by the canonical
B
-torsor
over
\mathcal{F}l_{R}
, we will also use the notation
\mathcal{V}:=F_{B}(V)
.
For
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(B)
let
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V):=U\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{U\mathfrak{b}}V
.

Theorem 1.2.1
.

[
Theorem

3.2.2
,
Theorem

3.2.4
]

(1)

There exist canonical
isomorphisms
e_{V}:F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V))\cong D_{\mathcal{F}l/R}\otimes\mathcal{V}
compatible with the filtrations on both sides.

(2)

They satisfy a number of compatibilities, for instance the map

\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(1))\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(1)
corresponds to the composition map
D_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes D_{\mathcal{F}l}\to D_{\mathcal{F}l}
.

(3)

There is a natural map

\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}V_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}V_{2})\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1})

producing Hecke equivariant maps away from
p
,
which is compatible with composition on both sides.

The analogue result works for
P
and
\textnormal{Sh}/R
, and both extend to toroidal compactifications.

Part
(3)
follows formally from parts
(1)
and
(2)
. Over the complex numbers, the functor in
(3)
was known
from
[
18
]
, and used in the integral setting by
[
60
]
,
[
48
]
.
Our treatment emphasizes the use of crystalline differential operators, and we give a more detailed description of
these isomorphisms,
which is needed to get a finer control on the operators produced in this way. Over the complex numbers one
can prove the theorem above using
the Borel embedding from the universal cover of
\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathbb{C}}

to
G/P
. Integrally, we lack an analogous global period map, so we will use the pointwise Grothendieck–Messing period map instead.

Remark 1.2.2
.

There is also a notion of non-crystalline differential operators
\textnormal{DiffOp}_{G}

[
70
,
Tag 09CH
]
, and a ”PD”-Verma
module using the algebra of distributions
\textnormal{Ver}^{PD}_{B}(V)
. To illustrate the difference between the two,
for
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}
, we have that

D_{G,X}
is generated by
\frac{1}{n!}(\frac{d}{dx})^{n}
,
while
D_{X}
is generated by
(\frac{d}{dx})^{n}
.
However, the functor from point
(3)

does not extend to a functor

\textnormal{Hom}_{(U(G),B)}(\textnormal{Ver}^{PD}_{B}V_{1},\textnormal{Ver}^{PD}_{B}V_{2})\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{G}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1})

in any reasonable sense, see
Remark

3.2.3
.
Similarly, there is a notion of baby Verma modules in characteristic
p
, and
”Frobenius differentials” on
\mathcal{F}l_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
, but the above functor does not relate the two. See
Remark

4.3.4
.
This justifies that crystalline differential operators are the right kind of operators for our purpose.

We prove
(1)
and
(2)
in
Section

3.2
, and in
Section

3.1
we introduce some of necessary
ingredients.
First, we construct the maps
e_{V}
on PD formal completions of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-points,
where it is given by the differential
of the Grothendieck–Messing period map, which is étale in an appropriate sense.
We use that on the flag variety
G/B
there is an analogous relation
between Verma modules and
G
-equivariant differential operators which is much easier to prove i.e.
we use the equivalence between
B
-representations and
G
-equivariant sheaves on
G/B
.
All the compatibilities will be transferred from the flag variety in this way.
Then we construct the isomorphism
e_{V}
.
First, we can reduce to constructing it at the level of the degree at most
1
part
\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}(V)
using
the compatibility of
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B})\twoheadrightarrow\textnormal{Ver}_{B}
with composition on
D_{\mathcal{F}l}
. We construct
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
for
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
using the Gauss–Manin connection
attached to
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
and the Kodaira–Spencer isomorphism. Namely, for
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
the associated
bundle on
G/B
is equipped with a connection, which defines an isomorphism of
B
-representations

\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}_{B}(V)\cong V\otimes\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}_{B}(1).

The dual of the stratification
P^{1}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\cong\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\otimes P^{1}

associated to the Gauss–Manin connection on
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
and the Kodaira–Spencer isomorphism
defines
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
after applying
F_{B}
to the isomorphism above.
For general
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(B)
we construct it by devissage. A priori this construction
is not well-defined, but since it agrees with the construction on PD formal completions, it is well-defined and all the required properties hold.

1.2.2.
Basic theta operators

We construct the basic theta operators in
Section

4
.
Consider the map
\mathcal{F}l\to\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt ZipFlag}}}\nolimits\to\textnormal{Sbt}_{G}=[B\times B\backslash G]
as in
[
34
, §3.1]
. All our operators will come from
pullback from this map. We remark that by construction of the map, all automorphic vector bundles arise
as pullbacks along this map.
Let
U\subset\mathcal{F}l
be the pullback of the open Bruhat cell
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}:=[B\times B\backslash Bw_{0}B]
.
Over
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
,
there is a
T
-reduction of the
B
-torsor
[1\times B\backslash G]\to\textnormal{Sbt}_{G}
, which we can pull back
to a
T
-reduction over
U
of the canonical
B
-torsor over
\mathcal{F}l
.
Therefore, we obtain a functor
F_{T}:\textnormal{Rep}(T)\to\text{Coh}(U)
which is compatible with
F_{B}:\textnormal{Rep}(B)\to\text{Coh}(\mathcal{F}l)

in the obvious sense.
Thus, applying
F_{T}
and using
Theorem

1.2.1
(1,2) yields a functor

(1.2.1)

\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda,\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))\to\cup_{n}\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{U}}(P^{n}\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\mu),\mathcal{L}(-\lambda))=\textnormal{DiffOp}_{U}(\mathcal{L}(-\mu),\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)).

The left-hand side is in bijection
with elements of
U\mathfrak{u}_{B}
of weights
\mu-\lambda
. In this way, for each
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}

we get operators
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma)
over

U
corresponding to
x_{-\gamma}\in\mathfrak{u}_{B}
.

Remark 1.2.3
.

Even though it is framed in a group-theoretic language, the
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
can also be defined
more classically in terms of the Gauss–Manin connection, see
4.2.5
(1).

To extend the
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
to
\mathcal{F}l
, we must determine the appropriate Hasse
invariants by which to post-compose them. Crucially, via
Theorem

1.2.1
the functor above (
1.2.1
)
is pulled-back
from the functor

\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda,\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}_{B}(\mu))\to\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}}(\underline{\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}_{B}(\mu)},\underline{-\lambda}),

where
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}\subseteq\textnormal{Sbt}_{G}
is the open cell, and the underline denotes the functor
sending representations of
B\times B

to vector bundles on
Sbt
(here both representations factor through the first projection).
The Hasse invariants
H_{\alpha}
are also defined via pullback from
Sbt
, so
to extend
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
we need to figure out for which powers
n_{\alpha,\gamma}\geq 0
the map

\underline{\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}_{B}(\lambda+\gamma)}\to\underline{-\lambda}\xrightarrow{\prod H^{n_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{\alpha}}\underline{(-\mu_{1},-\mu_{2})}

extends to
Sbt
. Then the pullback of this composition to
\mathcal{F}l
defines
\theta_{\gamma}
via

Theorem

1.2.1
.
This becomes a simple problem in Schubert calculus, which we can compute explicitly, following

[
34
]
.
For
(5)
and
(6)
of
Theorem

1.1.2
we prove some relations between the restriction of basic
theta operators to codimension
1
strata. These restrictions are well-defined when the associated Hasse invariant has a simple zero
(which always holds if
G^{\text{der}}
is simply connected),
by point
(2)
.
By our construction, we can
just check these relations on
Sbt
, i.e. by checking that two maps on closed codimension
1
stratum
\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}

agree.
We illustrate
(5)
and
(6)
for
G=GU(2,1)
, with simple roots

\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
. In
Theorem

4.4.2
and
4.4.4
we prove that
the restriction to the closed codimension
1
stratum
\overline{D}_{\alpha_{i}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}l

of
\theta_{\alpha_{i}}
on
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)

is

\theta_{\alpha_{i},\overline{D}_{\alpha_{i}}}=\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}_{i}\rangle B_{\alpha_{i}}
,
where
B_{\alpha_{i}}
is a section on
\overline{D}_{\alpha_{i}}
which is non-vanishing on the interior.
This proves
(5)
.
Furthermore,
\theta_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\overline{D}_{\alpha_{2}}}=B_{\alpha_{2}}\theta_{\alpha_{1},\overline{D}_{\alpha_{2}}}
. Now, let
f\in\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda))
for a generic

\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
. Suppose that
\theta_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}(f)=0
,
since the weight is small we may assume it is not divisible by a Hasse invariant, by
2.8.2
(2).
From the above

\displaystyle\theta_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\overline{D}_{\alpha_{2}}}(f)=0

\displaystyle\implies H_{\alpha_{2}}\mid\theta_{\alpha_{1}}(f)\implies\theta_{\alpha_{1}}(f)=0

\displaystyle\implies\theta_{\alpha_{1},\overline{D}_{\alpha_{1}}}(f)=0\implies H_{\alpha_{1}}\mid f\implies f=0,

where the second implication also follows from
2.8.2
(2).
In general, point
(6)
is proved via
an inductive process
like this one.

Points
(2)
,
(3)
,
(4)
of
Theorem

1.1.2
can be proved over
U
.
For this, we give an explicit description of the isomorphism
e_{V}
of
Theorem

1.2.1

over
U
, which by its
construction reduces to an explicit description of the Gauss–Manin connection on some local basis.
This is treated in
Section

3.3
.
The crucial fact that we need (
Lemma

2.2.6
),
is that there is a
G
-representation
V
such that after some compatible choice of
Borels the induced full Hodge and conjugate flag on
\mathcal{V}
are in general position. That is,
there exists some choice of Borels of
\textnormal{GL}(V)
such that the embedding
\textnormal{Sbt}_{G}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}

maps the open cell to the open cell. In fact, one can take
V
to be the Hodge embedding.
Then we can describe
\nabla
on
\mathcal{V}_{U}
with respect
to an appropriate section of the
T
-torsor over
U
which we call
adapted elements

(
Lemma

3.3.10
),
in terms of the Kodaira–Spencer isomorphism.
The definition of an adapted element uses the property that the full conjugate filtration of
\mathcal{V}
,
which by our assumption can be expressed in terms of the Hodge filtration,
is flat for
\nabla
with trivial
p
-curvature on its graded pieces. As a toy example,
for the modular curve, an adapted element would be given by
a local basis
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
of
\mathcal{H}
with
e_{2}\in\textnormal{Ker}(V)
such that
\nabla(e_{2})=0
, and

e_{1}\in\omega
such
that
\nabla(e_{1})=e_{2}\otimes\textnormal{KS}(e^{2}_{1})
. This basis is well-defined up to
p
th powers.
In such a basis we get a simple expression for
e_{V}
in
3.3.12
,
which proves point
(2)
. Point
(3)

follows from compatibility of (
1.2.1
) with composition in an appropriate sense. Roughly,
if a map of Verma modules
is given by an element
f(x_{-\gamma})
in
U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}
, then its corresponding theta operator on
U
will
be
f(\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma})
.
To prove this compatibility with composition we use the explicit description of
e_{V}
when fixing
an adapted element.
Furthermore,
(4)
reduces to a computation involving the
p
-curvature map
on
\mathcal{V}
, where the
p
th iterate of a derivation
shows up. In this computation we use the fact that
\mathcal{V}
has the structure of a
de Rham F-gauge
.
That is, the Kodaira–Spencer map on
\mathcal{V}
agrees with
the map induced by the
p
-curvature and the Cartier isomorphism, see
3.3.9
.

1.2.3.
The linkage maps

We construct the theta linkage maps via
Theorem

1.2.1
,
by constructing maps of Verma modules in characteristic
p
.
The key observation is that

\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})

is invariant under
p
-translation of the weights,
so that we can reduce to the case where

\lambda\uparrow s_{\gamma}\cdot\lambda=\mu
, in which case there exists a map in characteristic
0
, by the classical
theory. See
5.1.3
. Then we simply define theta linkage maps as the differential operators coming from maps
of Verma modules, and similarly for parabolic theta linkage maps and parabolic Verma modules.
To prove that theta linkage maps are related to combinations of basic theta operators, we prove that when restricted to
U
the linkage maps
are a combination of
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
. This follows from
the compatibility with composition of (
1.2.1
), as explained above.
To prove the generic injectivity of

Theorem

1.1.5
, by the compatibility of (
1.2.1
) with composition,
the simple theta linkage map is given by repeated application of a
basic theta operator. Then we use point
(4)
in
Theorem

1.1.2
to reduce the injectivity of
the linkage map to the injectivity of
a basic theta operator.

1.2.4.
The entailments

To prove the entailments we need
3
facts.

(1)

The injectivity of a certain theta linkage map in
Theorem

1.1.5
, or the injectivity
of any operator we are using.

(2)

The fact that for generic non-Eisenstein
\mathfrak{m}
and generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
we have the relation

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\iff\textnormal{H}^{d}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0,

where
\eta
is the weight of the canonical bundle of
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
. This is proved in
7.2.4

for instance.
The forward direction follows
from the vanishing results for
\textnormal{H}^{1}
coherent cohomology of
[
1
]
,
[
76
]
(where
we use that
\mathfrak{m}
is non-Eisenstein to identify canonical and subcanonical coherent cohomology)
to lift coherent cohomology to characteristic
0
, and then use
the BGG decomposition over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. The other direction is more delicate, first one
uses the vanishing results of étale cohomology of
[
28
]

[
13
]

to lift étale cohomology to characteristic
0
.
Then one has to check that for non-Eisenstein eigensystems, if an element of the archimedean L-packet
contributes to the middle degree étale cohomology in characteristic
0
,
then so does the holomorphic component of the L-packet, which
contributes to coherent cohomology in degree
0
. In the
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}/\mathbb{Q}
case and the Harris–Taylor case
one can prove this by
using the fact that, under the assumptions on
\mathfrak{m}

the Galois representation
\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is contained in middle degree étale cohomology.

(3)

Finally, we need the decomposition of dual Weyl modules
V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
for

\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
into Serre weights.
This is a difficult
problem, so even for
G=\textnormal{GL}_{5}
, there are no results written in the literature
2
2

2

Although Lusztig’s conjecture

[
36
, §II.7.20]

describes the constituents of
V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
in terms of Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
for
p
sufficiently large, which can in principle be computed.
, but
in our examples these decompositions have been explicitly figured out.

For simplicity let us illustrate the generic weak entailment for
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
. The one for
\textnormal{GL}_{4}
in

7.2.6
is proved in the same way. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be generic non-Eisenstein, and
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
.
Let
\lambda_{i}\in C_{i}
be its affine Weyl reflections in the other
p
-restricted alcoves.
We want to prove that the statement ’
F(\lambda_{0})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
implies

F(\lambda_{i})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})

for either
i=1
or
i=2
’ holds for
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
generic.
For point
(1)
we know that the theta linkage map

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda_{1}+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\xrightarrow{\theta_{\lambda_{1}\uparrow\lambda_{2}}}\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda_{2}+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}

is injective, and in point
(3)
we have following relations in
the Grothendieck group of
\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}G(\mathbb{F}_{p})
:
[V(\lambda_{1})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]=F(\lambda_{0})+F(\lambda_{1})
,

[V(\lambda_{2})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]=F(\lambda_{1})+F(\lambda_{2})
.
Then
F(\lambda_{0})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
implies

\displaystyle\textnormal{H}^{3}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(F(\lambda_{0}))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{3}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(V(\lambda_{1})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda_{1}+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0

\displaystyle\implies\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda_{2}+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{3}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(V(\lambda_{2})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0,

which means
F(\lambda_{i})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
for
i=1
or
i=2
. The first arrow is implied by

[V(\lambda_{1})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]=F(\lambda_{0})+F(\lambda_{1})
and the concentration of étale cohomology in middle degree.
The second is point
(2)
above, the third follows by point
(1)
, and the last by point
(2)
again.
Finally, the decomposition
[V(\lambda_{2})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]=F(\lambda_{1})+F(\lambda_{2})
yields the final result.
Thus, using the more general injectivity result
Theorem

1.1.5
, we could
prove more general entailments if we understood the constituents of certain linked dual Weyl modules,
but this gets very hard as the rank of
G
grows.

1.3.
Organization

In
Section

2
we explain all of the geometric tools that we will need to construct the operators
in the setting of Hodge type Shimura varieties. In particular, we prove a general version of
the Kodaira–Spencer isomorphism, and
its compatibility with a Grothendieck–Messing period map. Although not difficult, this result does not appear explicitly in the literature.
In
Section

3
we prove the relation between Verma modules and
differential operators on the Shimura variety, and we give an explicit description
of this on the open stratum of
\mathcal{F}l_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. In
Section

4
we construct the basic theta operators,
and we prove a number of properties about them. In
Section

5
we construct the theta linkage maps, and
we prove the injectivity of a particular simple theta linkage map. In
Section

6
we prove that
duality of differential operators is compatible with duality of (parabolic) theta linkage maps, defined by
Serre duality on the flag variety. In
Section

7
we prove some generic and non-generic entailments.
In
Section

8
we construct the de Rham realization functor, and we reprove the lowest alcove integral BGG
decomposition of
[
48
]
.

This article supersedes most of the author’s first version of
[
61
]
, which
concerns the case of
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
. The main novelties, apart from dealing with general
Hodge type Shimura varieties, are as follows.
Section

3.1
and
Section

3.2
are mostly the same, but we also consider objects
coming from category
\mathcal{O}
. In
Section

3.3
we use a more general method, not relying on the existence
of an ordinary locus.
In
Section

4
we give a new, more conceptual construction of
the basic theta operators,
a proof of
Theorem

4.3.2
without using Serre–Tate coordinates,
and we prove relations between restrictions of basic theta operators to codimension
1

strata.
Section

6
is new and so is the relation between Borel and parabolic theta linkage maps in
Section

5
.

Section

7
is new, although the method to obtain the entailments is the same.
In
Section

8
we introduce the de Rham realization functor.
