\textnormal{H}^{\bullet}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega^{\textnormal{can}}(w\cdot\lambda)^{\vee})

vanishes below degree
d-l(w)

[
49
, Prop 8.12]
, where
d
is the dimension
of
Sh
.

In
[
76
]
they
moreover obtain sharp bounds on the ample cone of automorphic line bundles, which makes the genericity conditions
on the weights
explicit, i.e. one can determine what
n
is in
2.8.2
(1). Similarly, in the results of

Theorem

2.8.3
(2) one is able to explicitly compute a bound for
m
in
2.8.2
(2).
We also note that the PEL assumption in
(3)
is not essential, and one could reprove their result in the Hodge case.
In
[
49
]
they also prove a more general result without the restriction on

G^{\textnormal{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
being almost-simple, where the weights considered are in a smaller region than the
lowest alcove.

3.
Verma modules and differential operators

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum of Hodge type, and
Sh
its associated integral model.
The goal of this section is to relate
Verma modules to differential operators on the integral model of the (flag) Shimura variety.
First we define the kind of differential operators and Verma modules that we will use.

3.1.
Sheaves of differential operators and Lie algebras

In this section we consider
G
a split reductive group scheme over a ring
R
, which will be either
\mathcal{O}:=W(k)
for some finite
k/\mathbb{F}_{p}
, or
\mathcal{O}/p^{n}

for some
n\geq 1
. Let
(T,B)
be a Borel pair of
G
.
We start by reviewing
various definitions of differential operators, and the relation to Verma modules in the case of flag varieties.

Definition 3.1.1
.

•

(Universal enveloping algebra)
Let
U\mathfrak{g}
be the
R
-algebra
\bigoplus\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes n}/\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x-[x,y]\rangle
with
x,y,
running along
\mathfrak{g}
.

•

(Restricted universal enveloping algebra) Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
, and write
q=p^{n}
.
Over
R
the Lie algebra has a
q
-operation
x\mapsto x^{[q]}

corresponding to considering
x
as a left invariant derivation of
G
and composing it
q
times. For each
m\geq 1
,
the
image of the map

\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{(q^{m})}\hookrightarrow U\mathfrak{g}_{R}

sending
X
to
X^{q^{m}}-X^{[q^{m}]}
lands in the center of
U\mathfrak{g}_{R}

3
3

3

To see why, apply
[
5
, Lem 1.3.1]
to
G
and take its fiber at the identity.
. Denote it by
Z^{\text{Fr}^{m}}
.
Define
U^{m}\mathfrak{g}=U\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{Z^{\text{Fr}^{m}}}R
, where the character
Z^{\text{Fr}^{m}}\to R

is induced by augmentation map
\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{(q^{m})}\to R
.

The first one is a filtered algebra, for
m\geq 0
we denote
U^{\leq m}\mathfrak{g}
the pieces of
degree at most
m
.

Remark 3.1.2
.

One can also consider the algebra of distributions
U(G)=\textnormal{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{O}^{\wedge}_{G,e},R)
.
There is a map of algebras
U\mathfrak{g}\to U(G)
induced by the
isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G}/I^{2}\to\mathcal{O}_{G}/\mathfrak{m}_{e}^{2}

sending
x\otimes 1-1\otimes x
to
x
. For
R=\mathcal{O}
this is an injection that becomes an isomorphism
after inverting
p
, but it is not
injective over
k
. In fact, the surjection
U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}\to U^{0}\mathfrak{g}
can be identified
with the image of
U\mathfrak{g}\to U(G)
.
One can see this explicitly:
let
\{H_{i}:i=1,\ldots k\}
be some generators of the Cartan algebra for the maximal torus

\mathfrak{h}
,
and extend it to a basis

\{H_{i},X_{j}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Then
U(G)_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is generated by elements of the form

{H_{i}\choose k}=\frac{H_{i}(H_{i}-1)\ldots(H_{i}-k+1)}{k!}
and
\frac{X^{n}_{i}}{n!}
. Since
X^{[p]}_{i}=0

and

H^{[p]}_{i}=H_{i}
we see that
U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}\to U(G)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
factors through
U^{0}\mathfrak{g}
.

We want to define an integral/mod
p
analogue of category
\mathcal{O}

over the complex numbers. Let us first recall its definition for motivation.

Definition 3.1.3
.

(Complex category
\mathcal{O}
)
Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie algebra of a complex reductive group,
\mathfrak{h}
a Cartan subalgebra,
\Phi^{+}

a choice of positive roots
and
\mathfrak{u}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
.
Then the category
\mathcal{O}
with respect to
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{u})

consists of
U\mathfrak{g}
-modules
M
such that

(1)

M
is finitely generated as a
U\mathfrak{g}
-module.

(2)

The action of
\mathfrak{h}
on
M
is semisimple.

(3)

For each
v\in M
, the
U\mathfrak{u}
-submodule generated by
v
is finite dimensional.

Fix a choice
T\subseteq B\subseteq Q\subseteq G
of maximal torus, Borel, and some parabolic

Q
, with its opposite
Q^{-}
. Let
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
be the category of algebraic representations of
Q_{R}
which are
finitely generated as
R
-modules.

Definition 3.1.4
.

(Verma modules and category
\mathcal{O}
integrally)
A
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-module over
R
is a
R
-module
M
equipped with an action of
U\mathfrak{g}

and an algebraic action of
Q
, i.e.
M
is a filtered union of elements in
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
.
Moreover,
M

satisfies the following properties.

(1)

M
is a finitely generated
U\mathfrak{g}
-module.

(2)

Since the action of
Q
is algebraic, the derivative of
Q
on
M
is well-defined.
It agrees with the restriction of the

U\mathfrak{g}
action to
U\mathfrak{q}
.

We denote this category of
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules by
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
. Note that
the next two properties are automatically satisfied by
M\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
, which are analogues of
the ones of complex category
\mathcal{O}
.

3.

The action of
T\subseteq Q
on
M
is semisimple.

4.

Let
v\in M
, then the
Q
-submodule generated by
v
is in

\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
.

Similarly, in characteristic
p
a
(U^{0}\mathfrak{g},Q)
-module is an element of
\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(Q)

with an action of
U\mathfrak{g}
factoring through
U^{0}\mathfrak{g}
, such that the derivative of the
Q
-action
factors through
U^{0}\mathfrak{q}
. Let
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
.

•

Let
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\coloneqq U\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{U\mathfrak{q}}V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
, with

U\mathfrak{g}
acting on the left and
Q
by the adjoint action on the left, and by its action on
V
on the right.

•

(Baby Verma modules) For
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
and
m\geq 1
let

\textnormal{Ver}^{m}_{Q,R}(V)\coloneqq U^{m}\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{U^{m}\mathfrak{q}}V
as
a
(U^{m}\mathfrak{g},Q)_{R}
-module.

•

For
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
we can define the variants

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q/B}(V)\coloneqq U\mathfrak{q}\otimes_{U\mathfrak{b}}V

and
\textnormal{Ver}^{m}_{Q/B,R}(V)\coloneqq U^{m}\mathfrak{q}\otimes_{U^{m}\mathfrak{b}}V
.

The first one has a filtration induced by the one on
U\mathfrak{g}
, i.e.

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)^{\leq m}
is generated by simple tensors
x\otimes v
with
x\in U^{\leq m}\mathfrak{g}
.
These filtered pieces are preserved by the action of
Q
.

We have
\text{gr}^{\bullet}\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\cong\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{q}\otimes V

as
Q
-representations. In general the graded pieces don’t split,
except in the case

\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}_{Q}(1)\cong 1\oplus\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{q}.

It is clear that
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
is an abelian category, since
U\mathfrak{g}
is a Noetherian ring, by the PBW theorem.
Our definition of
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
mirrors the definition of category
\mathcal{O}
over
the complex numbers, with two main changes. One is that we work with a general parabolic instead of a Borel.
The second is that when working integrally one cannot just work with representations of Lie algebras,
since in characteristic
p
the Lie algebra does not distinguish between weights
which are
p
-translations of each other.
When
Q=B
this category also appears in
[
54
]
under the name modular category
\mathcal{O}
,
and some of its basic properties are also discussed in
[
66
, §2]
,
which appeared when the contents of this paper were already written.
We define the admissible dual of an element of
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
.

Definition 3.1.5
.

(Duality)
Let
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
. We define its dual

V^{\vee}
as the linear functionals
\phi:V\to R
with the property that
\phi
factors through a surjection
V\twoheadrightarrow W

with
W
a finitely generated
R
-module.
We define a
U\mathfrak{g}
action on
V^{\vee}
given by
xf(v):=f(-xv)
for
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
v\in V
.

In complex category
\mathcal{O}
it is customary to define the
\mathfrak{g}
action on
V^{\vee}
by

xf(v)=f(-\tau(x)v)
, where
\tau
is the anti-involution on
\mathfrak{g}
acting as
-id
on
\mathfrak{h}
and as the identity
on the root spaces. We do not do this, and as a result
the weight spaces are related by the relation
(V^{\vee})_{\lambda}=V_{-\lambda}
, which will be key for us.
Importantly, duality behaves differently mod
p^{n}
compared to characteristic zero. For that we need to define
a variant of
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
.

Definition 3.1.6
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,R}
be the category of
R
-modules
M
with an action of
U\mathfrak{g}
and
an algebraic action of
Q
, i.e.
M
is a filtered union of elements in
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
satisfying
property
(2)
from
3.1.4
. Moreover, the weights of
M
must be bounded below
in the
\leq
order.

A Verma module
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}W
for
W\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
belongs to
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
, and we will see that its
dual belongs to
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,R}
.

Lemma 3.1.7
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
, and
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
. Then the
U\mathfrak{g}

action on
V^{\vee}
integrates to an algebraic action of
Q
, in a way that
V^{\vee}\in\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,R}
.

Proof.

Let
V=\langle v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}\rangle_{U\mathfrak{g}}
, and let
W=\langle v_{i}\rangle_{Q}
.
It is an element of
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
since
V
is a filtered union of elements of
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
.
There is a natural map

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)\to V
. Its image is

U\mathfrak{q}^{-}W=\langle v_{i}\rangle_{U\mathfrak{q}^{-}Q}\supseteq\langle v_{i}\rangle_{U\mathfrak{q}^{-}U\mathfrak{q}}=V
,
so it is surjective.
Thus, induces an injection
V^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)^{\vee}
which
is
U\mathfrak{g}
-equivariant. Therefore, to prove that
V^{\vee}\in\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,R}
we may assume that
V=\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)
.
For each
m\geq 1
we have surjections
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)\twoheadrightarrow\textnormal{Ver}^{m}_{Q,R}(W)
as
Q
-modules. From the PBW theorem
we see that only the zero element in
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)
vanishes in all of the
\textnormal{Ver}^{m}_{Q,R}(W)
,
since
U^{m}\mathfrak{g}
is generated
as a vector space by
\{\prod x^{n_{\gamma}}_{\gamma}:0\leq n_{\gamma}<q^{m}\}
. For any given

\phi\in\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)^{\vee}
one can then find some
m
such that
\phi
factors through
\textnormal{Ver}^{m}_{Q,R}(W)
, since
\phi
factors thorough a finitely generated quotient of

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)
.
Therefore,

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)^{\vee}=\cup_{m}\textnormal{Ver}^{m}_{Q,R}(W)^{\vee}
is a union of elements in

\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
. We can then see that the properties of
3.1.6
are satisfied.

∎

Note that the proof implies that if
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
then its weights are bounded above in the
\leq
order.
Thus, dual Verma modules are not in
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
since their weights are not bounded above.
Alternatively, one could prove directly that over
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
dual Verma modules
are not finitely generated as
U\mathfrak{g}
modules. For instance, for
Q=B
,
R=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, and
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}

the elements
x^{p}_{\gamma}\in Z(U\mathfrak{g})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
act trivially on
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(1)^{\vee}
, so that
the
U\mathfrak{g}
submodule generated by any vector is finite dimensional.

Remark 3.1.8
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,\mathcal{O}/p^{n}}
. Even though the action of
\textnormal{Lie}(Q^{-})
is locally finite on
V^{\vee}
it does not upgrade
to an algebraic action of
Q^{-}
. For an example take
G=\textnormal{GL}_{2}
,
R=k
,
Q=B
,
V=\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(1)
,
and let
x_{+},x_{-}\in\mathfrak{g}

be the positive and negative root.
Then for
n\geq p
,
x^{p}_{-}(x^{n}_{-}\otimes 1)^{\vee}=(x^{n-p}_{-}\otimes 1)^{\vee}\neq 0
, but
x^{p}_{-}
is zero in
U(B^{-})_{k}
. This
means that one cannot integrate the
\mathfrak{g}
action into an action of
B^{-}
.
If
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
then
V^{\vee}\in\mathcal{O}_{Q^{-},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
since the action of
\textnormal{Lie}(Q^{-})
is locally finite,
but
V^{\vee}\notin\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. In the example of
G=\textnormal{GL}_{2}
and
V=\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(1)

we have that
x_{+}(x^{n}_{-}\otimes 1)^{\vee}=\frac{1}{(n+1)(-n)}(x^{n+1}_{-}\otimes 1)^{\vee}
, so that the action of
\textnormal{Lie}(B)
is not locally finite.

Note that we can still define the admissible dual of an element of
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,R}
word by word
from
3.1.5
.

Lemma 3.1.9
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
. Then

(1)

The functor
(-)^{\vee}:\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}\to\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,R}
is exact.

(2)

Let
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
. Then
(V^{\vee})^{\vee}=V
.

(3)

Let
V,W\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
. Then
(V\otimes W)^{\vee}=V^{\vee}\otimes W^{\vee}
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, since
V^{\vee}
is a submodule of
\textnormal{Hom}_{R}(V,R)
we just have to prove that given
an embedding
V_{1}\hookrightarrow V_{2}
the map
V^{\vee}_{2}\to V^{\vee}_{1}
is surjective. Let
\phi_{1}\in V^{\vee}_{1}

such that it factors through
V_{1}\twoheadrightarrow W_{1}
with
W_{1}
finitely generated.
We can construct a finitely generated quotient
V_{2}\twoheadrightarrow W_{2}
such that
W_{1}\hookrightarrow W_{2}
.
Thus, we have reduced to proving exactness duality on

\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}(R)
. This follows since each finitely generated
R
module is a direct sum of free and torsion modules, by the structure theorem for modules over a PID.
For
(2)
the
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-equivariant injection
V\hookrightarrow(V^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism.
Write
V\twoheadrightarrow V_{n}
for an exhaustive cofiltration
with
V_{n}\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
, we claim that
(V^{\vee})^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow(V^{\vee}_{n})^{\vee}
is also exhaustive.
Then one uses the observation that finitely generated
R
-modules are reflexive.
We can prove the claim as follows.
Let
\phi\in(V^{\vee})^{\vee}
, then it factors through
some projection
V^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow W
with
W
finitely generated. Let
W
be generated
by some

\phi_{1},\ldots\phi_{k}\in V^{\vee}
, and for each
1\leq i\leq k
let
\phi_{i}
factor through
V\twoheadrightarrow W_{i}
with

W_{i}\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
.
Consider
V\twoheadrightarrow\tilde{V}
with
\tilde{V}\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
so that all
\phi_{i}
factor through it.
Then
\phi
is not in the kernel of
(V^{\vee})^{\vee}\to(\tilde{V}^{\vee})^{\vee}
. Observe that
(V^{\vee})^{\vee}
is countably generated,
so that we can inductively construct an exhaustive cofiltration

V\twoheadrightarrow V_{n}
by repeating the process along the generators of
(V^{\vee})^{\vee}
. The above argument shows that

(V^{\vee})^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow(V^{\vee}_{n})^{\vee}
is exhaustive.
For
(3)
there is a natural map
e:W^{\vee}\otimes V^{\vee}\to(W\otimes V)^{\vee}
.
By writing
V=\cup_{n}V_{n}
,
W=\cup_{n}W_{n}
with
V_{n},W_{n}\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
, the map
e
factors through an isomorphism

W^{\vee}_{n}\otimes V^{\vee}_{n}\cong(W_{n}\otimes V_{n})^{\vee}
. This implies that
e
is injective. Given

\phi\in(W\otimes V)^{\vee}
, let it factor through
W\otimes V\twoheadrightarrow U
for some
U\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
.
By finding some simple tensors in
W\otimes V
that generate
U

we can construct projections
W\twoheadrightarrow U_{1}
,
V\twoheadrightarrow U_{2}
and
\phi_{1}\in W^{\vee}

\phi_{2}\in V^{\vee}
factoring through these
such that
\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}
maps to
\phi
.
Thus,
e
is an isomorphism.
∎

Now we define the sheaves of differential operators that we will use.

Definition 3.1.10
.

(Sheaves of differential operators)
Let
Y/S
be a smooth map of schemes. Suppose first that
Y/S
is separated.
Let
P_{Y}
be the divided power envelope of the diagonal
\Delta:Y\to Y\times_{S}Y
, i.e. it is the divided power
envelope of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
with respect to
I
, the ideal sheaf of
\Delta
. If
p^{n}S=0
for some
n\geq 1

then
P_{Y}
is well-defined without any assumption on
Y/S

[
4
, Rem 3.31]
.
We consider
P_{Y}
as a
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-bimodule via the two projections.
Let
J
be the kernel of
P_{Y}\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. Denote by
J^{[n]}

the ideal of
P_{Y}
generated by elements
\prod\gamma_{n_{i}}(x_{i})
for
x_{i}\in I
and

\sum n_{i}\geq n
.

•

(Crystalline differential operators)
For an integer
m\geq 0
, let
P^{m}_{Y}=P_{Y}/J^{[m+1]}
as a
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-bimodule via each
projection. It is well-defined for any
Y/S
.
The sheaf of crystalline differential operators
of degree at most
m
is
D^{m}_{Y}=\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(P^{m}_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y})
,
where the
Hom
is taken as left
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules.
Then the sheaf of crystalline differential operators is
D_{Y}=\cup_{m\geq 0}D^{m}_{Y}
.
More explicitly,
D_{Y}
is the sheaf of rings generated by
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
and
T_{Y/S}
, under
the relations
AB-BA=[A,B]
,
Af=fA+A(f)
for all
A,B\in T_{Y/S}
(seen as derivations),
and
f\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}
.

•

(Log crystalline differential operators) Let
D\hookrightarrow Y
be a relative Cartier divisor with normal crossings.
Define
D^{\log}_{(Y,D)}
as the sheaf generated by
T_{Y/S}(-\log D)
and
\mathcal{O}_{Y}

together with the relations described above. Define
P^{\log}_{(Y,D)}
as in
[
60
, §4.2]
, using the
fiber product in the category of log schemes.

Remark 3.1.11
.

One can also consider Grothendieck’s sheaf of differential operators

\tilde{D}_{Y}
, defined by taking the formal completion of
Y\times Y
along the diagonal.
To illustrate the difference between the two, for
Y=\mathbb{A}^{1}
we have that
\tilde{D}_{Y}
is generated by the divided powers

\frac{1}{n!}\frac{d^{n}}{dx}
, while
D_{Y}
is generated by the
\frac{d^{n}}{dx}
. In this way we see that
D_{Y}

does not act faithfully on
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
in characteristic
p
.
We won’t make use of
\tilde{D}_{Y}
, except to remark that crystalline differential operators are the ones that
make our formalism work, as opposed to
\tilde{D}
.

Then
P_{Y}
is a cofiltered
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
bi-module, and
one has
\text{gr}^{\bullet}P_{Y}=\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}\Omega^{1}_{Y}
. It also carries a natural coalgebra structure,
given by the maps
\delta:P_{Y}\to P_{Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}P_{Y}

of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-bimodules, defined on local coordinates by

a\otimes_{R}b\mapsto a\otimes_{R}1\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}1\otimes_{R}b.

For
x\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}
let
\xi_{x}=x\otimes 1-1\otimes x\in I
. If
Y/S/(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
is smooth then

P_{Y}
is locally freely generated
by divided powers of elements
like this, using étale coordinates
[
4
, Prop 3.32]
. In these coordinates
\delta
is given by

\delta:\prod_{i}\frac{\xi^{n}_{x_{i}}}{n!}\mapsto\prod_{i}(\frac{\xi^{n}_{x_{i}}}{n!}\otimes 1+1\otimes\frac{\xi^{n}_{x_{i}}}{n!})
, and one sees
that

P^{n+m}_{Y}
lands in
P^{n}_{Y}\otimes P^{m}_{Y}
.

Definition 3.1.12
.

Let
Y/S
be a scheme, and

\mathcal{F}_{1,2}
sheaves of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules.
Define the sheaf of (crystalline) differential operators of degree at most
m
from
\mathcal{F}_{1}
to
\mathcal{F}_{2}
as

\textnormal{DiffOp}^{m}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})\coloneqq\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(P^{m}_{Y/S}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})

where in the tensor product
P^{m}_{Y}
is a
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module on the right,
and the
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module structure is given by the left
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
action on
P^{m}_{Y}
.
Then
\textnormal{DiffOp}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}):=\cup_{m}\textnormal{DiffOp}^{m}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
.
There is a map

(3.1.1)

\textnormal{DiffOp}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}),

defined by precomposing with the (not
\mathcal{O}
-linear) map
\mathcal{F}_{1}\to P^{m}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{1}
given by

v\mapsto 1\otimes 1\otimes v
.
Given
\phi\in\textnormal{DiffOp}^{m}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}),\psi\in\textnormal{DiffOp}^{n}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathcal{F}_{3})

its composition is defined by

\psi\circ\phi\coloneqq P^{n+m}_{Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{F}_{1}\xrightarrow{\epsilon\otimes\text{id}}P^{n}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}P^{m}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{1}\xrightarrow{\text{id}\otimes\phi}P^{n}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{F}_{2}\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathcal{F}_{3},

This makes (
3.1.1
) compatible with composition on the right-hand side.
When
p^{n}S=0
we will occasionally need the notion of a HPD differential operator between
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
: this
is an element of
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(P_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
. Thus, every HPD differential operator
is a differential operator, but not conversely, see
3.1.14
(4) ahead.
We define log differential operators on
(Y,D)
as

\textnormal{DiffOp}^{\log}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})=\cup_{n}\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(P^{\log,n}_{(Y,D)}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
,
and similarly for HPD log differential operators.

Notation 3.1.13
.

For a left
\mathcal{O}
-module
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
a bimodule
we will always use
\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{F}
for the tensor product with the right

\mathcal{O}
structure on
\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}
where the tensor product is taken
with respect to the left
\mathcal{O}
structure on
\mathcal{G}
.

Differential operators are tightly related to vector bundles with connections, and more generally to stratifications.

Definition 3.1.14
.

(Stratifications)
For a quasi-coherent sheaf
E
on a smooth scheme
Y/S
we define the following.

(1)

A connection on
E

can be seen as a
\mathcal{O}
-linear isomorphism
P^{1}_{Y}\otimes E\cong E\otimes P^{1}_{Y}

such that
the associated projection map
E\to E
is the identity, and the connection is flat
if the isomorphism satisfies some cocycle condition described in the point below

[
4
, §2]
.

(2)

An extension to a collection of compatible isomorphisms of left
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules

(\epsilon_{n}:P^{n}_{Y}\otimes E\cong E\otimes P^{n}_{Y})_{n\geq 1}
satisfying a cocycle condition with respect to

Y\times_{S}Y\times_{S}Y

is said to be a PD stratification on
E
. We also have the notion
of a stratification when using the non-PD version
\tilde{P}^{n}
as opposed to
P^{n}
.
The cocycle condition is the commutativity
of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{n}_{Y}\otimes P^{m}_{Y}\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\otimes P^{m}_{Y}\otimes P^{n}_{Y},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{m}_{Y}\otimes E\otimes P^{n}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes\epsilon_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{*}\epsilon_{n+m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{m}\otimes\text{id}}

where
\delta:P^{n+m}\to P^{n}\otimes P^{m}
is the coalgebra structure map
[
4
, 2.10]
.
Here by
\delta^{*}\epsilon_{n+m}
we mean the extension of scalars of

\epsilon_{n+m}:P^{n+m}\otimes E\cong E\otimes P^{n+m}
along the map of algebras

\delta
. The left-hand side of
\epsilon_{n+m}
is seen as a left
P^{n+m}
-module, and the right-hand side as a
right
P^{n+m}
-module.

(3)

Suppose that
p^{m}S=0
for some
m\geq 1
. An extension to an isomorphism
P_{Y}\otimes E\cong E\otimes P_{Y}
of
P_{Y}
-modules
satisfying the analogue of the diagram above (
2
)
is said
to be a HPD stratification
4
4

4

HPD stands for hyperstratification.
.
Given
E
any quasi-coherent sheaf there is a canonical HPD stratification
on
P_{Y}\otimes E

\epsilon_{P_{Y}\otimes E}:P_{Y}\otimes P_{Y}\otimes E\cong P_{Y}\otimes E\otimes P_{Y}

given by
(a\otimes b)\otimes(c\otimes d)\otimes m\mapsto(ac\otimes b)\otimes m\otimes(1\otimes d)
.

(4)

The data of a flat connection on
E
is equivalent to the data of a PD stratification.
The data of a flat quasi-nilpotent connection is equivalent to the data of a HPD stratification. A connection
(\nabla,E)

over
Y/S
with
p^{n}S=0

is quasi-nilpotent if for any local section
s\in E
there exist étale local coordinates
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
and a
positive integer
k
such that
\nabla(\partial/\partial x_{i})^{k}s=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,n

[
4
, Thm 4.12]
. In particular, the condition of being quasi-nilpotent is independent of the choice of coordinates.
All these results can be extended to the log setting of
(Y,D)

as in
[
60
, §4.2]
.

Crucially the Gauss–Manin connection on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
is quasi-nilpotent.

Lemma 3.1.15
.

[
41
]

Let
X/Y/S
schemes with
Y/S
smooth,
X/Y
proper, and
S
is annihilated by a power of
p
.
Then the Gauss–Manin connection on
\textnormal{H}^{i}_{\textnormal{dR}}(X/Y)
is
flat and quasi-nilpotent.

In characteristic
p
crystalline differential operators interact nicely with the relative Frobenius.

Proposition 3.1.16
.

(Frobenius differentials)
Let
Y/S
be a smooth map of schemes over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
E
a
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module. Let
F:Y\to Y^{(p)}

be the relative Frobenius. Then for all
m\geq 1

there is a natural map

(3.1.2)

F^{*}F_{*}E\to P^{m}_{Y/S}\otimes E

which is injective for sufficiently large
m
. For

\mathcal{V}_{1,2}
sheaves of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules
define the sheaf of Frobenius differential operators as

D^{[p]}(\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2})\coloneqq\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2}).

The composition of two maps
f:F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}_{1}\to\mathcal{V}_{2}
and
g:F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}_{2}\to\mathcal{V}_{3}

is defined as
F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}_{1}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{id}\circ s\circ\textnormal{id}}F^{*}F_{*}F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}_{1}\xrightarrow{F^{*}F_{*}f}F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}_{2}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{V}_{3}
, where
s:1\to F_{*}F^{*}
is the unit of the adjunction.
Then (
3.1.2
) induces a surjection
\textnormal{DiffOp}(\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2})\to D^{[p]}(\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2})

compatible with composition.

Proof.

Define a map
E\to P^{m}_{Y/S}\otimes E
is given by
v\mapsto 1\otimes 1\otimes v
,
which is Frobenius linear since
(f\otimes 1-1\otimes f)^{p}=0
on
P_{Y/S}
for all

f\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. By adjunction it defines the map (
3.1.2
).
The rest of the statements can easily be checked on étale local coordinates.
∎

The above also says that crystalline differential
operators are always Frobenius linear in characteristic
p
, and that the image of (
3.1.1
)
is precisely
D^{[p]}
.

Example 3.1.17
.

Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. Then
D_{X/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}=\textnormal{DiffOp}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})

is generated by the elements
(\frac{d}{dx})^{n}
for
n\geq 0
. On the other hand

D^{[p]}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})
is generated by
(\frac{d}{dx})^{n}
for
0\leq n\leq p-1
.
We see that for
n\geq p
,
(\frac{d}{dx})^{n}
acts trivially as an operator in

\textnormal{Hom}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})
, so that the image of
D_{X}\to\textnormal{Hom}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})

is indeed

D^{[p]}
.

3.1.1.
Differential operators on flag varieties

Let
Q\subset G
be a reductive group and a parabolic subgroup, defined over
R
, as in the beginning
of the section.
Differential operators on the flag variety
G/Q
are intimately related to Verma modules.

Lemma 3.1.18
.

Let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}/p^{n}\}
.
There is an equivalence of categories between
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)

and
G
-equivariant coherent
\mathcal{O}_{G/Q}
modules. The functor in one direction

F_{G/Q}:\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)\to\textnormal{Coh}_{G}(\mathcal{O}_{G/Q})
is given by

V\mapsto(G\times V)/Q
with
Q
acting as

h\cdot(g,v)=(hg,hv)
. The functor in the other direction is taking the fiber
at
\infty=[Q]\in G/Q
. Both are exact and tensor functors, in particular
F_{G/Q}
commutes with duality.

This equivalence readily extends to intermediate flag varieties
like
P/B
, and to the situation where the representations are a countable
union of finite free pieces. We will use the notation
\mathcal{V}:=F_{G/Q}(V)
whenever it is clear we are
working on
G/Q
.

Proposition 3.1.19
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}
or
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
.
Let
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(Q)
. Under the equivalence of
Lemma

3.1.18
we have the following
identifications.

(1)

There exists a canonical isomorphism

F_{G/Q}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V))=D_{G/Q}\otimes F_{G/Q}(V)

compatible with the filtrations on both sides.
If
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}

F_{G/Q}(\textnormal{Ver}^{\vee}_{Q}(V))=P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}.

The second isomorphism extends to
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
, where now

\mathcal{V}^{\vee}:=F_{G/Q}(V^{\vee})
.

(2)

If
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(G)
, then
F_{G/Q}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q,k}(V))^{\vee}=F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
, where
F:G/Q\to(G/Q)^{(p)}

is the Frobenius. The surjection

D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V}=\textnormal{DiffOp}_{G/Q}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee},\mathcal{O})\twoheadrightarrow D^{[p]}_{G/Q}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee},\mathcal{O})

from
3.1.16
corresponds under the equivalence of
Lemma

3.1.18

to the surjection

(3.1.3)

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\twoheadrightarrow\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q,k}(V)

of
Q
-modules induced by
U\mathfrak{g}\to U^{0}\mathfrak{g}
.

(3)

Let
V,W\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(Q)
, and
f:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V^{\vee})\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W^{\vee})
a map of

(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules.
It descends to a map
\overline{f}:\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q}(V^{\vee})\to\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q}(W^{\vee})
. By
(1)

the dual of
f
induces a map

\phi(f)\in\textnormal{DiffOp}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V})
and the dual of
\overline{f}
induces a map

g:F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{W}\to F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}
by part
(2)
. Then
g=F^{*}F_{*}\phi(f)
where

\phi(f):\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{V}
is the map of sheaves associated to
\phi(f)
. In particular

\textnormal{Ker}F^{*}F_{*}\phi(f)=F_{G/Q}(\textnormal{coker}\overline{f})^{\vee}.

Proof.

Let
\pi:G\to G/Q
. For the first part of
(1)
we have to prove that
(D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V})_{\kappa(\infty)}\cong\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)

as
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
modules.
There is a
G
-equivariant map (hence also
\mathfrak{g}
-equivariant)

\pi_{*}(D_{G}\otimes_{R}V)\to D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V}
given as a map of bundles
by
(g,D\otimes v)\mapsto(\overline{g},d\pi(D)\otimes v)
.
Taking its fiber at
\infty
we get a map

\pi_{*}(D_{G}\otimes_{R}V)_{\kappa(\infty)}\to(D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V})_{\kappa(\infty)}

which is
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-equivariant. There is also a
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-equivariant map

U\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{R}V\to\pi_{*}(D_{G}\otimes_{R}V)_{\kappa(\infty)}
by identifying
U\mathfrak{g}
as

G
-equivariant differential operators in
D_{G}
. Thus, we get a
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-equivariant
map
\phi:U\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{R}V\to(D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V})_{\kappa(\infty)}
.
We claim that it factors through
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)
, and then it becomes an isomorphism. Since
\phi
is compatible
with the composition maps
U\mathfrak{g}\otimes U\mathfrak{g}\otimes V\to U\mathfrak{g}\otimes V
and

D_{G/Q}\otimes D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V}\to D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V}
, it is enough to prove the factorization of

\phi

for the degree at most
1
part. The latter follows from the fact that
T_{G/Q,\kappa(\infty)}\cong\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{q}
,
and keeping track of the
Q
-action. Then
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\to(D_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V})_{\kappa(\infty)}
is an isomorphism
since on the associated graded module it is given by the symmetric powers of the degree at most
1
map
\phi^{\leq 1}
.
We now prove the second part of
(1)
. We compute the fiber of
P_{G/Q,R}
at
\infty
on the open Bruhat cell
containing
\infty
. Namely, let
M
be the Levi of
Q
, and
w\in W^{M}
the longest element, then

\infty\in w^{-1}BwQ/Q
. We have that
w^{-1}BwQ/Q\cong\mathbb{A}^{d}
where the map is given
by composition of the
u_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\to G
for
\alpha\in\Phi
not contained in
\textnormal{Lie}(Q)
.
Therefore,
P_{G/Q,R,\kappa(\infty)}
as an
R
-module is the PD polynomial algebra on those

\xi_{\alpha}:=x_{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes x_{\alpha}
.
We define the map
(P_{G/Q,R}\otimes\mathcal{V})_{\kappa(\infty)}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)^{\vee}
as follows.
By choosing appropriate projections we may assume that
V
is free, let
\{v_{i}\}
be a basis for
V
. The map sends

\prod\frac{\xi^{n_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}}{n_{\alpha}!}\otimes v_{i}
to the dual of

(\prod x^{n_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}\otimes v_{i})\in\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)

with respect to the PBW basis induced by
\{x_{\alpha}\}
and
\{v_{i}\}
. One can check that this descends to maps

(P^{n}_{G/Q,R}\otimes\mathcal{V})_{\kappa(\infty)}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)^{\leq n,\vee}
, which coincide with the dual
of the maps defined in the first part. Therefore the map defined is an isomorphism, and it is
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-equivariant.

Part
(2)
was originally proved in
[
27
]
in the case of
Q=B
and
V
a character,
we sketch a proof in general. We have that the fiber at
\infty
of

(F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee})^{\vee}=\underline{\textnormal{Hom}}_{G/Q}(F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee},\mathcal{O})=\underline{\textnormal{Hom}}_{(G/Q)^{(p)}}(F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee},F_{*}\mathcal{O})

is
\textnormal{Hom}_{G_{1}Q/Q}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee},\mathcal{O})
. Here
G_{1}=G[F]\subseteq G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}

is the Frobenius kernel of
G
.
The latter in turn is identified with
\text{Ind}^{G_{1}Q}_{Q}V^{\vee}
.
Moreover, the
the category of
(U^{0}\mathfrak{g},Q)_{k}
-modules is equivalent to the category of

G_{1}Q
-representations.
Under this equivalence, we have that
\text{Ind}^{G_{1}Q}_{Q}V^{\vee}\cong\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q,k}(V)
by
[
36
, Lem 9.2]
,
since the baby Verma is an example of a coinduced representation.
Since
F_{G/Q}
is compatible with duality we obtain the desired isomorphism. The second part of
(2)

can be proved noting that in the étale local coordinates of the proof of
(1)
, the fiber

F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
at
\infty
is generated as a vector space by
\prod x^{n_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}\otimes v
for

\alpha\in\Phi
not contained in
\textnormal{Lie}(Q)
,
0\leq n_{\alpha}<p
and
v\in V^{\vee}
. Then the isomorphism

F_{G/Q}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q,k}(V))^{\vee}=F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
is given by the natural map given these coordinates.
Part
(3)
is equivalent to
proving that the following square commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}_{Q}(V^{\vee},\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W^{\vee}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{DiffOp}_{G/Q}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}_{Q}(V^{\vee},\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q,k}(W^{\vee}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{[p]}_{G/Q}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

For this, one needs to check that the dual of
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W^{\vee})\to\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{Q,k}(W^{\vee})
corresponds under point
(1)

and
(2)
to the map

F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{W}\to P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}
defined in
3.1.16
.
This is part of point
(2)
.
∎

With the above we can upgrade
Lemma

3.1.18
to the category of
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules.
We work over
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
.

Proposition 3.1.20
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
.

(1)

Let
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
. Then
F_{G/Q}(V^{\vee})
is a
G
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaf on
(G/Q)_{R}

which is moreover equipped with a
G
-equivariant HPD stratification. Conversely, if

\mathcal{W}
is a
G
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaf (which is a countable union of coherent sheaves)
equipped with a
G
-equivariant HPD stratification,
then
\mathcal{W}=F_{G/Q}(W)
for
W
a
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-module satisfying property
2
in
3.1.4
.
Moreover, whenever they are both defined, these two procedures are inverse to each other.

(2)

If
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(G)
then
\mathcal{V}\cong V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G/Q}

G
-equivariantly and the associated
HPD stratification by part
(1)
is the trivial one.

(3)

(Tensor identity) For
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
, there exists an isomorphism of

(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules
\phi_{V}:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\cong V\otimes\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(1)
such that
under
3.1.19
(1) its dual induces
the HPD stratification on
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}:=F_{G/Q}(V^{\vee})
given by part
(1)
.

(4)

For
W
a filtered colimit of elements of
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
, the isomorphism

F_{G/Q}\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)^{\vee}\cong P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}

from
3.1.19
(1) is an isomorphism of
G
-equivariant
sheaves with HPD stratifications, where we are using the stratification defined by
(1)

on the left-hand side, and the stratification of

3.1.14
(3) on the right-hand side.

Proof.

We can identify the
Q
torsor
G\mapsto G/Q
with trivializations of

\Lambda\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G/Q}
respecting the Hodge tensors
s_{\alpha}
and the flag corresponding to

Q
. Therefore, by
Remark

2.2.13

if
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
, then
F_{G/Q}(V^{\vee})
is
equipped with a
G
-equivariant flat connection, using the trivial connection on

\Lambda\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G/Q}
. We check that it is quasi-nilpotent, so that it extends to a HPD
stratification, which will be automatically
G
-equivariant.
Given a local section
\phi\in Q
, together with the trivial connection
it induces the element
\xi_{\phi}\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{G/Q}
.
There exist some étale local sections
\{x_{i}\}
for
G/Q
such that each
x_{\phi,i}:=\partial_{x_{i}}\circ\xi_{\phi}\in\mathfrak{g}

is nilpotent, since the trivial connection on
\Lambda\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G/Q}
is
quasi-nilpotent.
That is,
x_{\phi,i}
is in the span of
\{x_{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Phi\}
. If
x_{\gamma}\in\textnormal{Lie}(Q)
then it acts
locally nilpotently on
V^{\vee}
since the action of
Q
is algebraic. If
x_{\gamma}\in\textnormal{Lie}(B^{-})
, let

\psi\in V^{\vee}
factoring through

\pi:V\twoheadrightarrow V_{1}
with
V_{1}\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
. Then, for
n
large enough

\pi(x^{n}_{\gamma}V)=0
, since the weights of
V
are bounded above. Therefore

x^{n}_{\gamma}\psi=0
, so that
x_{\phi,i}
acts locally nilpotently on
V^{\vee}
. Since

\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}}(\phi,\psi)=(\phi,x_{\phi,i}\psi)

this implies that
the connection is quasi-nilpotent. Now let
\mathcal{W}
as in the second part of
(1)
. We can write it as

\mathcal{W}=F_{G/Q}(W)
for
W
a
Q
-module. The HPD stratification on
\mathcal{W}
induces a map

\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}\otimes P_{G/Q}\cong P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}
.
Under the identification
3.1.19
(1) its dual defines a map

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)\to W
, which defines the
U\mathfrak{g}
action on
W
.

For
(2)
the isomorphism is given by
(g,v)\mapsto(\overline{g},gv)
.
The fact that the associated connection on
\Lambda\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G/Q}
is the trivial one
follows from
Remark

2.2.13
.
For
(3)
the map
\phi_{V}
is defined by
x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\otimes v\mapsto(x_{1}\otimes 1+1\otimes x_{1})(x_{2}\otimes 1+1\otimes x_{2})\ldots(x_{n}\otimes 1+1\otimes x_{n})v\otimes 1

for
x_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
, and
1\otimes v\mapsto v\otimes 1
. This is an isomorphism by

[
20
, Prop 1.7]
,
and it can easily checked to be
\mathfrak{g}
-equivariant. In fact
\phi_{V}
is an isomorphism even for
R=\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
\mathbb{Z}
is torsion-free we see that it is also
U(Q)
-equivariant over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Then using the PBW theorem we deduce that
\phi_{V}
is
U(Q)
-equivariant for any
R
, hence
it is
Q
-equivariant. To prove that its dual induces the HPD stratification of point
(1)
, we only need to check it at the
degree at most
1
part. There it follows from the way the connection is defined on
F_{G/Q}(V^{\vee})
, and the way its associated
degree
1
stratification is built from the mentioned connection.
For
(4)
, by the second part of
(1)
we just need to check that the
U\mathfrak{g}
-action
on the fiber of
P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}
induced by the HPD stratification
\epsilon_{P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}}

from
3.1.14
(3) agrees with the
U\mathfrak{g}
-action on
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)^{\vee}
.
In the notation of the proof of
3.1.19
(1), on an open Bruhat cell the map

P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}\to P_{G/Q}\otimes P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}
is given by

\prod\frac{\xi^{n_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}}{n_{\alpha}!}\otimes v\mapsto\prod\frac{\xi^{n_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}}{n_{\alpha}!}\otimes 1\otimes v
.
Under the (dual) isomorphism in the proof of
3.1.19
(1) the dual of this map agrees with the natural map

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)
. This means that the fiber at
\infty
of
P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}

is isomorphic to
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)^{\vee}
as elements of
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{Q,R}
.

∎

Remark 3.1.21
.

For
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(Q)
we can define
\textnormal{Ver}^{\text{PD}}_{Q}(V)=U(G)\otimes_{U(Q)}V
.
Then for
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
we also get a tensor identity of the form

\textnormal{Ver}^{\text{PD}}_{Q}(V)\cong V\otimes\textnormal{Ver}^{\text{PD}}_{Q}(1)
since the trivial connection
on
\mathcal{V}
also extends to a (non-PD) stratification.

3.2.
Differential operators on
\mathcal{F}l
and Verma modules

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}

and
V\in\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
. Then
F_{P}(V^{\vee})
is equipped with a flat quasi-nilpotent connection
on
\textnormal{Sh}_{R}
,
which agrees with the one of
2.2.12
whenever
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(G)
. It extends to a log quasi-nilpotent
connection on
\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}_{R}
.

Proof.

To define a flat connection we mimic the construction of
2.2.12
. We prove that it is quasi-nilpotent on
\textnormal{Sh}_{R}
,
the proof on the toroidal compactification is similar. It follows the proof of
3.1.20
(1).
Let
\psi\in V^{\vee}
.
Given a local section
\phi\in P_{\textnormal{dR}}
, recall
\xi_{\phi}\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\textnormal{Sh}_{R}}
from the proof of

2.2.12
. There exist some étale local sections
\{x_{i}\}
such that
x_{\phi,i}:=\partial_{x_{i}}\circ\xi_{\phi}\in\mathfrak{g}

is nilpotent, since the Gauss–Manin connection on
\mathcal{H}
is quasi-nilpotent
Lemma

3.1.15
.
That is,
x_{\phi,i}
is in the span of
\{x_{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Phi\}
. If
x_{\gamma}\in\textnormal{Lie}(Q)
then it acts
locally nilpotently on
V^{\vee}
since the action of
Q
is locally finite. If
x_{\gamma}\in\textnormal{Lie}(B^{-})
, let
let

\psi\in V^{\vee}
factoring through

\pi:V\twoheadrightarrow V_{1}
with
V_{1}\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
. Then, for
n
large enough

\pi(x^{n}_{\gamma}V)=0
, since the weights of
V
are bounded above. Therefore

x^{n}_{\gamma}\psi=0
, so that
x_{\phi,i}
acts locally nilpotently on
V^{\vee}
. Since

\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}}(\phi,\psi)=(\phi,x_{\phi,i}\psi)

this implies that
the connection is quasi-nilpotent.
∎

The goal of this subsection
is to prove the following theorem, which will immediately allow us to construct differential operators out of
maps of Verma modules.

Theorem 3.2.2
.

Let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}/p^{n}\}
.
For any
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
and
W\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(P)
there exist canonical isomorphisms

e_{V}:F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)\cong D_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}}\otimes\mathcal{V},\;\;\;

e_{W}:F_{P}\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(W)\cong D_{\textnormal{Sh}_{R}}\otimes\mathcal{W},\;\;\;

e^{P/B}_{V}:F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(V)\cong D_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}/\textnormal{Sh}_{R}}\otimes\mathcal{V}

which are compatible with the filtrations on both sides, i.e. they induce isomorphisms

e^{\leq n}_{V}:F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq n}_{B}(V)\cong D^{\leq n}_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}}\otimes\mathcal{V}
.
For
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
, let
V\in\mathcal{O}_{B,R}
or
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
,
and
W\in\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
or
W\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(P)
.
Then we have dual isomorphisms

e^{\vee}_{V}:F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}^{\vee}(V)\cong P_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\;\;\;e^{\vee}_{W}:F_{P}\textnormal{Ver}_{P}^{\vee}(W)\cong P_{\textnormal{Sh}_{R}}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}.

Moreover, for
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(B)
there exist isomorphisms

e^{0}_{V}:F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(V)^{\vee}\cong F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee},

where
F:\mathcal{F}l\to\mathcal{F}l^{(p)}
is the relative Frobenius with respect to
\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
. They are
all Hecke equivariant away from
p
, and they satisfy the following properties.

(1)

The map
\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(V)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)
is identified with the map

D_{\mathcal{F}l/\textnormal{Sh}}\otimes\mathcal{V}\to D_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}
induced by

T_{\mathcal{F}l/\textnormal{Sh}}\to T_{\mathcal{F}l/\mathcal{O}}
.

(2)

e^{0}_{V}
is uniquely determined by the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}\otimes\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{P/B}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{0}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}

where
\pi:\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(V)\to\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B}(V)
is the map (
3.1.3
), and

\Pi
is defined in
3.1.16
.

(3)

If
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(W)
is induced
by a map
f:V\to W
of
Q
-representations, then the associated map

D\otimes\mathcal{V}\to D\otimes\mathcal{W}
is

\textnormal{id}\otimes F_{Q}(f)
.

(4)

The map
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V))\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)
given by
x\otimes(y\otimes v)\mapsto xy\otimes v
is sent
to
D\otimes(D\otimes\mathcal{V})\to D\otimes\mathcal{V}
induced by composition of differential
operators on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
or
\mathcal{F}l
.

(5)

On graded pieces of
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)
and
D\otimes\mathcal{V}
,
e_{V}
induces the map

F_{B}(\textnormal{gr}^{n}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V))\cong F_{B}(\textnormal{Sym}^{n}\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{b}\otimes V)\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}e^{1}_{1}\otimes\text{id}}\textnormal{Sym}^{n}T_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}\cong\textnormal{gr}^{n}D_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}
, where
e^{1}_{1}
is defined in
2.6.7
.

(6)

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
. For
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
, consider the isomorphism

\phi_{V}:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\cong V\otimes\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(1)
from
3.1.20
(3).
Then
e^{\vee}_{V}
fits into the following
commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{Q}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{Q}(V^{\vee}\otimes\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(1)^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\otimes P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\phi^{\vee}_{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\vee}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{id}\otimes e^{\vee}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}

where the diagonal map is the isomorphism corresponding to the HPD stratification associated to
the Gauss–Manin connection on

\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
by
Lemma

3.2.1
. The same holds for
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)

and
e^{\leq n,\vee}_{V}
using the PD stratification.

(7)

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
. For
V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
,
the dual of
\phi_{\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)}:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\cong\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)\otimes\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(1)
induces a
HPD stratification on

P\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
under
e^{\vee}_{V}
. It coincides with
\epsilon_{P\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}}
of

3.1.14
(3).

(8)

All the isomorphisms extend to (sub)canonical extensions on toroidal compactifications by replacing
D

with
D^{\log}
and
P
with
P^{\log}
. In
(2)
one just considers
D_{\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}}/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}}}
. They satisfy all the previous properties.

The proof of
Theorem

3.2.2
will take the rest of the subsection. Here
we sketch the strategy of the proof. First we construct local isomorphisms on divided power
formal completions
of points by using the Grothendieck–Messing period map to a flag variety, which allows to transport

3.1.19
to the Shimura variety. Then we construct candidate maps
e_{V}
, and we show that they agree
with the local ones on divided power formal completions, so that we can check all their properties locally.
We could only manage
to construct
e_{V}
in an indirect way. First we reduce to the case of
\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}(V)

by an inductive process relating the maps

\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}(\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq n}(V))\to\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq n+1}(V)
to the algebra structure
on
D
. If
V
is a
G
-representation we can naturally construct

e^{\leq 1}_{V}
via property
(6)
. For an arbitrary
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(Q)
we prove that it is a subquotient
of the restriction to
Q
of a
G
-representation, so that we can construct
e_{V}
by exactness of
F
and
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(-)
.
A priori the inductive process might not be well-defined, but the compatibility with the local isomorphisms
ensures that it is. To construct
e^{\vee}_{V}
we use that the PD stratification given by the
Gauss–Manin connection extends to a HPD stratification, so that we can lift all the isomorphisms
of vector bundles
e^{\leq n,\vee}_{V}
to the desired isomorphism.

Remark 3.2.3
.

Let
\tilde{D}
be Grothendieck’s sheaf of differential operators, and define the variant

\textnormal{Ver}_{P}^{PD}(V)=U(G)\otimes_{U(P)}V
.
It is not true that
F_{P}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P}^{PD}(V))\cong\tilde{D}_{\textnormal{Sh}}\otimes F_{P}(V)
for all
V
in a functorial way. If we had such isomorphisms extending
e_{V}

along the embeddings
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(V)\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Ver}_{P}^{PD}(V)
and

D_{\textnormal{Sh}}\hookrightarrow\tilde{D}_{\textnormal{Sh}}
for both
V
the trivial and
the standard representation
\Lambda
, using the diagram of part 6) together with
Remark

3.1.21

would imply that the Gauss–Manin connection on
\mathcal{H}
over
\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathcal{O}}
extends to a stratification

(\tilde{P}^{n}_{\textnormal{Sh}}\otimes H\cong H\otimes\tilde{P}^{n}_{\textnormal{Sh}})_{n}
.
Take for example
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
the Siegel Shimura variety.
Over a lift of the ordinary locus
W\subset\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
there is a lift
of the Frobenius isogeny
F:A\to A^{\prime}
, and by functoriality the existing PD stratification
respects this morphism.
Since the cokernel of
P^{n}_{\textnormal{Sh}}\to\tilde{P}^{n}_{\textnormal{Sh}}
is
p
-torsion, and all the sheaves are locally free,
we see that the potential stratification on
W
would have to respect
F
too. Therefore, it would
also be compatible with Frobenius on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\text{ord}}
, and hence in all of
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
since
being compatible with Frobenius is
an open condition. Such an extension of
\nabla
to a stratification on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
compatible with the Frobenius
is well-known not to exist, by considering the rank of powers of Verschiebung,
see
[
4
, Ex 2.18]
.

As an immediate corollary from
Theorem

3.2.2
we get a functor from
maps of Verma modules to differential operators on the (flag) Shimura variety.

Theorem 3.2.4
.

Let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},k\}
,
V_{1},V_{2}\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(P)
, and
W_{1},W_{2}\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(B)
.
There exist functorial
embeddings

\Phi_{P}:\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},P)_{R}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(V_{1}),\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(V_{2}))\hookrightarrow\textnormal{DiffOp}_{\textnormal{Sh}_{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1}),

\Phi_{B}:\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{R}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(W_{1}),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(W_{2}))\hookrightarrow\textnormal{DiffOp}_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{2},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{1}),

\Phi_{P/B}:\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{p},B)_{R}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(W_{1}),\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(W_{2}))\hookrightarrow\textnormal{DiffOp}_{\mathcal{F}l/\textnormal{Sh}_{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{2},\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{1}).

If
W_{i}\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(B)
there also exist functorial embeddings

\Phi^{\textnormal{Fr}}_{P/B}:\textnormal{Hom}_{(U^{0}\mathfrak{p},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(V_{1}),\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(V_{2}))\hookrightarrow D^{[p]}_{\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1}).

All the functors extend to functors
\Phi^{\textnormal{can},\textnormal{sub}}
on (sub)canonical extensions on
toroidal compactifications,
by using
log crystalline differential operators.
All the differential operators produced in this way are Hecke equivariant away from
p
.
Moreover, they satisfy the following properties.

(1)

For
f\in\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{p},B)}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(V_{1}),\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(V_{2}))
, let
\overline{f}

be the induced map on baby Verma modules. Then
\Phi_{P/B}(f):\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2}\to\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1}

is Frobenius linear with respect to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
, and it satisfies

F^{*}F_{*}\Phi_{P/B}(f)=F_{B}(\overline{f}^{\vee})

under the canonical isomorphism
e^{0}_{P/B}
, where
F:\mathcal{F}l\to\mathcal{F}l^{(p)}
is the relative Frobenius
with respect to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

(2)

For
\Phi_{B},\Phi_{P},\Phi_{P/B}
, and
\Phi^{\textnormal{Fr}}_{P/B}

composition as maps of
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules on the left matches with composition
of differential operators on the right.

Proof.

Note that
\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},Q)}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{1}),\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2}))=\textnormal{Hom}_{Q}(V_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2}))

and similarly for the
\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{B}
. Moreover, since the
V_{i}
are finitely generated any map must factor through some

\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq n}_{Q}(V_{2})
.
Applying

F_{P}
or
F_{B}
respectively, and then dualizing gives the desired differential operators as in

3.1.10
, by
Theorem

3.2.2
. The maps are embeddings since
F_{P}
is
exact and compatible with duality on
\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(P)
,
and
\textnormal{Hom}_{Q}(V_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2}))=\cup_{n}\textnormal{Hom}_{Q}(V_{1},\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq n}_{Q}(V_{2}))
.
The functors extend
to toroidal compactifications since
Theorem

3.2.2

does, and they are Hecke equivariant away from
p
. Properties
(1)
and
(2)
can be checked on the interior,
being closed conditions.

Part
(1)
follows directly from

Theorem

3.2.2
(2) and the way that
\Pi
is defined in
3.1.16
.
For part
(2)
, given two
Q
-equivariant maps

f_{i}:V_{i}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{i+1})

its composition
is given by

f_{2}\circ f_{1}:V_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2})\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Ver}(f_{2})}\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{3}))\xrightarrow{j}\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{3})
, where
j
is induced from multiplication on
U\mathfrak{g}
.
By part
(3)
and
(4)
of
Theorem

3.2.2
, after dualizing,
applying
F_{Q}
and the canonical isomorphisms,
the previous map is identified with

P^{n+m}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{3}\xrightarrow{\delta}P^{n}\otimes P^{m}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{3}\xrightarrow{\text{id}\otimes\Phi_{Q}(f_{2})}P^{n}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{Q}(f_{1})}\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1}
,
where
\delta
is the coalgebra map on
P
.
This is precisely how composition is defined for elements of

\textnormal{DiffOp}(-,-)
.
∎

Remark 3.2.5
.

Suppose that there exists some open embedding
U\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}l
and a subgroup
Q\subseteq B
together
with a
Q
-reduction
Q_{\textnormal{dR}}\subseteq B_{\textnormal{dR}}
over
U
,
i.e.
B_{\textnormal{dR}}
is the pushout of
Q_{\textnormal{dR}}
along
Q\to B
. This defines a functor

F_{Q}:\textnormal{Rep}(Q)\to\text{Coh}(U)
, and
for
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(B)
by restricting
Theorem

3.2.4
we have

F_{Q}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)\cong F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}V\cong D_{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}
. Therefore we still get a functor

\Phi_{Q}:\textnormal{Hom}_{Q}(V_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V_{2}))\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{U}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1}),

where
V_{i}\in\textnormal{Rep}(Q)
. This situation will occur on the special fiber of
\mathcal{F}l
,
and by this procedure we will construct
the basic theta operators.

3.2.1.
The isomorphism of divided formal completions of points

Here we construct the isomorphisms of
Theorem

3.2.2
on PD formal completions of points, using
the Grothendieck–Messing period map.
Fix
y\in\mathcal{F}l(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
mapping to

x\in\textnormal{Sh}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
.

Definition 3.2.6
.

Let
(R,I,\gamma_{i})
be a ring with divided powers over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•

Define the sheaf of PD differential forms

\Omega^{1}_{(R,I)}
as the quotient of the sheaf of differentials
\Omega^{1}_{R/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}

by imposing the relations
d\gamma_{n}(x)=\gamma_{n-1}(x)dx
for all
x\in I
and
n\geq 1
.

•

Let
I_{\Delta}\subset D_{I_{\Delta}}(R\otimes R)

be the ideal of
the diagonal inside its divided power envelope, which we also denote as
P_{(R,I)}
.
Then the sheaf of divided power differentials can be identified with

I_{\Delta}/(I^{[2]}_{\Delta}+K^{[2]})
, where
K

is generated by elements of the form
\gamma_{m}(x)\otimes 1-1\otimes\gamma_{m}(x)
for

x\in I
,
m\geq 1

[
70
,
Tag 07HT
]
.

•

Define
P^{n}_{(R,I)}\coloneqq P_{(R,I)}/(I^{[n+1]}_{\Delta}+K^{[n+1]})

with the natural bimodule structure and coalgebra structure,
and let
D^{n}_{(R,I)}
be its dual.
Then
\text{gr}^{\bullet}P_{(R,I)}=\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}\Omega^{1}_{(R,I)}
.

•

If
f:(R,I)\to(S,J)
is a map
of PD pairs, the usual differential extends to a map
df:f^{*}\Omega^{1}_{(R,I)}\to\Omega^{1}_{(S,J)}
,
and a map
f^{*}:f^{*}P_{(R,I)}\to P_{(S,J)}
which on graded pieces is induced by
df
.

•

For
\mathfrak{X}=\text{Spf}(\varprojlim R/I_{n})
a formal scheme with an implicit divided power ideal

J\subset I_{0}
, let
P_{\mathfrak{X}}:=\varprojlim P_{(R/I_{n},J+I_{n})}
and

D_{\mathfrak{X}}\coloneqq\cup_{n}\textnormal{Hom}(P^{n}_{\mathfrak{X}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X}})
.
In what follows, we will only
use this for
\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}
and
\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}
.

Recall that the differentials of the Grothendieck–Messing period maps induce isomorphisms

T_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}}\cong\pi^{*}_{P}T_{G/P^{\wedge}_{\infty}}
and

T_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}}\cong\pi^{*}_{B}T_{G/B^{\wedge}_{\infty}}
. We are again fixing a trivialization
\phi

of
M_{0}
for the definition of
\pi_{B,P}
. In particular, by the property that

\text{gr}^{\bullet}P_{(R,I)}=\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}\Omega^{1}_{(R,I)}
, the maps

\pi_{P}

and
\pi_{B}
induce isomorphisms between their respective sheaves of crystalline differential operators
and their duals
P
.
In general let
f:X\to Y
be a map of schemes (or PD formal schemes) such that

df:D_{X}\to f^{*}D_{Y}
is an isomorphism, and let
V
be a quasicoherent sheaf on
Y
. Define
the isomorphism

(3.2.1)

df_{V}:D_{X}\otimes f^{*}V\to f^{*}(D_{Y}\otimes V)

as follows.
Locally, let
f:A\to C
be a map of rings,

B
an
A
-bimodule,
V
a left
A
-module,
D
a
C
-bimodule and
\psi:C\otimes_{A}B\cong D

an isomorphism of left
C
modules which respects the right
A
module structure on both sides. Then
\psi

extends to an isomorphism
\varphi:C\otimes_{A}(B\otimes_{A}V)\to D\otimes_{C}(C\otimes_{A}V)
given by

c\otimes b\otimes v\mapsto\psi(c\otimes b)\otimes(1\otimes v)
. Define
df_{V}
to be the inverse of
\varphi
.
On graded pieces
df_{V}
is given by
\textnormal{Sym}^{\bullet}df
on the left and the identity
on
f^{*}V
.
One can check the following compatibility with composition: with
f
and
V
as above
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{X}\otimes f^{*}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(D_{Y}\otimes V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{X}\otimes D_{X}\otimes f^{*}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{X}\otimes f^{*}(D_{Y}\otimes V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(D_{Y}\otimes D_{Y}\otimes V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces df_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\otimes\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes df_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces df_{D_{Y}\otimes V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(\mu\otimes\text{id})}

commutes. Similarly, if
f^{*}:P_{Y}\to f^{*}P_{X}
is an isomorphism we define

(3.2.2)

df^{\vee}_{V}:P_{X}\otimes f^{*}V\to f^{*}(P_{Y}\otimes V)

in the same way as above, using that
P_{X}
is also a
bimodule.
The next result defines the canonical isomorphisms of
Theorem

3.2.2
on divided power
formal completions of points. Recall the notation
j:\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Sh}
,

i:\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}l
.

Proposition 3.2.7
.

Let
y\in\mathcal{F}l(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
and
x\in\textnormal{Sh}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
its image, define
\pi_{B/P}
for some
fixed choice of
\phi
as in
2.7.2
. Let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},k\}
and
let
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(P),W\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(B)
. Define the following composition of isomorphisms,

e_{V,x}:j^{*}(D_{\textnormal{Sh}}\otimes F_{P}(V))\cong D_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}}\otimes\pi_{P}^{*}F_{G/P}(V)\xrightarrow{d\pi_{P,V}}\pi^{*}_{P}(D_{G/P^{\wedge}_{\infty}}\otimes F_{G/P}(V))\cong j^{*}F_{P}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(V))

e_{W,y}:i^{*}(D_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F_{B}(W))\cong D_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}}\otimes\pi_{B}^{*}F_{G/B}(W)\xrightarrow{d\pi_{B,W}}\pi^{*}_{B}(D_{G/B^{\wedge}_{\infty}}\otimes F_{G/B}(W))\cong i^{*}F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(W)),

where the isomorphisms on the sides come from
2.7.2
(1) and
3.1.19
,
and the ones in the middle are defined
by (
3.2.1
). The first two isomorphisms above induce an isomorphism

e_{P/B,W,y}:j^{*}(D_{\mathcal{F}l/\textnormal{Sh}}\otimes F_{B}(W))\cong D_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}/\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}}\otimes\pi^{*}_{B}F_{G/B}(W)\to j^{*}F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(W)).

Similarly, for
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
and
V\in\mathcal{O}_{B,R}
or
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(B)
there is an isomorphism

e^{\vee}_{V,x}:j^{*}(P_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee})\cong P_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{x}}\otimes\pi^{*}_{B}F_{B}(V^{\vee})\xrightarrow{df^{\vee}_{V^{\vee}}}\pi^{*}_{B}(P_{G/B}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee})=j^{*}(F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}(V)^{\vee})

over
\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y,R}
.
Then
e_{V,x}
,
e_{V,y}
,
e_{P/B,W,y}
, and
e^{\vee}_{V,x}
do not depend on the choice of
\phi
.
If
W\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(B)
there is also a canonical
isomorphism

e^{0}_{W,y}:j^{*}F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B}(W))\cong j^{*}(F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{W}^{\vee})^{\vee},

where
F:\mathcal{F}l\to\mathcal{F}l^{(p)}
is the relative Frobenius
with respect to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.
Furthermore, these local isomorphisms satisfy the same properties

(1)
to
(7)
as in
Theorem

3.2.2
.

Proof.

By the definition of
D
on divided power formal schemes
we get maps
d\pi_{P}:D_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}}\to\pi^{*}_{P}D_{G/P^{\wedge}_{\infty}}
and

d\pi_{B}:D_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}}\to\pi^{*}_{B}D_{G/B^{\wedge}_{\infty}}

that respect the natural
\mathcal{O}_{G/Q^{\wedge}_{\infty}}
-bimodule
structure on both sides, and similarly for
P_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}}
. We
then define the maps
d\pi_{P,V}
as in (
3.2.1
).
Using the first diagram of
2.7.3

we see that on graded pieces
e_{V,x}
is induced by the Kodaira–Spencer, so that it is an isomorphism.
Similarly, on graded pieces
e_{W,y}
is induced by the symmetric powers of the isomorphism
e^{1}_{1}

from
Lemma

2.6.5
, so it is also an isomorphism.
Both are independent of the choice of
\phi
, since both
\pi_{Q,V}
and the isomorphisms from
2.7.2
(1)
vary in the same way when changing
\phi
. Namely, if we use the notation
\pi_{Q,\phi}

to emphasize the dependence on
\phi
, then
\pi_{Q,g\phi}=g\circ\pi_{Q,\phi}

for
g\in G(\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
.
The map
e_{P/B,y}
is induced by the cartesian diagram on
2.7.2
, which identifies
the relative differentials
D_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}/\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}}
and
D_{(G/B)^{\wedge}_{\infty}/(G/P)^{\wedge}_{\infty}}

under
\pi_{B}
, and then

3.1.19
identifies
D_{(G/B)^{\wedge}_{\infty}/(G/P)^{\wedge}_{\infty}}
with
\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(1)
.
For
e^{\vee}_{V}
we use (
3.2.2
) to define it, it is independent of
\phi

since if we write
V=\cup V_{n}
with
V_{n}\in\textnormal{Rep}(B)
, it is given by the dual of
e^{\leq N}_{V_{n},x}
on all the corresponding
quotients.

Let
W\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(B)
. There is an isomorphism
j^{*}F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B}(W))\cong(G^{*}G_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{W}^{\vee})^{\vee}
, where in the right-hand side

G
is the relative Frobenius with respect to
\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}\to\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\sharp}_{x}
.
It follows from the corresponding isomorphism
on the flag variety
P/B
in
3.1.19
(2), and the cartesian diagram in
2.7.2

that identifies both relative Frobenius.
To obtain
e^{0}_{W,y}
we claim that
there is a canonical isomorphism
G^{*}G_{*}j^{*}E\cong j^{*}F^{*}F_{*}E

for
E
a vector bundle. After localizing on
\mathcal{F}l
we may assume that
E=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l}
.
Write
\mathcal{F}l\to\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
locally as
A\to B
,
\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}\to\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\sharp}_{x}

as
\tilde{A}\to\tilde{B}
and
j:B\to\tilde{B}
. By smoothness, we may assume that

\tilde{A}=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[[\frac{x^{n}_{i}}{n!}]]
and
\tilde{B}=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[[\frac{x^{n}_{i}}{n!},y_{j}]]
.
The sheaf
G^{*}G_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}}
is finite locally free,
since one can check that
G
is flat and of finite presentation.
There is a map
\phi:j^{*}F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l}\to G^{*}G_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}}

as follows. Locally it is given by

\tilde{B}\otimes_{B}(B\otimes_{B\otimes_{A,F}A}B)\to\tilde{B}\otimes_{\tilde{B}\otimes_{\tilde{A},G}\tilde{A}}\tilde{B}
sending

1\otimes x\otimes y\mapsto j(x)\otimes j(y)
. Since both the domain and target are finite locally free of
the same rank
it is enough to check whether the determinant of
\phi
is a unit in
\tilde{B}
. The map
\tilde{B}\to(\tilde{B}/\mathfrak{m}_{\tilde{A}}\tilde{B})
sends non-units to non-units, so we can check it
after replacing
\tilde{B}
by
\tilde{B}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}\tilde{B}
, and

B
by
B/\mathfrak{m}_{A}B
. Relabelling everything,

\tilde{B}\to\tilde{A}=A=\kappa(x)
becomes
the formal completion of
B
at
\kappa(y)
,
F
is the Frobenius for
B/\kappa(y)
and

G
the Frobenius for
\tilde{B}/\kappa(y)
. We can work étale locally since the formal completions don’t change
and the Frobenius pushforward commutes with étale base change.
Thus, we can reduce to affine space, where it can be checked by hand.

Properties
(1)
and
(2)
follow by construction of
e_{P/B,V}
and
e^{0}_{V}
,
and the second diagram in
2.7.3
.
Property
(3)
follows directly from the associated statement on the flag variety, and
d\pi
being functorial.
Property
(4)
follows from the statement on the flag variety and diagram
(
3.2.1
).
Part
(5)
follows from
2.7.3
and the statements in

3.2.6
. For
(6)

it is sufficient to prove it after restricting to the degree at most
1
filtered piece of the filtration,
since the Gauss–Manin connection
extends uniquely to a
HPD stratification (and
e^{\vee}_{1,y}
is just given by the differential of
\pi_{B}
). The commutativity of the diagram is equivalent to
\nabla
on
\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}
being
the pullback connection along
\pi_{P}
of the trivial connection on
F_{G/P}(V)\cong V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G/P^{\wedge}_{\infty}}
, since the tensor identity is precisely
the one inducing the trivial connection on
F_{G/P}(V)
. We can reduce to the case where
V=\Lambda

from the definition of
\nabla
,
where this is precisely
2.7.2
(2). For
(7)
it follows from
3.1.20
(3) and the fact that
the stratification
\epsilon
of
3.1.14
(3) is compatible with maps that induce an isomorphism
on differentials, which
can be checked on étale local coordinates.
∎

3.2.2.
Definition of the canonical isomorphisms

We construct the canonical isomorphism
e_{V}
of
Theorem

3.2.2
,
by reducing to the case of
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}^{\leq 1}(V)

for
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
.

Lemma 3.2.8
.

Let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}/p^{n}\}
.
Let
Q\in\{P,B\}
. Every element of
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
is a subquotient as a
Q
-module of
the restriction to
Q
of an element of
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)
.

Proof.

Let
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
. Write it as
V=\oplus_{i}(R/p^{i})^{n_{i}}
and let

\pi_{i,j}:V\to R/p^{i}
be the corresponding
projections.
The map
V\to\oplus\mathcal{O}^{j}_{Q,R/p^{i}}
sending
v\to(g\mapsto\pi_{i,j}(gv))
defines an
injective map of
Q
-representations, where
Q
acts on
\mathcal{O}_{Q}
by
q\cdot f(x)=f(q^{-1}x)
.
For each
i,j
the restriction map
\phi:\mathcal{O}^{j}_{G,R/p^{i}}\to\mathcal{O}^{j}_{Q,R/p^{i}}
is a surjection of
Q
-representations.
Let
\{v_{i,j}\}
be the generators of
\oplus\mathcal{O}^{j}_{Q,R/p^{i}}
.
By
[
67
, §1.5 Prop 2]
there exists
W\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
which
contains some choice of elements
w_{i,j}\in\phi^{-1}(v_{i,j})
, and which is contained in
\phi^{-1}(V)
,
so that
W
maps surjectively to
V
. Similarly, there exists
M\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)

containing
W
and contained in
\oplus\mathcal{O}^{j}_{G,R/p^{i}}
. Then
W\subset M
gives a presentation
of
V
as a subquotient.
∎

We prove a small lemma that will allow us to check all the properties of the canonical
isomorphisms on PD formal completions.
To apply it in our case, we will use
that one can cover
\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathcal{O}}
by connected affine open subsets that have at least one
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point.
This follows from the fact that
\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}
is proper,
so no connected component of it can live in characteristic
0
.

Lemma 3.2.9
.

Let
R/\mathcal{O}
be a smooth connected algebra of finite type having at least one
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point.
Let
M,N
be
R
-modules which are the sum of a finite free part and finitely many summands of the form

R/p^{n}
,
and let
\psi:M\to N
be a map between them. Then
\psi
is
0
if and only if it is
0

after base change to every PD formal completion of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-points. Alternatively, if
S/k
is
smooth connected of finite type,
M,N
locally free finite
S
-modules, and
\phi:M\to N
a map, then
\phi
is zero if and only if
it is zero on every
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point.

Proof.

The map
\psi
decomposes into maps between free modules and maps of the form
R\to R/p^{n}
or

R/p^{m}\to R/p^{n}
.
For the former
the lemma follows since under the assumptions on
R

the composition
R\to R_{\mathfrak{m}}\to R^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{m}}\to R^{\sharp}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is injective for
any
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point
\mathfrak{m}
. The last injection can be checked using étale coordinates, and will
be of the form
\mathcal{O}[[x_{i}]]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}[[\frac{x^{n}_{i}}{n!}]]
.
For the latter
R/p^{n}\to R^{\sharp}_{\mathfrak{m}}/p^{n}
is not necessarily injective for a particular
\mathfrak{m}
,
but any element in the kernel will be contained in
\mathfrak{m}
. Therefore, an element
x

that is killed for all maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
above
p

will be contained in
pR
, since
R\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a Jacobson ring.
Writing
x=py
, since
R^{\sharp}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
p
-torsion free, we see that
n=1
or

y
is also in the kernel
for every
\mathfrak{m}
,
so that by induction
x=0
. The second statement follows easily since
S
is Jacobson, and
\phi
can be locally
written as a matrix.
∎

We define
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
using its relation to the Gauss–Manin connection prescribed by

Theorem

3.2.2
(6). We remark that the functors
F_{Q}
restricted to
\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{y}
are still exact over

\textnormal{Rep}_{\mathcal{O}}(Q)
, since
divided power formal completions are
\mathcal{O}
-flat.

Proposition 3.2.10
.

Let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}/p^{n}\}
.
Define
e^{\leq 1}_{1}:F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}(1))\cong F_{B}(1\oplus\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{b})\cong\mathcal{O}\oplus T_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}}\cong D^{\leq 1}_{\mathcal{F}l}

where the isomorphism in the middle is given by
2.6.7
.

(1)

For
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)
define
e^{\leq 1}_{V}

by the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}^{\leq 1}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(V\otimes_{R}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}^{\leq 1}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(V)\otimes D^{\leq 1}_{\mathcal{F}l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\leq 1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F_{B}(V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\leq 1}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(\phi_{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{id}\otimes e^{\leq 1}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}

where
\phi_{V}
is the tensor identity of
3.1.20
, and
\nabla
is the dual of the
Gauss–Manin connection on
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}/\mathcal{F}l_{R}
of

2.2.12
, seen as an isomorphism
P^{1}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\cong\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\otimes P^{1}
.
The definition makes sense since the horizontal and diagonal maps are isomorphisms.

(2)

Let
i:V\hookrightarrow W
be a
B
-equivariant embedding, where
W\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)

and
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
. Then

e^{\leq 1}_{W}
sends
F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}^{\leq 1}(V))
to
D^{\leq 1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F_{B}(V)\subseteq D^{\leq 1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F_{B}(W)
.
We define
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
as the restriction of
e^{\leq 1}_{W}
, by exactness of
F_{B}
and
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-)
.
It is an isomorphism independent of the embedding
i
.

(3)

Let
\pi:W\twoheadrightarrow V
be a surjection in
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
with
W
a submodule of
an element of
\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)
. Then

e^{\leq 1}_{W}
from point
(2)
sends
F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}^{\leq 1}(\textnormal{Ker}\pi))
to
D^{\leq 1}\otimes F_{B}(\textnormal{Ker}\pi)
, so that
we can define
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
as the induced map. It is an isomorphism independent of the surjection.

(4)

Let
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
. By
Lemma

3.2.8
it is a subquotient of a
G
-representation,
so we define
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
via the previous two points. It is an isomorphism
independent of the presentation as a subquotient. For
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(P)
we define
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
analogously.

Proof.

We prove
(2)
and
(3)
. The independence of embedding/quotient follows by comparing any two of them with their sum,
since the construction of
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
in
(1)
is clearly functorial.
Let
R=\mathcal{O}
first. By
Lemma

3.2.9
we can check well-definedness
on PD formal completions of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
points, e.g.
for
(2)
we have to prove that

F(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}^{\leq 1}(V))\to D^{\leq 1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F_{B}(W/V)
is
0
.
The key fact is that by
3.2.7
the diagram in
(1)
defining
e^{\leq 1}_{W}
agrees with the local
map
e^{\leq 1}_{W,x}
at every PD formal completion. Then by naturality of the local isomorphisms
parts
(2)
and
(3)
hold on PD formal completions. This shows that the maps
e^{\leq 1}

are well-defined, and they are isomorphisms
since one can construct an inverse by the same procedure, using
the inverse map on
W
. For
R=k
we use the Grothendieck–Messing map mod

(p,\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p})
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
is the maximal ideal of some
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point. Since
\mathfrak{m}_{x}

has divided powers we still have that the restriction of
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
agrees with the isomorphisms
in
3.2.7
. Then we use the second part of
Lemma

3.2.9
to conclude.

∎

We define
e^{\leq n}_{V}
by induction on
n
, by leveraging the algebra structure on the sheaf of differential
operators.

Proposition 3.2.11
.

Let
R\in\{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}/p^{n}\}
, and

V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(Q)
. For
n=1

e^{\leq 1}_{V}
is defined in

3.2.10
. For
n\geq 2
the surjection

\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}^{\leq 1}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}^{\leq n-1}V)\twoheadrightarrow\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}^{\leq n}(V)
induces by
3.2.10
a surjection

\mu:D^{\leq 1}\otimes F_{Q}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}^{\leq n-1}(V))\twoheadrightarrow F_{Q}(\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}^{\leq n}(V)).

Let
v\in F_{Q}(\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq n}(V))
be a local section on a small enough open so that it lies in the image of
\mu
,
and let
g=\sum D_{i}\otimes v_{i}
be any element in
\mu^{-1}(v)
.
Inductively define
e^{\leq n}_{V}(v)\coloneqq\sum D_{i}\cdot e^{\leq n-1}_{V}(v_{i})
, where

\cdot:D^{\leq 1}\otimes D^{\leq n-1}\otimes F(V)\to D^{\leq n}\otimes F(V)
is given by composition
of differential operators. Then

e^{\leq n}_{V}
is a well-defined isomorphism independent of the choice of
g
. Moreover, on PD formal completions of

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-points it agrees with the local isomorphisms in
3.2.7
.

Proof.

We can check well-definedness on PD formal completions of points, by
Lemma

3.2.9
.
It follows from property

(4)
at the end of
3.2.7
that
e_{V}
and
e_{V,x}
agree on PD-formal completions
of points, so in particular
e_{V}
is well-defined, and it is independent of the choice of
g
.
By its inductive construction the map
e_{V}
is surjective. Let
N
be its kernel.
For every
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point
x
of
Sh
we have that
N_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\sharp}_{x}}=0
by
3.2.7
,
so in particular
N_{\kappa(x)}=0
.
Since

Sh
has no closed point in characteristic
0
, and
N
is finitely generated as a
\mathcal{O}_{\textnormal{Sh}}
-module
being the kernel of a map between coherent sheaves,
we get that
N=0
by Nakayama’s lemma.
Thus, the map is an isomorphism.
∎

We extend the definition of the dual canonical isomorphism to
\mathcal{O}_{B,R}
.

Proposition 3.2.12
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
,
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
or
V\in\mathcal{O}_{B,R}
. Write
V=\cup V_{n}
with
V_{n}\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(B)
.
Let
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}=F_{B}(V^{\vee})
.
Then there exists
an isomorphism
e^{\vee}_{V}:F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)^{\vee}\cong P_{\mathcal{F}l_{R}}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
fitting
in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq N,\vee}_{B}(V_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{N}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\vee}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\leq N,\vee}_{V_{n}}}

for all
n,M
,
where
e^{\leq N}_{V_{n}}
are defined in
3.2.11
.
The analogous statement holds for
\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
.

Proof.

Note that if
e^{\vee}_{V}
exists and it fits in the diagram, then it is unique and it is an isomorphism.
Using the
e^{\leq N,\vee}_{V_{n}}
we can define a map

F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)^{\vee}\to[P_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}]^{\wedge}
,
where the right-hand side is the formal completion with respect to the cofiltration
P^{N}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{n}
.
We want to prove that it factors through

P_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
. We can check this on
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
points. Locally we have that

[P_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}]^{\wedge}/[P_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}]

is of the form
\prod_{I}S/\oplus_{I}S

for
S/R
as in

Lemma

3.2.9
. Then we can check that an element of

\prod_{I}S/\oplus_{I}S
is zero if and only if it is so on each geometric point.
Then it follows from the properties of
3.2.7
.

∎

We now combine the results above to prove
Theorem

3.2.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.2.2
.

The isomorphisms
e_{V}
for
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(V)
and
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)
are defined in
3.2.11
,
and
e^{\vee}_{V}
is defined in
3.2.12
.
They
agree on PD formal completions with the ones defined on
3.2.7
, by all the functoriality
