and compatibility properties that the local isomorphisms satisfy.
For
e^{P/B}_{V}
,
let
E
be the cokernel of

D_{\mathcal{F}l/\textnormal{Sh}}\otimes F(V)\to D_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F(V)
. We claim that the composition

F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(V))\to F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V))\xrightarrow{e_{V}}D_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F(V)\to E
is
0
.
By
Lemma

3.2.9
it is enough to check it on PD formal completions, where it holds by

3.2.7
(1).
By exactness of
F_{B}
this defines the isomorphism
e^{P/B}_{V}
, and it automatically
satisfies property
(1)
. To define
e^{0}_{V}
we use the same strategy with the square of property
(2)
: now using that we can
check if a map of vector bundles over
\mathcal{F}l_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
is zero on closed points,
so in particular on PD completions of points.
This automatically proves property
(2)
. To check
(3)-(5)
we can use
Lemma

3.2.9
, by considering
the difference of the expected map and the actual map
(to be strict we consider each of these maps on
\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq n}
),
and then it holds over PD formal completions by
3.2.7
.
Part
(6)
for
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)
holds since it does on PD formal completions, for
V\in\mathcal{O}_{B,R}

it holds since it does on every finite free projection.
Part
(7)
also follows from

3.2.7
, since we can check it at the level of PD stratifications,
where the objects are vector bundles.
The isomorphisms
e^{\leq 1}

are Hecke equivariant since the Gauss–Manin connection and the isomorphisms of
2.6.7
are,
so the maps
e^{\leq n}

are also Hecke equivariant by
their inductive definition.

Finally, we construct
e_{V}
on toroidal compactifications. For
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)
,
e^{\leq 1}_{V}

is constructed as in
3.2.10
, using the (sub)canonical extension of Gauss–Manin to a log connection.
To construct
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
for general
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(Q)
we use the same procedure and the fact that one can check whether
a map of sheaves as in
Lemma

3.2.9
vanishes on the interior
\textnormal{Sh}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}
.
It is easy to see that they are isomorphisms, since the ones for
V\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(G)
are by definition.
For
e^{\leq n}

we follow the procedure of
3.2.11
, which is well-defined since it can be checked on the interior.
From its construction we immediately see that
e_{V}
is a surjection, and it is injective since it is so in the interior.
We define
e^{\vee}_{V}
as in
3.2.12
, it is well-defined since we can check the lifting
property on the interior.
One can see that all the required properties can be checked on an open dense subset of
\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}
, so they
follow from the properties on
Sh
.
∎

Remark 3.2.13
.

One could have also constructed
e^{0}_{V}
from
Theorem

3.2.2
via an appropriate base change theorem along the
cartesian diagram (see the next subsection)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt ZipFlag}}}\nolimits}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt Zip}}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

which identifies the two relative Frobenii, and then using the isomorphism of

3.1.19
(2) on
P/B
.

3.3.
Description on the open stratum of the special fiber

We now describe the canonical isomorphisms of
Theorem

3.2.2
more explicitly on some open dense subset

U\subseteq\mathcal{F}l_{k}
. As the main application, in
Theorem

3.3.13

we prove the compatibility with composition of a functor coming from

Remark

3.2.5
, in a way that it produces differential operators over
U
.
Consider
G/\mathbb{F}_{p}
, let
\sigma\in\textnormal{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
be the
p
th power Frobenius,
and let
P^{(p)}=P\times_{k,\sigma}k
.
First we introduce the notation for
G
-Zips. Recall that we have a
tuple
\mathcal{Z}=(T,P,B,G)
of a maximal torus, parabolic, and Borel; all defined over
\mathbb{F}_{p}

except
P
. Let
P^{-}
be the opposite
parabolic, and
M
the Levi of
P
. In this subsection, fix a choice of
B_{0}
fitting
in the embedding
(G,P,B)\hookrightarrow(\textnormal{GL}(\Lambda),P_{0},B_{0})
defined over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. We will invoke
Lemma

2.2.6
whenever a more precise
embedding is needed,
in which case we will use such an embedding.

Definition 3.3.1
.

A
G
-Zip of type
\mathcal{Z}:=(G,P,B)
over some scheme
X/\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a tuple
(I,I_{+},I_{-},\phi)
where
I
is a
G
-torsor
over
X
,

I_{+}\subseteq I
is a
P
-torsor,
I_{-}\subseteq I
a
P^{-(p)}
-torsor, and

\phi:I^{(p)}_{+}/U^{(p)}_{P}\cong I_{-}/U_{P^{-(p)}}
is an isomorphism of
M^{(p)}
-torsors.
Here
I^{(p)}_{+}
, or on any torsor, denotes the pullback along the absolute Frobenius on
X
.
Similarly, a
G
-Zip flag of type
\mathcal{Z}
is a tuple as before together with a
B
-subtorsor

I_{B}\subseteq I_{+}
. We denote by
\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt Zip}}}\nolimits
and
\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt ZipFlag}}}\nolimits
the stacks of
G
-Zips (flags) of type
\mathcal{Z}
.

There is a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt ZipFlag}}}\nolimits}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt Zip}}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{P}}

where the maps
\xi_{P}
and
\xi_{B}
are smooth by
[
77
]
. The map
\xi_{P}
is defined by sending a point of
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}

to the universal
G
-zip
(G_{\textnormal{dR}},P_{\textnormal{dR}},P^{-(p)}_{\textnormal{dR}},\phi)
where

P^{-(p)}_{\textnormal{dR}}=\text{Isom}((\text{Ker }V\subseteq\mathcal{H},s_{\alpha,\textnormal{dR}}),(L^{\vee}\subseteq\Lambda,s_{\alpha})\otimes\mathcal{O})

is a
P^{-(p)}
-torsor through the base change map
P^{-(p)}\to P^{-}
acting on
the right-hand side.
Then
\phi:P^{(p)}_{\textnormal{dR}}/U^{(p)}_{P}\cong P^{-(p)}_{\textnormal{dR}}/U_{P^{-(p)}}
is the isomorphism induced by the Cartier
isomorphisms
\textnormal{Ker}V\cong\omega^{\vee,(p)}_{A^{\vee}}
and
\mathcal{H}/\textnormal{Ker}V\cong\omega^{(p)}_{A}
.
Recall that the pushout of
B_{\textnormal{dR}}
along
B\to B_{0}
defines a refinement
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{H}}
of the
Hodge filtration on
\mathcal{H}
.
On
\mathcal{F}l
let

D_{\mathcal{H},\bullet}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be the ascending full flag refining the conjugate filtration,
which is obtained from
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{H}}
using the isomorphism of graded filtrations
\phi
.
That is, the filtration
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{H}}
on
\mathcal{H}/\omega\cong\omega^{\vee}_{A^{\vee}}

induces a filtration on

\textnormal{Ker}V\cong\omega^{\vee,(p)}_{A^{\vee}}
, and the Hodge filtration on
\omega
induces a filtration on

\mathcal{H}/\textnormal{Ker}V\cong\omega^{(p)}
.
We will take
D_{\mathcal{H},0}=0

and
D_{\mathcal{H},n}=\mathcal{H}
.
Let

B^{z}_{\textnormal{dR}}=\text{Isom}_{\mathcal{F}l}((D_{\mathcal{H},\bullet},s_{\alpha,\textnormal{dR}}),(F^{n-\bullet}_{\Lambda},s_{\alpha})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l}).

It is a torsor for the Borel
B^{z}:=zBz^{-1}
generated by
(B\cap M)^{(p)}
and
U_{P^{-(p)}}
.
This is because
B^{z}
is the intersection of
G
and the Borel of
\textnormal{GL}_{n}
generated by

U_{P^{-(p)}_{0}}
and
B^{(p)}_{0}\cap M^{(p)}_{0}
.
Since
(B,T)
are defined over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, there exists such a
z\in W

[
24
, Lem 2.3.4]
.

Let
(V,B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0})
be some choice of data coming from
Lemma

2.2.6
.
By pushout we can define torsors
\textnormal{GL}(V)_{\textnormal{dR}}:=G_{\textnormal{dR}}\times^{G}\textnormal{GL}(V),P_{V,\textnormal{dR}},B_{0,\textnormal{dR}},P^{-(p)}_{0,\textnormal{dR}},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0,\textnormal{dR}}
.
Then
B_{0,\textnormal{dR}}\subseteq\textnormal{GL}(V)_{\textnormal{dR}}
defines a descending full flag
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}
of
\mathcal{V}

refining the Hodge filtration,
and
B^{z^{\prime}}_{0,\textnormal{dR}}
defines a full flag
D_{\mathcal{V},\bullet}

For a given
(V,B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0})
let
\mathcal{L}_{V,i}:=F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}/F^{n-i+1}_{\mathcal{V}}
, where
n
is the dimension
of
V
.
There is a map
\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt ZipFlag}}}\nolimits\to[B\times B^{z}\backslash G]
,
where
(b,b^{\prime})g=bgb^{{}^{\prime}-1}
, by only remembering the
B
-torsor, and the
B^{z}
-torsor induced
from the
P^{-(p)}
-torsor as above. We compose with the map
[B\times B^{z}\backslash G]\to\textnormal{Sbt}:=[B\times B\backslash G]

induced by multiplication by
z
on the right.
Thus, we get a map
\zeta:\mathcal{F}l\to\textnormal{Sbt}
. Similarly, let
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}}=[B_{0}\times B_{0}\backslash\textnormal{GL}_{n}]
,
there is a map
\textnormal{Sbt}\to\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}}
.

Definition 3.3.2
.

(1)

Let
B_{\textnormal{Sbt}}=[1\times B\backslash G\to\textnormal{Sbt}]
, it is a
B
-torsor such that
\zeta^{*}B_{\textnormal{Sbt}}=B_{\textnormal{dR}}
.

(2)

Let
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}=[B\times B\backslash Bw_{0}B]
be the open Schubert stratum.
Let
(B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0},\textnormal{GL}(V))
be as
Lemma

2.2.6
.
It induces a map
[B\times B^{z}\backslash G]\to[B_{0}\times B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\backslash\textnormal{GL}(V)]
, which
we identify with a map
\textnormal{Sbt}_{G}\to\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}}=[B_{0}\times B_{0}\backslash\textnormal{GL}(V)]
after twisting appropriately.
We will make this identification from now on.
Then
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
maps to

\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}(V),1}=[B_{0}\times B_{0}\backslash B_{0}w_{\textnormal{GL}(V),0}B_{0}]
.
Let
U=\zeta^{-1}(\textnormal{Sbt}_{1})
, it is
an open dense subspace.

By
Lemma

2.2.6

\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
maps to
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}(V),1}
, and we have that the extended Hodge filtration
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}

and the extended conjugate filtration
D_{\mathcal{V},\bullet}
on
\mathcal{V}
are in general position.
In particular there are isomorphisms

H_{n-i}:F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\cap D_{\mathcal{V},n-i+1}\cong{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{V},n-i}:=D_{\mathcal{V},n-i+1}/D_{\mathcal{V},n-i}
for
i=0,1,\ldots,n-1
.
Consequently, the line bundles
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{V},n-i}:=F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\cap D_{\mathcal{V},n-i+1}
provide a splitting of

F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

The
B
-torsor
B_{\textnormal{Sbt}}
on
Sbt
admits a
T
-subtorsor
T_{\textnormal{Sbt}}\subset B_{\textnormal{Sbt}}
over
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
.
Furthermore,
B_{\textnormal{Sbt}}
is the pushout of
T_{\textnormal{Sbt}}
along
T\to B
.

Proof.

It is given by
T_{\textnormal{Sbt}}=[U_{B}\times B\backslash Bw_{0}B]\to[B\times B\backslash Bw_{0}B]
.
∎

Definition 3.3.4
.

Let
T_{\textnormal{dR}}:=\zeta^{*}T_{\textnormal{Sbt}}
be the
T
-torsor over
U
obtained by pullback from
T_{\textnormal{Sbt}}
, it is a
T
-reduction
of
B_{\textnormal{dR}}
.
Let
F_{T}:\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(T)\to\text{Coh}(U)
be the corresponding functor.

We will use the splitting of
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}
to define a local section for
T_{\textnormal{dR}}
in
Lemma

3.3.10
.
Then with respect to such a section we can explicitly describe the canonical isomorphism
e_{V}
.

Lemma 3.3.5
.

Choose
V
and
B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}
as in
Lemma

2.2.6
.
Any element
\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
defines a trivialization of
\mathcal{V}
such that
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}
maps
to the split flag given by
B_{0}
and its splitting corresponds to the splitting of
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}

by the line bundles
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{V},i}
.
Further, if we modify
\phi
by an element of
T
, it will act naturally via
T\to T_{0}
on

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{V},i}
.

Proof.

This can be checked at the level of Schubert stacks, where we can replace
G
by
G^{\text{der}}
.
There it follows from the explicit description of
V
in
Lemma

2.2.6

as a sum of minuscule representations.
∎

It will be useful to consider not only the
G
-Zip structure on
\mathcal{V}
for
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(G)
,
but the structure of
a
de Rham
F
-gauge
. First recall the concept of
p
-curvature.

Lemma 3.3.6
.

[
41
, Thm 5.1]

Let
X/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
be a scheme, and
(E,\nabla)
a vector bundle with connection. The
p
-curvature map

\psi_{\nabla}:T^{(p)}_{X}\to\textnormal{End}_{X}(E)
is defined by

\psi_{\nabla}(D\otimes 1)v=\nabla^{p}_{D}(v)-\nabla_{D^{[p]}}(v)
, for
v\in E
.

(1)

For
V
any vector bundle there is a canonical connection on the Frobenius pullback
V^{(p)}

defined by
\nabla(v\otimes f)=v\otimes df
. We will denote it as the Frobenius connection.

(2)

A vector bundle with connection
(E,\nabla)
has
p
-curvature
0
if and only if one can write

E=V^{(p)}
for some
vector bundle
V
, and
\nabla
is the Frobenius connection. This happens if and only if Zariski locally there exists
a basis of
E
consisting of horizontal sections.

Remark 3.3.7
.

Under the equivalence of
3.1.20
and the isomorphism

F_{G/B}\textnormal{Ver}^{0,\vee}_{B}(V)\cong F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{V}^{\vee}
, we have that the right-hand side
is equipped with the Frobenius connection. A weight of the form
p\lambda
has the structure of a

(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
-module, by defining
x_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\Phi
to act trivially. This is
well-defined since the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
acts trivially on
p\lambda
.
In that case
F_{B}(p\lambda)=F^{*}F_{B}(\lambda)
is also equipped with
the Frobenius connection.

Definition 3.3.8
.

(de Rham
F
-gauge)
Let
X/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
be a scheme. A de Rham
F
-gauge on
X
consists of a tuple

(E,\nabla,F^{\bullet}_{E},D_{E,\bullet},\phi)
where

•

(E,\nabla)
is a vector bundle with a flat connection.

•

F^{\bullet}_{E}
is a descending filtration of
E
satisfying
Griffiths transversality.

•

D_{E,\bullet}
is an ascending filtration on
E
which is horizontal for
\nabla
,
and such that the associated connection on
\text{gr}^{\bullet}_{D}E
has trivial
p
-curvature.

•

\phi
is a graded
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear isomorphism

(\text{gr}_{F}E,\theta)^{(p)}\cong(\text{gr}_{D}E,\psi),

where
\theta=\text{gr}_{F}\nabla:\text{gr}^{\bullet}_{F}E\to\text{gr}^{\bullet-1}_{F}E\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X}

is induced by Griffiths transversality, and

\psi:\text{gr}^{\bullet}_{D}E\to\text{gr}^{\bullet-1}_{D}E\otimes\Omega^{1(p)}_{X}

is induced by
\psi_{\nabla}
and the trivial
p
-curvature condition.

One can also make sense of a de Rham
F
-gauge with a
G
-structure, by considering
G
-torsors with
certain structures. Concretely, one gets the following. Recall
2.2.5
.

Proposition 3.3.9
.

Let
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(G)
, the cocharacter
\mu
induces parabolics
P_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}
and
P^{-(p)}_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}
of
\textnormal{GL}(V)
,
which endow
\mathcal{V}=F_{P}(V)
with filtrations
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
D_{\mathcal{V},\bullet}
.
Our convention is that
F^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{V},F^{n}_{\mathcal{V}}=0
,

D_{\mathcal{V},0}=0,D_{\mathcal{V},n}=\mathcal{V}
, and the indices correspond to jumps in the filtration.
Then

(\mathcal{V},\nabla,F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}},D_{\mathcal{V},\bullet})
is naturally a de Rham
F
-gauge
over
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
. Concretely,
D_{\mathcal{V},\bullet}
is horizontal for
\nabla
,
and the
p
-curvature
\psi
is trivial on
\textnormal{gr}^{\bullet}D_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Given
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(G)
let
V^{\sigma}
be obtained by precomposing with
\sigma
on
G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
.
There is a Cartier isomorphism

D_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma},i}/D_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma},i-1}\cong(F^{i-1}_{\mathcal{V}}/F^{i}_{\mathcal{V}})^{(p)}

for
i\geq 1
, and the map induced from the
p
-curvature

\psi:\textnormal{gr}^{\bullet}D_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}\to\textnormal{gr}^{\bullet-1}D_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}\otimes\Omega^{1,(p)}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}

corresponds to the Frobenius twist of the Kodaira–Spencer map of

(\mathcal{V},\nabla,F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}})

under the Cartier isomorphism. Moreover, if we choose a pair of Borels
(B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0})
of
\textnormal{GL}(V)

such that
B\subseteq B_{0}\subseteq P_{V}
,
B^{z}\subseteq B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\subseteq P^{-(p)}_{V}
,
the
extended conjugate filtration on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{F}l}
is horizontal for
\nabla
and has trivial
p
-curvature
on its graded pieces.

Proof.

The statement that
(\mathcal{V},\nabla,F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}},D_{\mathcal{V},\bullet})
is a de Rham F-gauge
for
V=\Lambda
is a classical result of Katz
[
41
, Thm 3.2]
,
where he moreover proves that
\nabla
on the graded pieces
of the conjugate filtration
\textnormal{Ker}V\subseteq\mathcal{H}

is identified with the Frobenius connection under the Cartier
isomorphism. In general this is explained in
[
68
, §4.2]
.
The Cartier isomorphism comes from unpacking the isomorphism

P^{(p)}_{\textnormal{dR}}/U^{(p)}_{P}\cong P^{-}_{\textnormal{dR}}/U_{P^{-(p)}}
, since on the left-hand side it acts via

\sigma:M^{(p)}\to M
,
which after pushing out to
M^{(p)}_{V}
corresponds to considering the graded pieces of
\mathcal{\textnormal{GL}(V)}^{\sigma}
.
For the statement about the full conjugate filtration, we just observe that since

B^{z}\subseteq B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\subseteq P^{-(p)}_{V}
, it is constructed from
the full Hodge filtration via the Cartier isomorphism, so that each term in

\text{gr}^{\bullet}D_{\mathcal{V}}
is
a Frobenius twist coming from
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma^{-1}}}
,
with
\nabla
being the Frobenius connection. This can be checked from the case
V=\Lambda
,
by functoriality of
\nabla
.
∎

Using this result we can define a special kind of elements in
T_{\textnormal{dR}}
over
U
, which we will use
to describe
e_{V}
over
U
.

Lemma 3.3.10
.

(Adapted elements)
We work over
\mathcal{F}l_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
.
Choose
V,B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}
as in
Lemma

2.2.6
.
Choose some local section
\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
. Via the isomorphisms

H_{i}:F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\cap D_{\mathcal{V},n-i+1}\cong\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V},n-i}=\textnormal{gr}^{n-i}D_{\mathcal{V},\bullet}

this produces
a local basis
\{v_{\bullet}\}
of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V},\bullet}
. We can modify
\phi
by an element of
T
to
ensure that

\nabla_{\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}}_{V,\bullet}}(v_{\bullet})=0
.
We say that such a
\phi
is an adapted element of
T_{\textnormal{dR}}
.

Proof.

Using
3.3.9
and
Lemma

3.3.5
we reduce to the following claim.
Given
x\in T\subseteq T_{0}
there exists an element
t
of
T

such that all the coordinates of
tx
in
T_{0}
are
p
th powers.
After base changing to
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
we can assume that
T
is split,
so that
\mathbb{G}^{k}_{m}\cong T\to T_{0}\cong\mathbb{G}^{n}_{m}

is given by a tuple of cocharacters
\mu_{i}
. Then if

x=(x_{i})
we can take
t=(x^{p-1}_{i})
, so that each
\mu_{i}(x^{p}_{i})
is a
p
th power.

∎

Let

\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
be an adapted element with respect to the data of
Lemma

2.2.6
.
For
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
let
\{\omega_{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}_{U}\}
be the dual basis to

\{e^{1}_{1}(\phi,x_{-\gamma})\in T_{U}\}
from
2.6.7
.
We can describe
\nabla_{\mathcal{V}}
succinctly in this basis.

Proposition 3.3.11
.

Choose
V
as in
Lemma

2.2.6
, and choose an adapted element
\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
with respect to it. Then for all
v\in V
the connection
\nabla
on
\mathcal{V}_{U}
acts as

\nabla(\phi,v)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Phi^{+}}(\phi,x_{-\gamma}v)\otimes\omega_{\gamma}.

Moreover, let
\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
be an adapted element with respect to
V=\Lambda

and some
B_{0}
in
Lemma

2.2.6
. Then the identity above holds for all
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(G)

and
v\in V
.

Proof.

The Borel
B_{0}\subseteq\textnormal{GL}(V)
defines full descending
filtration
F^{\bullet}_{V}
of length
n
.
Let
\{v_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n\}
be a basis for
V
such that
v_{i}
generates

F^{n-i}_{V}/F^{n-i+1}_{V}
, and let
\pi_{i}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l}
be
the projection induced by
v_{i}
and
\phi
.
Then
(\phi,v_{i})
generates
F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\cap D_{\mathcal{V},n-i+1}
, and by construction
of
\phi
it satisfies

\nabla(\phi,v_{i})\in\langle(\phi,v_{i+1}),\ldots,(\phi,v_{n})\rangle\otimes\Omega^{1}_{U}
,
where the convention is that
\nabla(\phi,v_{n})=0
. Let

\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
, and write
x_{-\gamma}v_{i}=cv_{j}+y
,
where
y
is in the span of the rest of the basis vectors,
for some
i<j
and a non-zero constant
c\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.
Then the canonical map
e^{1}_{1}
fits in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{b})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathcal{F}l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B_{0}}(\mathfrak{gl}(V)/\mathfrak{b}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{1}_{1}}

by
Lemma

2.6.5
, since
\textnormal{Lie}(G^{\textnormal{der}})\to\textnormal{Lie}(\textnormal{SL}(V))
is an embedding.
Therefore, we have that

c\omega_{\gamma}=\pi_{j}\nabla(\phi,v_{i})
. Conversely,
if
\pi_{j}\nabla(\phi,v_{i})\neq 0
for some
i<j

it must be that there exists
x\in\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}

such that
v_{j}
appears non-trivially in basis expansion of
xv_{i}
, this follows from the diagram above.
By writing
x
as a sum of negative roots
we see that there exists a
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
such that
v_{j}
appears non-trivially
on
x_{-\gamma}v_{i}
. Moreover, such a
\gamma
is unique up to a scalar,
since otherwise
e^{1}_{1}
wouldn’t be injective.
This reasoning shows that the formula holds for
V=\Lambda
.
For a general
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(G)
it follows from the previous case,
since
\nabla_{\mathcal{V}}
is defined by transporting
\nabla_{\mathcal{H}}
along the action of
\mathfrak{g}
, in

2.2.12
.
∎

Furthermore, relative to an adapted element we can describe the
canonical isomorphisms
e_{V}
in a simple way.

Proposition 3.3.12
.

Let
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{k}(B)
. Let
\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
be an adapted element
with respect to
V=\Lambda
in
Lemma

2.2.6
.
For
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
let
D_{\gamma}=e^{1}_{1}(\phi,x_{-\gamma})\in T_{U}
.
Then
e_{V}:F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)\cong D_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes F_{B}(V)
restricted to
U
is given by

(\phi,\prod_{\gamma\in\Phi^{+}}x_{-\gamma}\otimes v)\mapsto\prod_{\gamma}D_{\gamma}\otimes(\phi,v),

where in the right-hand side the product means a composition of differential operators, in the same order as the
left-hand side.

Proof.

We can reduce to the case of
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
, by the compatibility between composition of differential operators and
the maps
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)\twoheadrightarrow\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(V)
. By the definition of
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
in
3.2.10

we can reduce to the case when
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
.
Let
\{v_{i}\}
be a basis for
V
. The dual of the tensor identity
\phi_{V}:\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}_{B}(V)\cong V^{\vee}\otimes\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}_{B}(1)
is given by
(x_{-\gamma}\otimes v_{i})^{\vee}\mapsto v^{\vee}_{i}\otimes x^{\vee}_{-\gamma}
and

(1\otimes v_{i})^{\vee}\mapsto v^{\vee}_{i}\otimes 1+\sum_{\gamma\in\Phi^{+}}x_{-\gamma}v^{\vee}_{i}\otimes x^{\vee}_{-\gamma}.

By the definition of
e^{\leq 1}_{V}
we see that the proposition is then equivalent to
3.3.11
.
∎

Using
Remark

3.2.5
we get a functor

\Phi_{T}:\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{2}))\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{U}(\mathcal{L}(-\lambda_{2}),\mathcal{L}(-\lambda_{1})).

As opposed to the case of maps of
B
-modules, it is not completely clear how to define composition on
the left-hand side. We show that the naive way is compatible with composition on the right-hand side.
Elements
f\in\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{2}))
can be described uniquely
by an element
x_{f}
of
U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}
,
by the PBW theorem.

Theorem 3.3.13
.

The map

\Phi_{T}:\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{2}))\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{U}(\mathcal{L}(-\lambda_{2}),\mathcal{L}(-\lambda_{1}))

is compatible with composition on both sides, where the composition on the left-hand side is defined as follows.
Let
f\in\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda_{1},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{2}))
and
g\in\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda_{2},\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{3}))
,
then
g\circ f
is defined by
x_{f}x_{g}\in U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}
.

Proof.

By the proof of
Theorem

3.2.4
(2)
it is enough to prove that for a
T
-equivariant map

f:\lambda_{1}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{2})

the map
h:\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{1})\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{2}))
defined by
\prod x\otimes\lambda_{1}\mapsto\prod x\otimes x_{f}\otimes\lambda_{2}
is identified with
D_{U}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{1})\xrightarrow{\text{id}\otimes F(f)}D_{U}\otimes D_{U}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{2})
after applying
F_{T}
and the canonical isomorphisms from

Theorem

3.2.2
. Once we write both
x
and
x_{f}
in a PBW basis this follows immediately
from
3.3.12
.
∎

4.
The basic theta operators

We construct the basic theta operators, and we prove several properties about them. We start by recalling
the construction of Hasse invariants on
\mathcal{F}l
.

4.1.
The Hasse invariants

Recall the map
\zeta:\mathcal{F}l\to\textnormal{Sbt}=[B\times B\backslash G]
from
Section

3.3
. For
\alpha\in\Delta
denote
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}

the locally closed stratum of
Sbt
corresponding to the orbit
Bs_{\alpha}w_{0}B
, and let

\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
be its closure. Let
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
be the open stratum corresponding to
Bw_{0}B
.
Let
\overline{D}_{\alpha}=\zeta^{-1}\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
, and
D_{\alpha}=\zeta^{-1}\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}
.
The complement of
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
is covered by the union of
\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Delta
,
and
\textnormal{Sbt}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}
has codimension at least
2
in
Sbt
. For a pair of weights

\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)
let
\mathcal{L}(\lambda,\mu)
be the line bundle of
Sbt
coming from
(\lambda,\mu)

as a character of
B\times B
. If
\mu=0
we will also write
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
.

We define the Hasse invariants on
\mathcal{F}l
as in
[
34
, §5.1]
.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
. For
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in X^{*}(T)
we have that
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sbt},\mathcal{L}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}))\neq 0

implies
\chi_{2}=-w_{0}\chi_{1}
, in which case the space is
1
-dimensional. There exists a character

\chi_{\alpha}\in X^{*}(T)
such that

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sbt},\mathcal{L}(-\chi_{\alpha},w_{0}\chi_{\alpha}))
is
1
-dimensional, and the divisor of any non-zero section

\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
is

\textnormal{div}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha})=N_{\alpha}\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha},

with
N_{\alpha}\geq 1
minimal. Moreover, the projection of
\chi_{\alpha}
to
X^{*}(T^{\textnormal{der}})
is unique.
More precisely,
N_{\alpha}
is the minimal positive integer such that there exists
\chi_{\alpha}\in X^{*}(T)

such that
\langle\chi_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=N_{\alpha}
and
\langle\chi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle=0

for all
\beta\in\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}
.
In particular, if
G^{\textnormal{der}}
is simply connected then
N_{\alpha}=1
.

Proof.

The first statement is
[
23
, Thm 2.2.1(a)]
.
According to Chevalley’s formula
[
23
, Thm 2.2.1(c)]

the divisor of
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
is
\sum_{\beta\in\Delta}\langle\chi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\beta}
.
If
G^{\text{der}}
is simply connected we can find a
\chi_{\alpha}
such that

\langle\chi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle
is
1
if
\alpha=\beta
and zero otherwise,
and such a character is unique in
X^{*}(T^{\text{der}})
. In general, by considering the index of

X^{*}(T)
in the weight lattice of the simply connected cover,
there will exist a
\chi_{\alpha}
such that
\langle\chi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle

is zero for
\alpha\neq\beta
,
and positive for
\alpha=\beta
.
∎

We denote by
H_{\alpha}
the pullback of some choice of
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
to
\mathcal{F}l
, then

N_{\alpha}
in
Lemma

4.1.1
is the order of vanishing of
H_{\alpha}
.
The weight of

H_{\alpha}
as a section of
\mathcal{L}(h_{\alpha})
is

(4.1.1)

h_{\alpha}=-\chi_{\alpha}+pw_{0,M}\sigma^{-1}\chi_{\alpha},

where

\sigma
is the arithmetic Frobenius acting on
X^{*}(T)
. This can be computed by remembering the twist by
z\in W

in
\mathcal{F}l\to\textnormal{Sbt}
,
and using the formula
z=\sigma(w_{0,M})w_{0}
from
[
34
, Lem 2.2.1]
.
Note that we get a different sign compared to

[
34
, §5.1]
,
since
we use different conventions for the simple roots. Also, for applications, in characteristic
0
, if
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)

are such that their projection to
X^{*}(T^{\text{der}})
is the same, then their associated
line bundles
\mathcal{L}(\mu)

and
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
on
\mathcal{F}l
are isomorphic, since they are pulled back from
G/B=G^{\text{der}}/B^{\text{der}}

via the Borel embedding. Thus, we don’t lose much from
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
not being unique. We also remark
that for
\alpha\in\Delta_{G_{i}}

we can compute
h_{\alpha}
using the weight lattice of the almost-simple quotient
G^{i}
.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Consider
(V,T_{0},B_{0})
as in
Lemma

2.2.6
.
Consider the map
r:\textnormal{Sbt}\to\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}=[B_{0}\times B_{0}\backslash\textnormal{GL}(V)]
. For
\alpha_{i}\in\Delta_{\textnormal{GL}}
one can similarly define
Hasse invariants
\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}
on
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}}
.
Then for
\alpha\in\Delta
,

\tilde{h}_{\alpha}=\prod_{\beta\in\Delta_{\textnormal{GL}}}r^{*}\tilde{h}^{n_{\beta}}_{\beta}

for some integers
n_{\beta}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{GL}}(\lambda,\mu)
be the line bundles on
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}
given by characters of
B_{0}\times B_{0}
.
Then we can use
\chi_{\alpha_{i}}=(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
, where there are
i
ones, to construct

\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}
in
Lemma

4.1.1
. We have

r^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{GL}}(\lambda,\mu)=\mathcal{L}(\lambda,\mu)
under the map
X^{*}(T_{0})\to X^{*}(T)
.
The pullback map
r^{*}
is injective on global
sections, since
Bw_{0}B\subseteq B_{0}w_{0,\textnormal{GL}}B_{0}
by
Lemma

2.2.6
(after the
appropriate twisting). Therefore,
by the first part of
Lemma

4.1.1

r^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{GL}}(\chi,-w_{\textnormal{GL},0}\chi)=\mathcal{L}(\chi,-w_{0}\chi)
whenever the former admits a non-zero section.
Thus, we reduce to expressing
\chi_{\alpha}

as a linear combination of the restrictions of
\chi_{\alpha_{i}}
. We conclude since the
\chi_{\alpha_{i}}

span
X^{*}(T_{0})
, and the map
X^{*}(T^{\textnormal{der}}_{0})\to X^{*}(T^{\textnormal{der}})
is surjective. This is because
V
is a
minuscule representation, which have the property that their weights span
X^{*}(T^{\textnormal{der}})
.
∎

That is, we can construct the Hasse invariants out of Hasse invariants for general linear groups.
Note that we are choosing
\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}
so that its pullback

H_{\alpha_{i}}
on
\mathcal{F}l
is
\det(F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathcal{V}/D_{\mathcal{V},n-i})
, where
\alpha_{i}

are the simple roots of
\textnormal{GL}(V)
.

4.2.
Construction

Every theta operator in
\mathcal{F}l

will be constructed via pullback along
\zeta:\mathcal{F}l\to\textnormal{Sbt}
. For
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(B\times B)
let
\underline{V}

be the corresponding vector bundle on
Sbt
. For
W\in\textnormal{Rep}(B)
we will use
\underline{W}
when considering
W
as a
representation of
B\times B
through the first factor. Recall from
Section

3.3
that we have an exact functor

F_{T}:\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(T)\to\text{Coh}(\textnormal{Sbt}_{1})\to\text{Coh}(U)

fitting in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Coh}(\mathcal{F}l)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Coh}(U).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{B}}
res
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{T}}

Therefore, by
Remark

3.2.5
we get a functor

(4.2.1)

\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda,\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{U}(\mathcal{L}(-\mu),\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)).

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
, and
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
. Consider the map of

T
-modules
\phi_{\gamma,\lambda}:-\lambda-\gamma\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)

given by
-\lambda-\gamma\to x_{-\gamma}\otimes-\lambda
. We define
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma)

to be the differential operator on
U\subset\mathcal{F}l
induced by
\phi_{\gamma,\lambda}
and the functor
(
4.2.1
) above.

Thus, the
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
are
precisely all the degree
1
differential operators coming from
the functor above. We show how one can extend their corresponding maps on
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
to
Sbt
.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

Let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
,
\alpha\in\Delta
and
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
. Let
n=n_{\gamma,\alpha,\lambda}\geq 0

be the smallest integer such that the map of vector bundles on
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}

\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma)\otimes\mathcal{L}(n\chi_{\alpha},-nw_{0}\chi_{\alpha})\xrightarrow{\tilde{h}^{n}_{\alpha}}\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma)\xrightarrow{\phi_{\gamma,\lambda}}\underline{\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}_{B}(-\lambda)}

extends to
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}\cup\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}
.
Let
n_{\gamma,\alpha}
be the maximum of
n_{\gamma,\alpha,\lambda}
along all
\lambda
.
Then

(1)

For
\gamma\in\Delta
,
n_{\gamma,\alpha}
is zero unless
\alpha=\gamma
, in which case it is
1
.

(2)

Let
p\geq 3
, and assume that
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
has a simple zero.
Then
n_{\gamma,\alpha}
is the largest integer
n
such that

\gamma-n\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\cup\{0\}
.

Proof.

Recall that we fix
\{x_{\gamma}\}
to sit in a Chevalley basis.
We follow
[
34
, §4.2]
.
We can consider
\phi_{\gamma,\lambda}
as a section of
\underline{V}
over
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}
for

V:=\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}_{B}(-\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma)\in\textnormal{Rep}(B\times B)
acting through its first factor.
Equivalently,
as the left and right
B
-equivariant rational map
\psi:G\mapsto V
given
by
w_{0}\mapsto v:=[x_{-\gamma}\otimes-\lambda]\otimes(\lambda+\gamma)
.
Then
n_{\gamma,\alpha,\lambda}
can be identified
with the pole (the highest non-trivial power of
t^{-1}
) of the element

F_{\alpha}(t)=u_{\alpha}(t^{-1})\alpha^{\vee}(t^{-1})v=u_{\alpha}(t^{-1})v\in V\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}[t,t^{-1}]
,
since whenever
F_{\alpha}(0)
is defined, it gives the value of
\psi
at
s_{\alpha}w_{0}
. Note that it is in this identification where
we use the assumption that
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
has a simple zero.
We get a slightly different formula for
F_{\alpha}(t)
compared to
[
34
, Lem 4.2.1]
because of our
conventions on simple roots.
Now,
u_{\alpha}(t^{-1})=\phi_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}1&t^{-1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
acts the formal exponential
\text{exp}(t^{-1}x_{\alpha}):=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{n!}t^{-n}x^{n}_{\alpha}
.
This follows from the equivalence between
\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(\textnormal{SL}_{2})

and
U(\textnormal{SL}_{2})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
-modules, and using that

du_{\alpha}(1)=x_{\alpha}
by definition.
Threfore, we reduce to computing
the pole of

\sum_{n=1}\frac{1}{n!}t^{-n}x^{n}_{\alpha}x_{-\gamma}\otimes-\lambda\in\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)[t^{\pm 1}].

If
\alpha\neq\gamma
we have that
[x_{\alpha},x_{-\gamma}]=cx_{-\gamma+\alpha}

if
\gamma-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
and otherwise it is zero.
Here
c
is the largest positive integer such that
(c-1)\alpha+\gamma\in\Phi
,
and we can check that
c
is at most
2
by going through all the Dynkin diagrams.
On the other hand, by our choice of Chevalley basis we have that

[x_{\alpha},x_{-\alpha}]\lambda=\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\lambda
.
It is then clear that, except in the case that
p=2
and
c=2
, or
p\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle

one has
n_{\gamma,\alpha,\lambda}=n_{\gamma,\alpha}
and point
(2)
holds.
∎

Since the map

(4.2.2)

\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\sum_{\alpha}n_{\alpha,\gamma}\chi_{\alpha},\sum_{\alpha}-n_{\alpha,\gamma}w_{0}\chi_{\alpha})\xrightarrow{\prod\tilde{h}^{n_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{\alpha}}\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma)\xrightarrow{\phi_{\gamma,\lambda}}\underline{\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1}_{B}(-\lambda)}

is defined over
\textnormal{Sbt}_{1}\cup_{\alpha}\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}
, it extends to
Sbt
, by Hartogs lemma.

Definition 4.2.3
.

For
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
via pullback by
\zeta:\mathcal{F}l\to\textnormal{Sbt}
the dual of the map
(
4.2.2
)
induces a map
F_{B}\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}(-\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma+\sum h^{n_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{\alpha})
,
where
h_{\alpha}
is the weight of
H_{\alpha}
.
Let
\mu_{\gamma}=\gamma+\sum n_{\alpha,\gamma}h_{\alpha}
.
Define
\theta_{\gamma}
to be the differential operator
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\mu_{\gamma})
on
\mathcal{F}l
obtained from the previous map via
Theorem

3.2.2
.

We make each
n_{\gamma,\alpha}
explicit depending on the Lie algebra of
G
. We also give some
examples in which a Hasse invariant can have a double zero.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
G^{i}
be the almost-simple quotients of
G^{\textnormal{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
,
and let
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
be
its Lie algebra. Let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
lying in some
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
.
Assume that
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
has a simple zero and that
p\geq 3
.
We give a list of all
n_{\alpha,\gamma}
,
according to the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
. We use
2.2.4
to determine what
G^{i}
can be.

(1)

(
A_{n}
) Assume that
G^{i}\cong\textnormal{SL}_{n+1}
. Its simple roots are
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
given by
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
, where
e_{i}
are the basis vectors. The weight space of
G^{i}

is given by the hyperplane where the sum of all coordinates is
0
.
The positive roots are of the form

\gamma=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}+\ldots+\alpha_{j}
for pairs
1\leq i<j\leq n
. Then

n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}
is
1
if
k\in\{i,j\}
and zero otherwise.

(2)

(
C_{n}
) Assume that
G^{i}=\textnormal{Sp}_{2n}
.
The simple roots are
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, and
\alpha_{n}=2e_{n}
.
Then positive roots are of the form

•

\gamma=e_{i}-e_{j}=\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{j-1}
for
i<j\leq n
. In this case
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
if

k\in\{i,j-1\}
and otherwise it is
0
.

•

\gamma=2e_{i}=2(\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1})+\alpha_{n}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
In this case
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=2
if
k=i<n
, it is
1
if
k=i=n
, and otherwise it is zero.

•

\gamma=e_{i}+e_{j}=\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{j-1}+2(\alpha_{j}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1})+\alpha_{n}
for
i<j
.
In this case
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
if
k\in\{i,j\}
, and otherwise it is zero.

(3)

(B_{n})
The positive roots are

\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
\alpha_{n}=e_{n}
inside
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the coroots are

\alpha^{\vee}_{i}=e^{\vee}_{i}-e^{\vee}_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
\alpha^{\vee}_{n}=2e^{\vee}_{n}
.
If
G^{i}=\textnormal{Spin}_{2n+1}
, the weight lattice is spanned by

\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1},\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{n})\}
, and the coweight lattice by the coroots.
The positive roots are

•

\gamma=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Here
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
if

k\in\{i,n\}
and zero otherwise.

•

\gamma=(\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{n})+(\alpha_{j}+\ldots+\alpha_{n})
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
. If
j>i+1

n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
for
k\in\{i,j\}
and zero otherwise. If
j=i+1
,

n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
for
k=i+1
and zero otherwise.

•

\gamma=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}+\ldots+\alpha_{j-1}
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
. Here

n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
if
k\in\{i,j-1\}
and zero otherwise.

(4)

(
D_{n}
) We can assume
n\geq 4
. The roots are
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1

and
\alpha_{n}=e_{n-1}+e_{n}
. Suppose first that
G^{i}=\textnormal{Spin}_{2n}
.
The weight lattice is spanned by
the
e_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n-2
,
\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{2}+\ldots+e_{n-1}-e_{n})
and

\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{2}+\ldots+e_{n-1}+e_{n})

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
The cocharacter lattice is spanned by
\{\alpha^{\vee}_{i}\}
under the natural pairing.
The coroots have the same coordinates under this pairing.
If
G^{i}=\textnormal{SO}_{2n}
the weight and coweight lattice are spanned
by
\{e_{i}\}
and
\{e^{\vee}_{i}\}
instead.
For
i\leq n-2

H_{\alpha_{i}}
has a simple zero, with
\chi_{\alpha_{i}}=e_{1}+\ldots+e_{i}
. For
i=n-1,n

H_{\alpha_{i}}
has a double zero, with
\chi_{\alpha_{n-1}}=e_{1}+\ldots+e_{n-1}-e_{n}
and

\chi_{\alpha_{n}}=e_{1}+\ldots+e_{n}
.
The positive roots are of the form

•

\gamma=e_{i}-e_{j}=\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{j-1}
,
1\leq i<j\leq n
. Then
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1

if
k\in\{i,j-1\}
and zero otherwise.

•

\gamma=e_{i}+e_{j}=\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{j-1}+2(\alpha_{j}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-2})+\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}
,
1\leq i<j\leq n-2
.
Then
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
for
k=i<j-1
,
k=j
, and zero otherwise.

•

\gamma=e_{i}+e_{n-1}=\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}
,
i<n-1
. Then
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1

for
k=i<n-2
,
k=n
,
k=n-1
, and zero otherwise.

•

\gamma=e_{i}+e_{n}=\alpha_{i}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-2}+\alpha_{n}
,
i<n-1
. Then
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1

for
k\in\{i,n\}
.

•

\gamma=e_{n-1}+e_{n}=\alpha_{n}
. Then
n_{\gamma,\alpha_{k}}=1
if
k=n
and zero otherwise.

In general if
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}
does not have a simple zero the result above gives an upper bound
for the
n_{\gamma,\alpha}
. We see that this upper bound might not be attained only in case that

G^{i}
is not simply connected of
C_{n}
type.

We can also relate the construction of
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
to the more classical language involving
the Gauss–Manin connection. Recall the exact functor
F_{T}:\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(T)\to\text{Coh}(U)
.

Proposition 4.2.5
.

(1)

The map
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma)
over
U

can also be described by the following composition. Embed
i:\lambda\hookrightarrow V
as
T
-representations
for some
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
, and let
g:V\to\lambda
be a
T
-equivariant section.
This can be done by taking
V=V(w\lambda)

where
w\lambda
is a dominant Weyl translate of
\lambda
. Consider the projection

\pi_{\gamma}:\Omega^{1}_{U}\to\mathcal{L}(\gamma)
given by applying
F_{T}
to the dual of

-\gamma\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{b}
. Then

\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\xrightarrow{F_{T}(i)}\mathcal{V}\xrightarrow{\nabla}\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{U}\xrightarrow{F_{T}(g)\otimes\pi_{\gamma}}\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma),

in particular the composition is independent of the choice of
i
and
g
.

(2)

The basic theta operators
\theta_{\gamma}
satisfy the Leibniz rule. That is, for

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in X^{*}(T)
, and
f\in\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{1}),g\in\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{2})
local sections,

\theta_{\gamma}(fg)=f\theta_{\gamma}(g)+\theta_{\gamma}(f)g
.

Proof.

Consider the
T
-equivariant map
\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}(-\lambda)\to\lambda+\gamma
sending

(x_{-\gamma}\otimes v_{-\lambda})^{\vee}
to
v_{\lambda+\gamma}
, and the rest of the basis elements to
0
.
After applying
F_{T}
it defines
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
.
Applying
F_{T}
to the map

\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}(-\lambda)\xrightarrow{i^{\vee}}\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}(V^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\phi^{\vee}_{V}}V\otimes\textnormal{Ver}^{\leq 1,\vee}(1)\xrightarrow{g\otimes\pi}\lambda\otimes(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{b})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\text{id}\otimes\pi_{\gamma}}\lambda+\gamma

yields the (linearized) composition in the statement,
where
\phi_{V}
is the tensor identity on
3.1.20
.
Let
v=i(v_{\lambda})
. Using that
\text{id}\otimes\pi\circ\phi^{\vee}_{V}(x_{-\beta}\otimes v^{\vee})^{\vee}=v\otimes x^{\vee}_{-\beta}

we obtain the equality in
(1)
. For
(2)
it is enough to prove it for

\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
over
U
, and then by
(1)
it reduces to proving the statement that the Gauss–Manin connection
satisfies the Leibniz rule for tensor products, which can be seen from its construction or from examining
the tensor identity
\phi_{V}
.
∎

Remark 4.2.6
.

(1)

This also agrees with the definition given in
[
61
, §2]
in the Siegel case, or in general
in the case where the ordinary locus is non-empty. In that case after base changing to a finite étale cover

\textnormal{Ig}\to\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\text{ord}}

one can trivialize
P_{\textnormal{dR}}
, which can be used split the exact sequence

(4.2.3)

0\to\pi^{*}\Omega^{1}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\text{ord}}}\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\text{ord}}}\to 0.

Using our formalism one can check
that there is a section of (
4.2.3
)
on the fiber of the ordinary locus, where all the Hasse invariants

H_{\alpha}
are isomorphisms for
\alpha\notin\textnormal{Lie}(M)
, and there exists
a reduction
B_{M,\textnormal{dR}}\subseteq B_{\textnormal{dR}}
, for
B_{M}=M\cap B
. Both sections agree since
the trivialization of
P_{\textnormal{dR}}
induces a section of
B_{M,\textnormal{dR}}
, with which we can easily compare
them over
Ig
.
Then we can further work on
U
to split
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{F}l}
into line bundles, since each graded piece of the exact sequence (
4.2.3
)
has a simple description as an automorphic vector bundle.

(2)

Let
Sh
be a PEL Shimura variety of type
A
or
C
such that
the ordinary locus is non-empty. For simplicity assume that
Sh
is the Siegel Shimura variety.
Let
\alpha\in\Delta\backslash\textnormal{Lie}(M)
so that
\pi_{*}H_{\alpha}
is the classical Hasse invariant.
In this setting
[
17
]
define an operator
\theta

\theta:\omega(\lambda)\to\omega(\lambda+w_{0,M}h_{\alpha}+w_{0,M}\alpha)

by embedding
\omega(\lambda)
into
\mathcal{V}
for some
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
, then applying
\nabla
, whose image
lands in the last non-trivial step of its Hodge filtration
F^{1}\mathcal{V}
. Lastly, they define a projection

F^{1}\mathcal{V}\to\omega(\lambda+w_{0,M}h_{\alpha})
using the Verschiebung and its adjugate

\text{Ad}V:\omega^{(p)}\to\omega\otimes\text{det}^{p-1}\omega
.
Then
\theta=\pi_{*}\theta_{\alpha}
. To see why we need two observations.
First the projection
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\to\mathcal{L}(\alpha)
is defined over
\mathcal{F}l
,
since
-\alpha\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{b}

is
B
-equivariant; and applying
\pi_{*}
one gets the identity on
\Omega^{1}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
.
Second, one checks that in
4.2.5
(1) multiplying by
H_{\alpha}
the projection

\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda)
corresponds to
F^{1}\mathcal{V}\to\omega(\lambda+w_{0,M}h_{\alpha})

on that piece of the filtration, using the explicit description of
H_{\alpha}
as the determinant of
V:\omega\to\omega^{(p)}
.

We can also prove some other properties typical of theta operators.

Proposition 4.2.7
.

(1)

Let
\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
be an adapted element
with respect to the data of some
(V,B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0})
from
Lemma

2.2.6
. It induces a basis
\{(\phi,v_{\lambda})\}
for each of the line bundles

\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
. Then with respect to this basis
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
is given by

f\mapsto D_{\gamma}(f)
, where
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l}
, and
D_{\gamma}=e^{1}_{1}(\phi,x_{-\gamma})
.

(2)

We have that
\theta_{\gamma}(H_{\alpha})=0
for all
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
and
\alpha\in\Delta
.
Therefore,
\theta_{\gamma}
and
H_{\alpha}
commute as operators.

(3)

(Commutation relations)
[\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma_{1}},\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma_{2}}]=\tilde{\theta}_{[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]}
,
where for
m\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
,
\tilde{\theta}_{m\gamma}:=m\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
. In particular,
\theta_{0}=0
.

Proof.

Part
(1)
follows directly from the way
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
is defined, and the explicit description of the canonical isomorphisms
e_{V}

with respect to an adapted element in

3.3.12
.
For
(2)
it is enough to check that

\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}(H_{\alpha})=0
over
U
.
By
Lemma

4.1.2

H_{\alpha}
is a combination of Hasse invariants
H_{\alpha_{i}}
coming from
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}
.
The latter can be obtained from the isomorphisms
H_{i}:F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\cap D_{\mathcal{V},n-i+1}\cong\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V},n-i}\cong\mathcal{L}^{p}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma^{-1}},i+1}
, i.e.

H_{\alpha_{i}}=\text{det}(F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathcal{V}/D_{\mathcal{V},n-i})=H_{1}\ldots H_{i}
,
where
\alpha_{i}
are the simple roots of
\textnormal{GL}(V)
, and we identify

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{V},i}=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{V},i}:=F^{n-i}_{\mathcal{V}}\cap D_{\mathcal{V},n-i+1}
.
Since by construction of
\phi
the

H_{i}
are the identity (up to a
p
th power) on the basis induced by
\phi
and
B_{0}
,
we get the conclusion from part
(1)
.
Part
(3)
follows from
Theorem

3.3.13
.
∎

Part (3) also extends to
\mathcal{F}l
and
\theta_{\gamma}
by multiplying appropriately by Hasse invariants,
thanks to part (2). We see that for
p\geq 3
all basic theta operators can be obtained from commutator relations from the
simple basic operators, but this procedure introduces superfluous powers of Hasse invariants in many cases.
Finally, it is easy to describe which basic theta operators become linear after pushing them to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

Lemma 4.2.8
.

Let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}\cap\textnormal{Lie}(M)
. Then
\pi_{*}\theta_{\gamma}
is a linear map on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

Proof.

For such
\gamma
we can factor
\phi_{\gamma,\lambda}:-\lambda-\gamma\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)

through
\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(-\lambda)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)
.
There exists a functor
\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda,\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(\mu))\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}(\mathcal{L}(-\mu),\mathcal{L}(-\lambda))_{\mid U}

analogous to (
4.2.1
), by using the isomorphism
e_{P/B}
from
Theorem

3.2.2
.
Therefore, we can consider

\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
as a differential operator with respect to
\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
over
U
.
Similarly, by mimicking the procedure of
4.2.3
we see that
\theta_{\gamma}
is a differential operator with respect to
\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.
Now, the map
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to P_{\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
becomes
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
-linear after applying
\pi_{*}
.
Therefore,
\pi_{*}\theta_{\gamma}:\pi_{*}(\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to P_{\mathcal{F}l/\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda))\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}g}\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma)
is linear, where

g:P_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma)
is the linearized version of
\theta_{\gamma}
.
∎

4.3.
p
-power relations between basic theta operators

In characteristic
p
we have the special behaviour that the
p
th iteration of a derivation is again a derivation.
Hence, we might expect that for some
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
we can rewrite
\theta^{p}_{\gamma}
in terms of another basic theta operator
and Hasse invariants. We prove that this is the case for
\gamma\in\Delta
. We do this by computing the

p
-curvature of certain elements on
\mathcal{V}_{U}
for
V
from
Lemma

2.2.6
, using

3.3.9
and
3.3.11
. We will need a small lemma first.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Let
G_{i}
be an almost-simple factor of
G^{\textnormal{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. There exists an irreducible minuscule representation

V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(G)
such that
G_{i}\to\textnormal{GL}(V)
has a finite kernel. Moreover, there exist Borel subgroups

B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\subseteq\textnormal{GL}(V)
satisfying the hypothesis of
Lemma

2.2.6
together with

B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\subseteq P^{-(p)}_{V}
.

Proof.

Consider
G^{\textnormal{der}}
as a quotient of
\prod H_{i}
as in
2.2.4
. We take
V
to be one of the summands
of the Hodge embedding, so that it is non-trivial on the
H_{i}
corresponding to
G_{i}
. We can write it as

V=\otimes V_{i}
, where each
V_{i}
is a minuscule representation of
H_{i}
(which can be trivial). Without loss of
generality we may assume that they are all non-trivial.
We follow
the proof of
Lemma

2.2.6
to choose a particular
B_{0}\subseteq\textnormal{GL}(V)
satisfying

B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\subseteq P^{-(p)}_{V}
. Since
V_{i}
is minuscule
there is an ordered basis of
T
-eigenvectors
\{v_{i,1},v_{i,2},\ldots,v_{i,n_{i}}\}
of it such that its associated Borel
(the convention is that
v_{i,1}
generates the rank
1
object in the flag) in
\textnormal{GL}(V_{i})

contains
B
. Explicitly, it satisfies that
w(v_{i,1})>w(v_{i,2})>\ldots>w(v_{i,n_{i}})
.
Let
\mu
be a dominant representative
of the Hodge cocharacter. Since the composition of
\mu
with
V
must still be minuscule, we deduce that
\mu
is trivial
on each
H_{i}
except the last one
H_{k}
. Then we choose an ordered basis of
V
in a ”lexicographic order”
as follows

\displaystyle\{

\displaystyle u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}\}:=\{v_{1,1}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{k,1},v_{1,2}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{k,1},\ldots

\displaystyle v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{k,1},v_{1,2}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{k,1},\ldots,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{k,n_{k}}\}.

We let
B_{0}
to be the Borel associated to this flag, and
T_{0}
the maximal torus
defined by the basis vectors. Note that it is constructed so that
B\subseteq B_{0}\subseteq P_{V}
.
This choice defines a map of Weyl groups
W_{G}\to W_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}
. It sends

w_{0}
to
w_{\textnormal{GL}(V)}
, since
w_{0}v_{i,j}=v_{i,n_{i}-j+1}
. Thus, as in the proof of
Lemma

2.2.6

take
z^{\prime}=zw_{0}w_{0,\textnormal{GL}(V)}=z=\sigma(w_{0,M})w_{0}
.
We check that
B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\subseteq P^{-(p)}_{V}
is also satisfied. We have that
P^{-(p)}
is the parabolic associated to
-\sigma\mu
, so we have
to prove that
\langle-\sigma\mu,z^{\prime}u_{r}\rangle\geq\langle-\sigma\mu,z^{\prime}u_{r+1}\rangle
for every
r
.
Using that
z^{\prime}=\sigma(w_{0,M})w_{0}
, this is equivalent to

\langle\mu,w_{0,M}v_{N-r}\rangle\leq\langle\mu,w_{0,M}v_{N-r-1}\rangle
. This holds using three observations.
First that
\mu
on
\textnormal{GL}(V)
is minuscule, second that
\langle\mu,v_{N-r-1}\rangle\geq\langle\mu,v_{N-r}\rangle
and
third that
\langle\mu,w_{0,M}\lambda\rangle=\langle\mu,\lambda\rangle
for any
\lambda
.
∎

Theorem 4.3.2
.

Let
G_{i}
be the almost-simple factors of
G^{\textnormal{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. Fix some
i
and
let
\alpha\in\Delta_{G_{i}}
. The Frobenius
\sigma
permutes the factors
G_{i}
, let
G_{j}

be
\sigma(G_{i})
.

(1)

Let
\alpha\in\Phi_{M}
, then
\theta^{p}_{\alpha}=0
.

(2)

Assume that
\alpha\notin\textnormal{Lie}(M)
i.e.
\alpha
is
the special node for
\mu
. Further assume that the almost simple quotient
G^{i}
is simply connected
or
\textnormal{SO}_{2n}
.
Let
\delta
be the longest root
of
G_{j}
. Then for
G_{i}
of rank at least
2
we have

\theta^{p}_{\alpha}=H^{p}_{\alpha}\theta_{\delta},

and for rank
1
(
G_{i}
isogenous to
\textnormal{SL}_{2}
) we have
\theta^{p}_{\alpha}=H^{p+1}_{\alpha}\theta_{\delta}
.

Proof.

For
(1)
we use the compatibility of the relative Frobenius with
\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B}
in
Theorem

3.2.4
(1) and
the compatibility with composition of the functor

\textnormal{Hom}_{T}(\lambda,\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{B\backslash Bw_{0}B}(\mathcal{L}_{G/B}(-\mu),\mathcal{L}_{G/B}(-\lambda)),

which can be proved via a reduction of the equivalence in
Lemma

3.1.18
to
\textnormal{Rep}(T)
. Then we observe that
\tilde{\theta}^{p}_{\alpha}
is given by the map of Verma modules
associated to
x^{p}_{-\alpha}
, which is trivial as a map of baby Verma modules.
Note that
this argument also proves that
\theta^{p}_{\gamma}=0
for
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{M}
.
For
(2)
, we can prove the identity over
U
. First we check that both sides have the same weight increase.
We check it case by case using the notation of
4.2.4
and
Lemma

4.1.1
.
Let
\theta_{\gamma}
have weight increase

\gamma+h_{\gamma}
, where
h_{\gamma}
is the sum of the weights of the Hasse invariants used in
\theta_{\gamma}
.
Thus, we want to prove that
p\alpha=\delta+h_{\delta}
.

(1)

(
A_{n}
) Assume that
n\geq 2
, the case of
n=1
can be checked by hand.
Use
\alpha_{i}
for the simple roots of
G^{i}\cong\textnormal{SL}_{n}

and
\beta_{i}
for
G_{j}
. Then
\alpha=\alpha_{i}
, for some
i
, and
\delta=\beta_{1}+\ldots+\beta_{n}
,
so that
h_{\delta}=h_{\beta_{1}}+h_{\beta_{n}}
. Let
\delta_{i}
be the
longest root of
G_{i}
. In the definition of Hasse invariants

Lemma

4.1.1
we can take
\chi_{\beta_{1}}=e^{j}_{1}
and
\chi_{\beta_{n}}=-e^{j}_{n}
.
From the formula for
h_{\beta_{j}}
(
4.1.1
) we reduce to proving
\alpha_{i}=w_{0,M}\sigma^{-1}\delta
.
But
w_{0,M}\alpha_{i}=\delta_{i}
. The result follows since the Frobenius
\sigma

always preserves the simple roots, i.e. it is an outer isomorphism
by Lang’s theorem, so that
\sigma^{-1}\delta=\delta_{i}
, by the characterization
of the longest root.

(2)

(
C_{n}
) Here
G^{i}=\textnormal{Sp}_{2n}
, and there is a unique minuscule character in this case, so that
\alpha=\alpha_{n}
.
We have
\delta=2e^{j}_{1}=2(\beta_{1}+\ldots+\beta_{n-1})+\beta_{n}
, so that
h_{\delta}=2h_{\beta_{1}}
. We can take

\chi_{\beta_{1}}=e^{j}_{1}
, so we reduce to proving
w_{0,M}\alpha_{n}=\sigma^{-1}\delta
.
Since
w_{0,M}\alpha=\delta_{i}

we conclude as before.

(3)

(B_{n})
Let
n\geq 3
. Here
G^{i}=\text{Spin}_{2n+1}
, and there is a unique minuscule character,
so that
\alpha=\alpha_{1}
. We have
\delta=e^{j}_{1}+e^{j}_{2}
,

h_{\delta}=h_{\beta_{2}}
,
and we can take
\chi_{\beta_{2}}=e^{j}_{1}+e^{j}_{2}=\delta
.
From (
4.1.1
) we reduce to proving

w_{0,M}\alpha_{1}=\sigma^{-1}\delta
. In this case
M^{\text{der}}=\textnormal{Spin}_{2n-1}
.
The longest element
w_{0}\in W_{B_{n}}=W_{C_{n}}
satisfies
w_{0}=-\text{id}
, so we get

w_{0,M}\alpha_{1}=\delta_{i}
. We conclude by
\sigma^{-1}\delta=\delta_{i}
as above.

(4)

(
D_{n}
) Let
n\geq 4
. We have

\delta=e^{j}_{1}+e^{j}_{2}=\beta_{1}+2(\beta_{2}+\ldots+\beta_{n-2})+\beta_{n-1}+\beta_{n}
, and

h_{\delta}=h_{\beta_{2}}
with
\chi_{\beta_{2}}=e^{j}_{1}+e^{j}_{2}
. Note that by
4.2.4

the last equality is independent
on whether
G^{i}
is
\textnormal{Spin}_{2n}
or
\textnormal{SO}_{2n}
.
Again we reduce to proving

w_{0,M}\alpha=\sigma^{-1}\delta
. First suppose
\alpha=\alpha_{n}
.
In this case
M^{i}\cong\textnormal{SL}_{n}
so that

w_{0,M}\alpha_{n}=e^{i}_{1}+e^{i}_{2}=\delta_{i}
, so it follows from
\sigma^{-1}\delta=\delta_{i}
.
Now suppose
\alpha=\alpha_{1}
. In this case
M^{i}=\textnormal{Spin}_{2n-2}
or
\textnormal{SO}_{2n-2}
.
Its Weyl group is the group of signed permutations with an even number of sign changes, and

w_{0,M}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=(a_{1},-a_{2},-a_{3},a_{4},\ldots,a_{n})
.
Thus,
w_{0,M}\alpha_{1}=\delta_{i}
, which proves the claim. The case of
\alpha=\alpha_{n-1}
follows
from the case
\alpha=\alpha_{n}
, since there is an outer automorphism of
G^{i}
swapping the two roots.

Choose some
(V,B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0})
as in
Lemma

4.3.1
such that
V
is non-trivial on
G_{i}
.
After checking that the weights agree, by
4.2.7
(1)
we reduce to proving the identity
D^{[p]}_{\alpha}=D_{\delta}
, where we have chosen an adapted element

\phi\in T_{\textnormal{dR}}
with respect to the data of
(V,B_{0},B^{z^{\prime}}_{0})
.
We will compute part of the
p
-curvature on
\mathcal{V}
in two different ways. Let
F^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{V}}
and

D_{\bullet,\mathcal{V}}
the extended Hodge and conjugate filtration given by
B_{0}
and
B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}
. Since

B^{z^{\prime}}_{0}\subseteq P^{-(p)}
by construction of
V
, the latter refines the one-step
conjugate filtration on
\mathcal{V}
. Let
N
be the dimension of
V
, and
r
such that
D_{N-r+1,\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{V}

but
D_{N-r,\mathcal{V}}\neq\mathcal{V}
.
We have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}/D_{N-r,\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{N-r,\mathcal{V}}\otimes\Omega^{1(p)}_{\mathcal{F}l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{N-r,(p)}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}/F^{N-r}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{F}l})^{(p)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{KS}^{(p)}_{\mathcal{V}}}

by
3.3.9
, where the vertical isomorphisms are the Cartier isomorphisms.
Recall the ordered basis
\{v_{1,s_{1}}\otimes v_{2,s_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,s_{i}}\}
of
V=\otimes V_{j}
.
Let
\{v_{i,1},v_{i,2},\ldots,v_{i,t}\}
be the Hodge filtration induced on
V_{i}
by
\mu
, which is
the only
V_{j}
that
has a non-trivial Hodge filtration.
Using the diagram above we will compute
\psi_{D^{(p)}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,s_{1}}\otimes v_{2,s_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,s_{i}})

in two ways
for certain basis vectors. We will use the following easy lemma.

Lemma 4.3.3
.

Let
G
be an almost-simple semisimple group of type
A,B,C
or
D
over an
algebraically closed field. Let
T\subseteq B\subseteq G
be a choice
of maximal torus and Borel.
Let
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(G)
be an irreducible
minuscule representation of
G
, and
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
a non-trivial dominant minuscule cocharacter.
Let
\alpha\in\Delta

be the special node for
\mu
, i.e.
\langle\mu,\alpha\rangle=1
, and
\delta
the longest root.
There is a unique total order on the weights appearing in
V
:
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\ldots\lambda_{n}
,
with
\langle\mu,\lambda_{1}\rangle=1=\langle\mu,\lambda_{r}\rangle
and
\langle\mu,\lambda_{r+1}\rangle=0
.
Then there exists basis vectors
v_{\lambda_{i}}\in V
with weight
\lambda_{i}
such that

x_{-\alpha}v_{\lambda_{r}}=v_{\lambda_{r+1}}\;\;\;x_{-\delta}v_{\lambda_{1}}=v_{\lambda_{n}}.

First, we claim that under the isomorphism
\mathcal{V}/D_{N-r,\mathcal{V}}\cong F^{N-r,(p)}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}

(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,1})
is sent to
(\phi,v_{1,1}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,t})^{(p)}+\sum g_{s}(\phi,v_{1,s_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{i,s_{i}})^{(p)}
where in the sum
s_{i}<t
.
By construction of
\phi
, we have that

(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,1})\in D_{N-\sum_{j<i}n_{j}+1,\mathcal{V}}
, and under the
Cartier isomorphism

D_{N-\sum_{j<i}n_{j}+1,\mathcal{V}}/D_{N-r,\mathcal{V}}\cong F^{N-r+\sum_{j<i}n_{j}-1,(p)}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}
.
The latter is generated by the vectors appearing in the expression. We can assume that
the coefficient of
(\phi,v_{1,1}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,t})^{(p)}
is
1
by construction of
\phi
(the coefficient will always be a
p
th power).

By
3.3.11
we have that

D^{(p)}_{\alpha}\circ\textnormal{KS}_{\mathcal{V}}(\phi,v_{1,1}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,t})=(\phi,v_{1,1}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,t+1})^{(p)}
, using that by
Lemma

4.3.3

x_{-\alpha}v_{i,t}=v_{i,t+1}
. Then under the isomorphism

(\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}/F^{N-r}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}})^{(p)}\cong D_{N-r,\mathcal{V}}
we have that

(\phi,v_{1,1}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,t+1})^{(p)}
corresponds to
(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,n_{i}})
. This is because by
definition of
\phi
,
(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,n_{i}})\in D_{1,\mathcal{V}}
, which
corresponds to the rank
1
object
(F^{N-r-1}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}/F^{N-r}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}})^{(p)}
,
which is generated by
(\phi,v_{1,1}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,t+1})
by construction of
B_{0}
. Therefore, on one hand by diagram above

\psi_{D^{(p)}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,1})=(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,n_{i}}).

On the other hand, by definition
\psi_{D^{(p)}_{\alpha}}=\nabla_{D^{[p]}_{\alpha}}-\nabla^{p}_{D_{\alpha}}
. From
3.3.11
we inmediately see that

\nabla^{p}_{D_{\alpha}}=0
, since
x^{p}_{-\alpha}
acts trivially on
V\in\textnormal{Rep}(G)
.
Locally write
D^{[p]}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Phi^{+}}f_{\gamma}D_{\gamma}
for some
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{O}_{U}
.
Using
3.3.11
we have

\displaystyle\psi_{D^{(p)}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,1})=(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,n_{i}})=

\displaystyle\nabla_{D^{[p]}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,1})=\sum_{\gamma\in\Phi^{+}}D^{[p]}_{\alpha}(\phi,x_{-\gamma}[v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,1}])\otimes\omega^{(p)}_{-\gamma}.

By
Lemma

4.3.3

x_{-\delta}v_{i,1}=v_{i,n_{i}}
and for
\gamma\neq\delta

x_{-\gamma}v_{i,1}

does not involve
v_{i,n_{i}}
, so we conclude that
f_{\delta}=1
.
Now let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
different to
\delta
. Assume first that
\gamma\notin\Phi^{+}_{G_{i}}
.
Then without loss of generality there exist basis elements
v_{1,a},v_{1,b}\in V_{1}
such that
x_{-\gamma}v_{1,a}=v_{1,b}
.
Under the isomorphism
\mathcal{V}/D_{N-r,\mathcal{V}}\cong F^{N-r,(p)}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}
,

(\phi,v_{1,a}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,2})
is sent to

\sum g_{s}(\phi,v_{1,s_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{i,s_{i}})^{(p)}
where in the sum
s_{i}<t
. Then for such

(s_{j})
we have
that
D^{(p)}_{\alpha}\textnormal{KS}_{\mathcal{V}^{\sigma}}(\phi,v_{1,s_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{i,s_{i}})^{(p)}=0
.
We conclude that
\psi_{D^{(p)}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,a}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,2})=0=\nabla_{D^{[p]}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,a}\otimes v_{2,1}\ldots\otimes v_{i,2})
, which implies that

f_{\gamma}=0
. Now suppose that
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{G_{i}}
. There exist
v_{i,a},v_{i,b}
with
(a,b)\neq(1,n_{i})

satisfying
x_{-\gamma}v_{i,a}=v_{i,b}
. By a similar argument, unless
a=1
,

\psi_{D^{(p)}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,a})=0
. In that case we again obtain

f_{\gamma}=0
. If
a=1
we use

\psi_{D^{(p)}_{\alpha}}(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,1})=(\phi,v_{1,n_{1}}\otimes v_{2,n_{2}}\ldots\otimes v_{i,n_{i}})
to conclude that
f_{\gamma}=0
. Therefore,
D^{[p]}_{\alpha}=D_{\delta}

as desired.

∎

We note that the restriction on
G^{i}
in
Theorem

4.3.2
is necessary, otherwise the weights of the Hasse invariants involved
in
\theta_{\delta}
might be too large for the identity to hold. In any case, the theorem proves that if the
weight increases of
\theta^{p}_{\alpha}
and
H^{p}_{\alpha}\theta_{\delta}
agree, then they are equal.

Remark 4.3.4
.

It is not true that
F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{B}(\lambda))=D^{[p]}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
. Otherwise, we would
have the analogue
of
Theorem

3.2.4
(1)
for
\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{B}(\lambda)
, which would imply that

\theta^{p}_{\alpha}=0
in case
(2)
above. Another way to see this is to use Serre-Tate coordinates.
Take the simplest case of the modular curve. Then on the formal completion of an ordinary point
x
, theorem of
Katz
[
39
]
about Serre-Tate coordinates tells us that
(\phi,x_{-})\in\Omega^{1}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\wedge}_{x}}
for an adapted element
is identified with
q\frac{d}{dq}
.
Then by
3.3.12
,
F_{B}(\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{B}(1))
is
the quotient sheaf of
D_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\wedge}_{x}}=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[[q-1]]dq
generated by
(q\frac{d}{dq})^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq p-1
.
On the other hand,

D^{[p]}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\wedge}_{x}}
is generated by
(\frac{d}{dq})^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq p-1
. One can explicitly
see that they are not isomorphic as quotients of
D_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\wedge}_{x}}
.

Example 4.3.5
.

Let us illustrate the proposition in the case of unitary Shimura varieties.
For simplicity take the signature
(2,1)
,
E
to be a quadratic imaginary field with embeddings
\{\sigma,\overline{\sigma}\}

as in
[
65
, §2.3]
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\subset\omega_{\sigma}
be part of the Hodge filtration over
\mathcal{F}l
.
We have
\alpha=\alpha_{2}
.
Suppose that
p
is inert in
E
. Then
G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}=\textnormal{GL}_{3}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}

with Frobenius acting via the non-trivial outer automorphism. We can choose
V
so that
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}
, and its
\sigma
-twist gives
\mathcal{H}_{\overline{\sigma}}
.
The
\sigma
-isotypic parts of
the extended Hodge and conjugate filtrations on
\mathcal{F}l
are

0\subset\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\subset\omega_{\sigma}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\omega^{-1}_{\overline{\sigma}}

0\subset(\omega_{\sigma}/\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})^{-p}\subset\textnormal{Ker}(V_{\sigma})\cong\omega^{\vee(p)}_{\sigma}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\omega^{p}_{\overline{\sigma}},

where the last map is the Verschiebung. Then an adapted element
\phi
is defined by a section

(\phi,f_{1})
of
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}

which maps under
\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\omega^{p}_{\overline{\sigma}}
to a horizontal
section for
\nabla
, an element
(\phi,f_{2})
of
\omega_{\sigma}\cap\textnormal{Ker}(V_{\sigma})
that under

\omega_{\sigma}\cap\textnormal{Ker}(V_{\sigma})\to\mathcal{L}^{-p}_{\sigma}
maps to a horizontal section,
and
(\phi,f_{3})
a horizontal section of
(\omega_{\sigma}/\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})^{-p}
.
In the
p
split case
V
and its
\sigma
-twist agree, and
we can just work with the
\sigma
-isotypic component.
In both cases we have
\theta^{p}_{\alpha_{2}}=H^{p}_{\alpha_{2}}\theta_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}
. This does not depend on
the splitting of
p
on
E
, even though the structure of the conjugate filtration on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
changes.

4.4.
Restriction to codimension
1
strata.

We study the restriction to codimension
1
strata of basic theta operators, culminating in a proof
the generic
injectivity of basic theta operators in the
p
-restricted region in
Theorem

4.4.7
.
Let
\alpha\in\Delta
and
i:\overline{D}_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}l
and

\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
. In this subsection assume that
H_{\alpha}
has a simple zero.
Then
\theta_{\gamma}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\mu_{\gamma})
fits in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\lambda-h_{\alpha})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\lambda-h_{\alpha}+\mu_{\gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\mu_{\gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\mu_{\gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\alpha}}

where
\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}
is defined by the diagram. When taking global sections of the diagram,
this means that for
f\in\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{F}l,\mathcal{L}(\lambda))
,
\theta_{\gamma}(f)_{\mid\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}(f_{\mid\overline{D}_{\alpha}})
. We use the notation

\Theta_{\gamma}:P^{1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\mu_{\gamma})
for the linearized map
corresponding to
\theta_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
,
\gamma,\beta\in\Phi^{+}
such that

i^{*}\Theta_{\gamma}=B\circ i^{*}\Theta_{\beta}
where
B
is a linear map over
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
.
Then
\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=i_{*}B\circ\theta_{\beta,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}
.

Proof.

We give another characterization of
\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}:i_{*}i^{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to i_{*}i^{*}\mathcal{L}(\mu)
.
Take a local section
\overline{v}\in i^{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
, and lift it to a section
v
over
\mathcal{F}l
. Then
by definition
\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}(\overline{v})
is the reduction of
\theta_{\gamma}(v)=\Theta_{\gamma}\circ g(v)
, where
g:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to P^{1}_{\mathcal{F}l}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
. This makes it clear that

\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=i_{*}B\circ\theta_{\beta,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}
.
∎

We can describe the behaviour of simple basic theta operators
\theta_{\alpha}
when restricted to their corresponding
closed stratum
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
.

Theorem 4.4.2
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
,
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
. Assume that
H_{\alpha}
has a simple zero. Then

(1)

When applied to
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
we have

\theta_{\alpha,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle B_{\alpha}
,
where
B_{\alpha}
is a section over
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
that does not vanish on
D_{\alpha}
.

(2)

Let
f\in\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
be a local section. Then
H_{\alpha}\mid\theta_{\alpha}(f)

if and only if
H_{\alpha}\mid f
or
p\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
.

(3)

Assume that
2.8.2
(2) holds.
Then the map

\theta_{\alpha}
is injective on
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda))

for
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
such that
\langle\lambda,\sigma^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}\rangle<p-\epsilon
where

\epsilon\geq 0
depends only on
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
p\nmid\langle w_{0,M}\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
.
In particular,
it is injective
for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
as in
2.2.1
.

Proof.

For
(1)
we can prove the statement at the level of linearized differential operators, by
Lemma

4.4.1
.
This further reduces to a statement about vector bundles on
\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
. Namely, we have to prove that
over
\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
there is a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\chi_{\alpha},-w_{0}\chi_{\alpha})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces cB_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha,\lambda}}

where
c=\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
and
B_{\alpha}
is non-zero.
We use the same method as in
4.2.2
.
We see both compositions as maps

\overline{Bs_{\alpha}w_{0}B}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\chi_{\alpha},-w_{0}\chi_{\alpha})

which are equivariant for the left and right
B
action on
\overline{Bs_{\alpha}w_{0}B}
.
The first one extends to
G
and it is given by

w_{0}\mapsto v=(x_{-\alpha}\otimes-\lambda)\otimes\lambda\otimes(\chi_{\alpha},-w_{0}\chi_{\alpha})
,
and the second one by

s_{\alpha}w_{0}\mapsto(c\otimes-\lambda)\otimes\lambda\otimes(\chi_{\alpha},-w_{0}\chi_{\alpha})
.
Note that a section with the same weight as
B_{\alpha}

is unique up to a constant, since it is determined by its restriction to the open dense
Bs_{\alpha}w_{0}B\subseteq\overline{Bs_{\alpha}w_{0}B}
,
and then by the
B
bi-equivariance it is determined by its value at
s_{\alpha}w_{0}\in Bs_{\alpha}w_{0}B
.
We compute that the value at
s_{\alpha}w_{0}

of both maps agrees.
This would prove that such a factorization exists over
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}
, and since the top arrow extends to

\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
, for any such choice of
B_{\alpha}
it
would automatically extend to
\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
.
Moreover, by construction
B_{\alpha}
is non-vanishing on

\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}
.
As in the proof of
4.2.2
, the value of the top map at
s_{\alpha}w_{0}
is given by the value at
0
of
F_{\alpha}(t)=\rho_{1}(u_{\alpha}(t^{-1})\alpha^{\vee}(t^{-1}))\rho_{2}(u_{-w_{0}\alpha}(-t^{-1}))v
, where
\rho_{i}

are the actions through the respective factors of
B\times B
. Since
\rho_{2}
is inflated from
T

we get

F_{\alpha}(t)=t^{\langle\chi_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}(v+t^{-1}x_{\alpha}v)
, so that

F_{\alpha}(0)=(\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\otimes-\lambda\otimes\lambda\otimes(\chi_{\alpha},-w_{0}\chi_{\alpha})
,
since
h_{\alpha}=[x_{\alpha},x_{-\alpha}]
acts on a vector of weight
\lambda
as the scalar

\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
.

For
(2)
, the if part follows from
4.2.7
and the observation that for

p\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
,
the map
\phi_{\alpha,\lambda}
is already
B
-equivariant. If
H_{\alpha}\mid\theta_{\alpha}(f)
and
H_{\alpha}\nmid f
,
then since
H_{\alpha}
has a simple zero
f_{\overline{D}_{\alpha}}\neq 0
, so that by part
(1)

\theta_{\alpha}(f)_{\mid\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle B_{\alpha}f_{\overline{D}_{\alpha}}\neq 0
, which implies

p\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
.
To prove
(3)
, we claim that any non-zero
f\in\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{F}l,\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda))

with generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
cannot be divisible by
H_{\alpha}
.
Let
\beta=\sigma^{-1}\alpha\in\Delta
.
By the compatibility of the action of Frobenius on the root system

\langle w_{0,M}h_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle=p\langle\chi_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle-\langle\sigma w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=p-\langle\sigma w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
, while for
\gamma\in\Delta\setminus\{\beta\}

\langle w_{0,M}h_{\alpha},\gamma^{\vee}\rangle=-\langle w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha},\gamma^{\vee}\rangle
. Therefore,

\langle w_{0,M}h_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle-p
and
\langle w_{0,M}h_{\alpha},\gamma^{\vee}\rangle

only depends on
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
as a reductive group.
Suppose that
2.8.2
(2) holds for a
constant
m\geq 0
.
Then we obtain the claim for

\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
satisfying

\langle\lambda+w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle<p-m
if we assume that

\langle w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha},\gamma^{\vee}\rangle>-m
for all
\gamma\in\Delta\backslash\{\alpha\}
,
which
we can do by modifying
m
.
Moreover, by part
(2)
if
\lambda

also
satisfies

p\nmid\langle w_{0,M}\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
then
the map
\theta_{\alpha}
is injective on global sections. All these conditions on
\lambda
only depend
on
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, and given
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
there are finitely many options for what
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
can be.
Thus,
\theta_{\alpha}

is injective for
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
generic.
∎

Remark 4.4.3
.

(1)

Another way to see that
\theta_{\alpha,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}
is linear is that since it is a degree
1

differential operator satisfying the Leibniz rule,
it is enough to check that when applied to
\lambda=0
the restriction of
\theta_{\alpha}
to
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
is
0
.
But this is clear since in that case
\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha}
already extends to
\mathcal{F}l
,
as the map
-\alpha\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{b}
is
B
-equivariant.

(2)

It is not necessarily the case that
B_{\alpha}
is a partial Hasse invariant for
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
, i.e.
it must not vanish on
D_{\alpha}
. For instance, for
G=\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
, and
\alpha=(1,-1)
,
B_{\alpha}
comes from a map
(\omega/\mathcal{L})\to(\omega\mathcal{L})^{p}

whose vanishing is given by the condition that
\omega
is contained in the rank
3
piece of the conjugate filtration. This is one of the two lower dimensional closed stratum
contained in
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
.

We can go further in some cases where the restriction to a stratum of a basic
theta operator is still a differential operator.
Even though we don’t attempt to extensively understand differential operators on general
codimension
1
strata (it is not
even clear how to understand the tangent bundle of
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
in general),
we can at least understand the ones that come by restriction from
\mathcal{F}l
.

Proposition 4.4.4
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
,
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
such that
\gamma+\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
.
Let
[x_{\alpha},x_{-\gamma-\alpha}]=c_{\alpha,\gamma}x_{-\gamma}
, and

c:=c_{\alpha,\gamma}\frac{(n_{\alpha,\gamma+\alpha}-1)!}{n_{\alpha,\gamma+\alpha}!}
,
where
n_{\alpha,\gamma+\alpha}
is as in
4.2.3
.
Assume that
H_{\alpha}
has a simple zero.
Then
on
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)

H\theta_{\gamma+\alpha,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=cB_{\alpha}\circ\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}},

where
B_{\alpha}
is a section of an automorphic line bundle over
\overline{D}_{\alpha}

defined in
Theorem

4.4.2
, and
H
is the product of the
Hasse invariants that are used in the definition of
\theta_{\gamma}
, but not on
\theta_{\gamma+\alpha}
.

Proof.

We use
Lemma

4.4.1
to
reduce to the statement at the level of linearized operators, which reduces to the factorization of
the following diagram of vector bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma-\alpha)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\chi,-w_{0}\chi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma-\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)}}
