{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\chi_{1},-w_{0}\chi_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces cB_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HH_{\gamma+\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\gamma+\alpha,\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\gamma,\lambda}}

on
\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
. We use
H_{\gamma}
to denote
the product of the Hasse invariants used in
\theta_{\gamma}
,
\chi
for the sum of
\chi_{\alpha_{i}}

used in
HH_{\gamma+\alpha}
, and similarly
\chi_{1}
for
H_{\gamma}
.
We only need to prove that the
diagram commutes on
\textnormal{Sbt}_{\alpha}
, since every arrow extends to
\overline{\textnormal{Sbt}}_{\alpha}
.
For that consider both as maps

\overline{Bs_{\alpha}w_{0}B}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{L}(\lambda+\gamma+\alpha)\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\chi,w_{0}\chi)
.
The one on top
extends to
G
, and it is given by

w_{0}\mapsto v_{1}=(x_{-\gamma-\alpha}\otimes-\lambda)\otimes(\lambda+\gamma+\alpha)\otimes(-\chi,w_{0}\chi)
.
As in
4.2.2
the value at
s_{\alpha}w_{0}
of the top map is given by the value at
0
of

F_{\alpha}(t)=t^{\langle\chi,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}(v_{1}+t^{-1}x_{\alpha}v_{1}+\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}x^{2}_{\alpha}v_{1}+\ldots)
.
For the bottom one, by the construction of
B_{\alpha}
, its value at
s_{\alpha}w_{0}
is given by
the value at
0
of

t^{\langle\chi_{1},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}(u_{1}+t^{-1}x_{\alpha}u_{1}+\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}x^{2}_{\alpha}u_{1}+\ldots)
,
where
u_{1}=c(x_{-\gamma}\otimes-\lambda)\otimes(\lambda+\gamma+\alpha)\otimes(-\chi,w_{0}\chi)
.
By the assumption that
H_{\alpha}

has a simple zero we have that

n_{\alpha,\gamma+\alpha}=\langle\chi,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\chi_{1},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle+1
,
since
\theta_{\gamma+\alpha}
uses one more copy of
H_{\alpha}
than
\theta_{\gamma}
by
4.2.2
,
and
H
does not contain
H_{\alpha}
.
Using that
x_{\alpha}v_{1}=c_{\alpha,\gamma}u_{1}
, and multiplying by

cB_{\alpha}
gives the desired equality.

∎

Remark 4.4.5
.

By
4.2.7
(2)
we have
H\theta_{\gamma,\alpha}=c_{-\alpha,-\gamma}[\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\gamma}]
. By
computing
\langle h_{\alpha}+\alpha,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
, together
with
4.4.4
and
Theorem

4.4.2
it shows that

\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}
commutes with
B_{\alpha}
.
Conversely, one could attempt to prove
4.4.4
by proving that

\theta_{\gamma,\overline{D}_{\alpha}}
and
B_{\alpha}
commute.

Remark 4.4.6
.

All the results of this section so far can be extended to
\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}}
, since one can extend the Zip map
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}\to\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt Zip}}}\nolimits

to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}}

[
23
, Thm I.2.5]
, so that we obtain a Zip map

\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}}\to\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt ZipFlag}}}\nolimits
. To construct the basic theta operators on
\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}}
we use the extended Zip map
and that

Theorem

3.2.4
extends to toroidal compactifications.
The statements of
4.2.7
and
Theorem

4.3.2

can be proved on an open dense subset of
\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}}
,
so they automatically extend to
\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}}
.
The rest of the results (e.g.
Theorem

4.4.2
,
4.4.4
or

4.2.5
) are proved by working on

\mathop{\text{$G$-{\tt ZipFlag}}}\nolimits\to\textnormal{Sbt}
and then pulling back to
\mathcal{F}l
, so they
also extend to the toroidal compactification.

Combining the previous results we get a general result about injectivity of basic theta operators
in the
p
-restricted region.

Theorem 4.4.7
.

Let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
, write it as
\gamma=\sum n_{\alpha}\alpha
in terms of simple roots, with

n_{\alpha}\geq 1
. Suppose that every
\alpha
appearing in the sum satisfies that
H_{\alpha}
has a simple zero.
Further assume that
2.8.2
(2) holds.
Then

\theta_{\gamma}
is injective on
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}},\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda))=\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda))
for
generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
.

Proof.

We use induction on the number of simple roots appearing in
\gamma
. If
\gamma\in\Delta

this is
Theorem

4.4.2
(3). Otherwise, let
\alpha\in\Delta
appearing in
\gamma

such that
\gamma-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. Suppose that

f\in\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}},\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda))
is non-zero and it is in the kernel of
\theta_{\gamma}
.
By the reasoning
as in the proof of
Theorem

4.4.2
for a generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
,
f
cannot be divisible by
any Hasse invariant. Moreover, by
4.4.4

\theta_{\gamma}(f)_{\mid\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=cB_{\alpha}\theta_{\gamma-\alpha}(f)_{\mid\overline{D}_{\alpha}}=0
,
which implies that
H_{\alpha}\mid\theta_{\gamma-\alpha}(f)
. Here we use that
from case by case inspection
\lvert c\rvert\leq 2
,
and that we can assume that
p\geq 3
since the statement concerns generic
\lambda
.
We repeat the process of restricting to
\overline{D}_{\alpha}
until
\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
does not contain
\alpha
,
and we obtain

H_{\alpha}\mid\theta_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}(f)
.
We claim that
2.8.2
(2) applies to the weight of
\frac{1}{H_{\alpha}}\theta_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}(f)

for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
, which implies that
\theta_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}(f)=0
.
Let
\mu=w_{0,M}(h_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}-h_{\alpha})
be the weight increase of
\frac{1}{H_{\alpha}}\theta_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}

on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
,
where
h_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}
is the sum of the weights of
the Hasse invariants appearing in
\theta_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}
.
Then as in the proof of
Theorem

4.4.2
(3)

\langle\mu,\sigma^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=-p+\langle\sigma w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle+\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\neq\alpha}p\langle\chi_{\alpha^{\prime}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle-\langle\sigma w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha^{\prime}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle,

where the sum runs along the simple roots of

\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha
whose Hasse invariant appears in
h_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}
, possibly with multiplicity.
Since
\langle\chi_{\alpha^{\prime}},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=0

we see that

\langle\mu,\sigma^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}\rangle+p
only depends on
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
as an reductive group.
Similarly, if
\beta^{\vee}\neq\sigma^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}

is a simple coroot

\langle\mu,\beta^{\vee}\rangle=\langle w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha},\beta^{\vee}\rangle+\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\neq\alpha}p\langle\chi_{\alpha^{\prime}},\sigma\beta^{\vee}\rangle-\langle w_{0,M}\chi_{\alpha^{\prime}},\beta^{\vee}\rangle.

We can check that
\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\neq\alpha}p\langle\chi_{\alpha^{\prime}},\sigma\beta^{\vee}\rangle\leq 2p
, since

\sigma\beta\in\Delta
and

H_{\sigma\beta}
can only appear at most twice in the weight increase of a basic theta operator, by
4.2.4
.
The rest of the expression only depends on
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
.
Given
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
, there are only finitely
many possibilities for what
G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
can be, so that for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
,
\lambda+\mu
satisfies
the hypothesis of
2.8.2
(2). Namely, there exists
m\geq 0
only depending on
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that

\langle\lambda+\mu,\sigma^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}\rangle<-m
and
\langle\lambda+\mu,\beta^{\vee}\rangle<3p+m
for
all other simple roots
\beta
.
Therefore
\theta_{\gamma-n_{\alpha}\alpha}(f)=0
,
and we conclude by the
induction hypothesis.
∎

In fact, the injectivity result does apply to a larger set of weights than
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
. For
if
f\in\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda))
is not divisible by any
Hasse invariant, the result only depends on
\langle\lambda,\sigma^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
being small
compared to
p
for all but one of the simple roots
\alpha
appearing in
\gamma
, and on the sharpness of the
results of
2.8.2
(2). It can also happen that for some of the simple roots one does not
need to use
2.8.2
(2), and one just uses that all the weights have to be
M
-dominant.
Further, even if
f
is divisible by a Hasse invariant
H_{\alpha}
, it might be the case that
f/H_{\alpha}

has a weight which is generic enough for the theorem to hold.
However, the genericity conditions on
\lambda
will keep getting worse
as one gets further away from the
p
-restricted region, and at around the point where

\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
is equal to
p^{2}
for all
\alpha\in\Delta

the theta operator will cease to be injective, since one could find sections
in the kernel by multiplying several Hasse invariants.
One could compute this more precisely in any particular example
appearing in
Theorem

2.8.3
.

5.
Theta linkage maps

5.1.
Construction on
\mathcal{F}l
.

We construct non-zero maps between
B
-Verma modules in characteristic
p

whose weights are linked, hence giving the name to their associated theta linkage maps.
We use the following notation.

Definition 5.1.1

(Linkage relation)
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)
.
For
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}

let
s_{\gamma,n}\cdot\lambda:=\lambda+(pn-\langle\lambda+\rho,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle)\gamma
.
We say that
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu
if there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\mu=s_{\gamma,n}\cdot\lambda
,

\lambda\leq\mu
, and
n
is the smallest integer
m
such that
\lambda\leq s_{\gamma,m}\cdot\lambda
.
Then
\lambda\uparrow\mu
if there exists a chain

\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma_{1}}\lambda_{1}\ldots\uparrow_{\gamma_{n}}\mu
.

Geometrically
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu

if and only if
\mu
is the reflection of
\lambda
in the positive root direction across the closest wall
defined by
\gamma^{\vee}
.
We start by recalling the classical result for maps of Verma modules in characteristic
0
.

Proposition 5.1.2

(
[
72
]
,
[
3
]
)
.

Let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in X^{*}(T)
, then

\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\mathbb{C}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{1}),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda_{2}))

is non-zero precisely when there is a sequence
\lambda_{1}\leq\mu_{1}\ldots\leq\mu_{n}=\lambda_{2}

such that
\mu_{i+1}=s_{\gamma_{i}}\cdot\mu_{i}
for
\gamma_{i}\in\Phi^{+}
,
and in that case the Hom space is
1
-dimensional.

In particular
there is no non-zero map between Verma modules of different weights, both of which are
G
-dominant,
since
W
acts transitively on the Weyl chambers. However, in characteristic
p

we get many more maps.

Proposition 5.1.3
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)
.

(1)

The Hom space of Verma modules in characteristic
p
is
invariant under
p
-translation, that is, for any
\nu\in X^{*}(T)

\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))=\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda+p\nu),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu+p\nu)).

(2)

Assume that there exists
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
such that

\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma,n}\mu
, and that there exists some
\nu\in X^{*}(T)

such that
\langle\nu,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle\mid n
. In particular, such a
\nu
always exists
if
G^{\textnormal{der}}
is simply connected.
Then we can construct an explicit
1
-dimensional subspace of

\langle\phi(\lambda\uparrow\mu)\rangle\subseteq\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))\neq 0.

In general if
G^{\text{der}}
is simply connected and
\lambda\uparrow\mu
, then

\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))\neq 0
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, let
f\in\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))
be given by

x_{f}\in U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}
. We claim that
x_{f}
also defines a map in

\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda+p\nu),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu+p\nu))
, which proves the claim by reversing the process.
We have to prove that
g:\lambda+p\nu\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu+p\nu)
is
B
-equivariant,
which is the same as being equivariant for the algebra of distributions
U(B)
.
We can reduce it to being equivariant for elements of the form

\frac{y^{n}}{n!}
for positive roots
y
, and
H\choose n
for
H\in\mathfrak{h}
. For the latter
it follows since the weight increase of both maps is the same. For the former, we have to prove that

\frac{y^{n}}{n!}x_{f}\otimes(\mu+p\nu)=0
. Write
\frac{y^{n}}{n!}x_{f}=x_{g}x_{h}
for

x_{g}\in U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}
and
x_{h}\in U(B)
by the following procedure.
By induction on the weight of
y^{n}
and the degree of
x_{f}
(with respect to a choice of PBW basis) it suffices to prove that
\frac{y^{n}}{n!}x\in x\frac{y^{n}}{n!}+U(B)U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}

for
x\in\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}
, where the terms in
U(B)
have smaller weight than
y^{n}
.
This follows from the PBW theorem on
U(G)
, and by
observing that in the expression for
[y^{n},x]
in a PBW basis
the degree of any element of
\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}
appearing can be at most
1
. Since
f
is equivariant
we have that
x_{g}\otimes x_{h}\mu=0
. Then

\frac{y^{n}}{n!}x_{f}\otimes(\mu+p\nu)=x_{g}\otimes x_{h}(\mu+p\nu)=0
, since

x_{h}p\nu=0
.

For
(2)

choose
\nu\in X^{*}(T)
such that
n=\langle\nu,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle m

for some
m\geq 1
. Consider
\tilde{\lambda}\coloneqq\lambda-pm\nu
, then there exists a unique
up to scalar non-zero map
\phi:\tilde{\lambda}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(s_{\gamma,0}\cdot\tilde{\lambda})
over

\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

by
5.1.2
,
given by an element
\tilde{x}_{f}\in U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
After rescaling
\tilde{x}_{f}
in some PBW basis, choose
x_{f}
over the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}

such that
x_{f}
is
not
0
mod
p
, this is unique up to a unit. Then
x_{f}
defines a map
\tilde{\lambda}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(s_{\gamma,0}\cdot\tilde{\lambda})

of
B
-modules over
\mathcal{O}
since the Verma modules are
p
-torsion free.
Since
\tilde{\lambda}-\lambda=s_{\gamma,0}\cdot\tilde{\lambda}-s_{\gamma,n}\cdot\lambda=-pm\nu
, part
(1)
ensures that the map

\lambda\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
defined by
\lambda\mapsto x_{f}\otimes\mu
is
B
-equivariant.
We claim that the one-dimensional subspace generated by this map is independent
of the choice of
\nu
. This amounts to the fact that in characteristic
0
the maps

\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(s_{\gamma}\cdot\lambda)
are given by an element of
U\mathfrak{b}^{-}

for all
\lambda
lying in a hyperplane
\langle\lambda,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle=r

[
33
, 4.12]
.
For two valid
\nu_{1,2}

the maps in characteristic zero

\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda-pn\nu_{i})\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(s_{\gamma}\cdot(\lambda-pn\nu_{i}))
are then given by
the same element
h=h_{1}\cdot h_{2}\in U\mathfrak{b}^{-}=U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B}\cdot U\mathfrak{h}
.
After rescaling to get a non-zero map over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, we have that
h_{2}
acts by the same scalar on both

s_{\gamma}(\lambda-pn\nu_{i})
,
since the difference of their weights is a multiple of
p
.
Thus, we end up with the same element in
U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. Finally, the last statement in
(2)
follows from the first
since
U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{B,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
has no non-trivial zero-divisors,
so we can compose the previously defined maps.
∎

Remark 5.1.4
.

(1)

If there is a non-zero map
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
then

\mu\in W_{\text{aff}}\cdot\lambda
by considering action of the Harish-Chandra center of
U\mathfrak{g}
.
Moreover, if the projection to
\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{B}(\lambda)\to\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{B}(\mu)
is non-zero and

\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
, then
\lambda\uparrow\mu
by
the linkage principle on
G_{1}B
-representations
[
36
, Cor 9.12]
,
since the simple
L(\lambda)
is the head of
\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{B}(\lambda)
.

(2)

5.1.3

doesn’t prove that for two linked weights
\lambda\uparrow\mu
the map constructed
above is independent of a choice of sequence

\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma_{1}}\lambda_{1}\uparrow_{\gamma_{2}}\ldots\uparrow_{\gamma_{n}}\mu
.
In general, we don’t know if the Hom space is at most
one-dimensional as in the characteristic
0
case. A key difference in characteristic
p
is that
Verma modules are not of finite length as
(U\mathfrak{g},B)
-modules. E.g. for
G=\textnormal{GL}_{2}

for each
n\geq 0
the submodule generated by
\{x^{i}_{-}\otimes\lambda:i\geq np\}\subseteq\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda)

is a
(U\mathfrak{g},B)
-module, forming
an infinite descending chain of submodules.

We define theta linkage map by plugging the maps of
5.1.3
into
Theorem

3.2.4
.

Definition 5.1.5
.

(Theta linkage maps)
Let
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu
as in part
(2)
of
5.1.3
. Recall the functor

\Phi_{B}:\textnormal{Hom}_{(U\mathfrak{g},B)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda),\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu))\to\textnormal{DiffOp}_{\mathcal{F}l_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\mathcal{L}(-\mu),\mathcal{L}(-\lambda))
from
Theorem

3.2.4
.
We define

\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}:=\Phi_{B}(\phi(\lambda\uparrow\mu)):\mathcal{L}(-\mu)\to\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)

for some non-zero choice of
\phi(\lambda\uparrow\mu)
in
5.1.3
(2).
We will refer to these as theta linkage maps. They extend to a toroidal compactification
\mathcal{F}l^{\textnormal{tor}}
.

Corollary 5.1.6
.

After post-composing with some powers of Hasse invariants, the theta linkage maps
\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
are combinations of the basic theta operators.
Namely, given
\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}

there exists some
f\in U\mathfrak{u}_{B}
which we see as a non-commutative polynomial on the
\{x_{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Phi^{+}\}
, and some
integers
n_{\alpha}\geq 0
such that

\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta}H^{n_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}\circ\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}=f([\theta_{\gamma}]_{\gamma\in\Phi^{+}}).

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

3.3.13
, which identifies
\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
over
U
with a combination
of basic theta operators
\tilde{\theta}_{\gamma}
. Since we know that this automatically extends to
\mathcal{F}l

it must be the case that the same combination of
\theta_{\gamma}
becomes divisible by all the Hasse invariants
used in the combination.
∎

Notation 5.1.7

(Reparametrization of theta linkage maps according to Serre weights)
.

As alluded to in the introduction the BGG decomposition over
\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
implies that for

\lambda
a dominant weight

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}_{\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{p}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{d}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{p}})\neq 0
,
where
\eta
is the sum of roots appearing in
\textnormal{Lie}(U_{P})
.
The same will hold with mod
p
coefficients provided that we can lift coherent cohomology to characteristic
0
.
This motivates the following reparametrization of the theta linkage maps.
Given two weights
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma,n}\mu
for some
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}\backslash\Phi^{+}_{M}
we have
that
-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta\uparrow_{w_{0,M}\gamma,-n}-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta
.
This comes down to the fact
that
\rho+w_{0,M}\rho=\eta
and
w_{0,M}\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
. This follow from the identities

\rho=\frac{1}{2}\eta+\rho_{M}
,
w_{0,M}\rho_{M}=-\rho_{M}
and that
w_{0,M}
preserves
the set
\Phi^{+}\backslash\Phi^{+}_{M}
.
Then we define
\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
to be the pushforward along
\pi
of the operator induced by

\phi(-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta\uparrow-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta)

\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}:=\pi_{*}\Phi_{B}[\phi(-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta\uparrow-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta)]:\omega(\lambda+\eta)\to\omega(\mu+\eta).

If
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{M}
then
w_{0,M}\gamma
is a negative simple root and

-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta\uparrow_{-w_{0,M}\gamma,n}-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta
. We define
\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}

to be the pushforward along
\pi
of the operator induced by

\phi(-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta\uparrow-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta)

\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}:=\pi_{*}\Phi_{B}[\phi(-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta\uparrow-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta)]:\omega(\mu+\eta)\to\omega(\lambda+\eta),

so we see that in this case the theta linkage map goes in the opposite direction.

Remark 5.1.8
.

(
p
-translation property of theta linkage maps)
Let
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in X^{*}(T)
such that
\lambda\uparrow\mu
. Let
F
be the absolute Frobenius on
\mathcal{F}l
,
then by the projection formula
F_{*}\mathcal{L}(-\lambda-p\nu)=F_{*}\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\nu)
,
we have

F_{*}\theta^{\lambda+p\nu\uparrow\mu+p\nu}:F_{*}\mathcal{L}(-\mu)\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\nu)\xrightarrow{F_{*}\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}\otimes\text{id}}F_{*}\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\nu)=F_{*}\mathcal{L}(-\mu-p\nu).

This is because by construction and
5.1.6

both
\theta^{\lambda+p\nu\uparrow\mu+p\nu}
and
\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
are constructed using the same
combination of
basic theta operators. Thus, to prove the above property we can replace the linkage map by a basic theta operator,
in which case it follows from the Leibniz rule. Therefore, in order to compute kernels of linkage maps as maps of
sheaves we can assume that the domain is in
X_{1}(T)
.

Example 5.1.9
.

(Simple theta linkage maps)
In the case that
\lambda\uparrow_{\alpha,n}\mu
with
\alpha\in\Delta
, the associated map of
Verma modules is easy to describe. Write
N_{\alpha}=pn-\langle\lambda+\rho,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
for some integer
n

with
the property that
1\leq N_{\alpha}\leq p
.
Then the map
-\mu\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(-\lambda)
is given by
-\mu\mapsto x^{N_{\alpha}}_{-\alpha}\otimes-\lambda
.
One can explicitly check that this is
B
-equivariant using the commutation relation

x_{\alpha}x^{N}_{-\alpha}=x^{N}_{-\alpha}x_{\alpha}+Nx^{N-1}_{-\alpha}h_{\alpha}-N(N-1)x^{N-1}_{-\alpha}

and that
\langle\rho,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=1
. Therefore,
by
Theorem

3.3.13

\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}=\frac{1}{H^{N_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}}\theta^{N_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}
.
We will call these
simple theta linkage maps
, they are the ones that can be understood explicitly
in terms of the basic theta operators. By composing two such simple affine Weyl reflections
we also get that
\lambda\uparrow_{\alpha}\lambda+p\alpha
, so that
H^{p}_{\alpha}\mid\theta^{p}_{\alpha}
.
Then
Theorem

4.3.2
says that this is
0
in some cases, or another basic theta operator in others.

We also note, that even though that
x^{p}_{-\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
is always a central element in
U\mathfrak{g}
, it does
not define a map of Verma modules unless
\gamma
is simple. This is because the resulting map won’t be
B
-equivariant, one can see that this already
fails by considering equivariance for
\frac{x^{p}_{\alpha}}{p!}
for
\alpha\in\Delta
appearing in
\gamma
.

Remark 5.1.10
.

We could also prove that in the example above

\frac{1}{H^{N_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}}\theta^{N_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}
is well-defined without appealing to

Theorem

3.3.13
and its relation to

\theta^{w_{0,M}\lambda\uparrow w_{0,M}\mu}
. We can prove this by combining

Theorem

4.4.2
(2) and
Theorem

4.3.2
as in
[
61
, Thm 3.10]
,
in a way closely resembling the proof of the classical theta cycle map.
Let
f\in\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
be a local section, and suppose first that it is not divisible by
H_{\alpha}
.
By the first one
we know that for
n<N_{\alpha}
we have

H_{\alpha}\nmid\theta^{n}_{\alpha}(f)
, and some power
k
of
H_{\alpha}
divides

\theta^{N_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}(f)
.
We also know by
Theorem

4.3.2

that
H^{p}_{\alpha}\mid\theta^{p}_{\alpha}(f)
, so by repeating the argument we get that

k\geq N_{\alpha}
. The case of
H_{\alpha}\mid f

follows by induction on
\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\bmod{p}
, with the case where
p\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
being the base case.

In the case of simple linkage maps we can understand their behaviour on global sections.

Theorem 5.1.11
.

Let
p\geq 3
.
Assume that
G^{\textnormal{der}}
is simply connected, and that
2.8.2
(2) holds.
Let
G_{i}
be an almost-simple factor of
G^{\text{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
,

\alpha\in\Delta_{G_{i}}
which is not on
\textnormal{Lie}(M)
.
Let
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
and
\mu\in X^{*}(T)

such that
\lambda\uparrow_{w_{0,M}\alpha}\mu
.
Then

\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}:\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\mu+\eta))

is injective for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
.

Proof.

From
5.1.7
we see that
\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
is a simple theta linkage map.
Using the identities
\frac{1}{H^{p}_{\alpha}}\theta^{p}_{\alpha}=\theta_{\mu\uparrow\lambda+pw_{0,M}\alpha}\circ\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
from
5.1.6
and

\frac{1}{H^{p}_{\alpha}}\theta^{p}_{\alpha}=\theta_{\delta}
from

Theorem

4.3.2
(the case of semisimple rank
1
is analogous) we can reduce to proving injectivity of
\theta_{\delta}
.
But then it follows from

Theorem

4.4.7
.
∎

Note that in the notation from above
w_{0,M}\alpha
is always
the longest root of
G_{j}
, see the beginning of the proof of
Theorem

4.3.2
. Therefore,
when considering unitary Shimura varieties of different signatures, one always finds
the same
\gamma\in\Phi
such that
Theorem

5.1.11
applies to
\theta_{\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu}
.

5.2.
Relation between Borel and parabolic linkage maps

We can also define a kind of theta linkage maps on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
by constructing maps between parabolic Verma modules.
These are harder to understand, but they occur naturally when considering the de Rham cohomology of
the Shimura variety. We prove that we can construct maps of parabolic Verma modules
such that the associated operator over
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
comes from pushforward
from a theta linkage map on
\mathcal{F}l
, which we can understand more explicitly
in terms of basic theta operators.

Lemma 5.2.1
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)
, and let
f:\lambda\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)
a
B
-equivariant map.
Then there is a unique
P
-equivariant map
g:W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)^{\vee}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}

fitting in the diagram of
B
-equivariant maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

The map
\phi
is given by a choice of a highest weight vector of
W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)^{\vee}
,
using that
-\lambda=w_{0,M}(-w_{0,M}\lambda)
is a lowest weight for
W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)
.
On the other hand,
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}
is defined by
yx\otimes\mu\mapsto y\otimes xv^{\vee}_{-\mu}
for
y\in U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{P}
,
x\in U\mathfrak{u}^{-}_{M}
,
and
v_{-\mu}
a vector of
W(-w_{0,M}\mu)
of weight
-\mu
.

Proof.

The map
f
induces a differential operator
P_{G/B}\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\mu)\to\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)
by

3.1.19
. Let
\pi:G/B\to G/P
.
Its pushforward
precomposed with
P_{G/P}\otimes W(-w_{0,M}\mu)\to\pi_{*}[P_{G/B}\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\mu)]
(using that
-\mu
is a lowest
weight for
W(-w_{0,M}\mu)
)
defines a
G
-equivariant differential operator

h:P_{G/P}\otimes W(-w_{0,M}\mu)\to W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)
.
We define
g
as the associated map of Verma modules.
Let
\epsilon:\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\to\text{id}
be the unit of the adjunction.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}[P_{G/P}\otimes W(-w_{0,M}\mu)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}\pi_{*}[P_{G/B}\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\mu)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G/B}\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}\pi_{*}f^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\vee}}

To prove
g
makes the diagram commute we must prove that the big square above commutes, where the composition
of the top horizontal arrows corresponds to
\pi^{*}g
.
By naturality of
\epsilon
we reduce to proving the commutativity of the small triangle, which is clear.
Conversely, if
g
fits
in the diagram it corresponds to the composition

\pi^{*}(P_{G/P}\otimes W(-w_{0,M}\mu))\to\pi^{*}\pi_{*}(P_{G/B}\otimes\mathcal{L}(-\mu))\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}\pi_{*}f}\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)
. Applying
\pi_{*}
and the projection formula recovers
the previous construction, so this proves the uniqueness.
∎

We will denote such a map
g
as
\pi_{*}f
. The maps of parabolic Verma modules that are pushforwards from
Borel Verma modules are sometimes denoted as
standard
in the literature,
e.g.
[
6
, §3]
. It is known that there can be
more maps than the standard one, see
[
7
, Prop 10.1]
for some examples in characteristic
0

for the group

G=\textnormal{GL}_{4}
.

Proposition 5.2.2
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)
, and let
f:\lambda\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)
a
B
-equivariant map. It induces
a differential operator
\theta(f):\mathcal{L}(-\mu)\to\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)
. Consider
\pi_{*}f
from

Lemma

5.2.1
, it induces a differential operator
\theta(\pi_{*}f):\omega(-w_{0,M}\mu)\to\omega(-w_{0,M}\lambda)
. Then

\pi_{*}\theta(f)=\theta(\pi_{*}f).

Proof.

We can prove it on PD formal completions of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-points, where we can use the Grothendieck–Messing
period map to reduce to the case of the flag variety, where it follows by definition of
\pi_{*}f
.
∎

Remark 5.2.3
.

The above shows that given
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu
for
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{M}
,

\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}:\omega(\mu+\eta)\to\omega(\lambda+\eta)
is linear, and it is induced
by the map of
P
representations induced by
\pi_{*}\phi(-w_{0,M}\lambda-\eta\uparrow-w_{0,M}\mu-\eta)
.

As a corollary of the above we get a relation between theta linkage maps on
\mathcal{F}l
and
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

Corollary 5.2.4
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T)
such that
\lambda\uparrow_{\gamma}\mu
for some
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
.

(1)

There is a
P
-equivariant map
g:W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)^{\vee}
,
and it satisfies

\pi_{*}\theta^{\lambda\uparrow\mu}=\Phi_{P}(g)
.

(2)

Moreover, if
\gamma=\alpha\in\Delta
,
\alpha\notin\textnormal{Lie}(M)
,
and
W(-w_{0,M}\mu)
is irreducible, then there is only one
P
-equivariant map

W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)^{\vee}
.

Proof.

For
(1)
we take
g
the pushforward of
the map of Borel Verma modules
\phi(-\mu\uparrow-\lambda)
in
5.1.3
.
To prove uniqueness in
the second case, note that

\mu-\lambda
is a multiple of
\alpha
. Since
W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}
is generated by the
highest weight vector
v_{\mu}

[
36
, 2.13(b)]
,
such a map
g
must send
v_{\mu}
to
x^{N_{\alpha}}_{-\alpha}\otimes v_{\lambda}
,
where
v_{\lambda}
is the unique vector of weight
\lambda
.
∎

5.3.
Kernel of flag-like linkage maps

By
3.1.16

a crystalline differential operator
\theta:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\mu)
over
\mathcal{F}l
is always Frobenius linear,
so it is natural to try to understand the kernel of
F_{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to F_{*}\mathcal{L}(\mu)
, where
F

is the absolute Frobenius. If one understood this one could reduce the study of whether a theta operator is injective on
global sections to understanding the sections of the kernel. Sadly we currently don’t understand much about this,
for instance, we don’t even know if
F_{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
is an automorphic vector bundle on
\mathcal{F}l
.
We can only understand this systematically
in the case that
\theta
is a linkage map such its pushforward to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
is linear, and replacing
F
by the relative Frobenius.

Definition 5.3.1
.

We say that a
B
-equivariant map
\lambda\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)
is flag-like if it factors through
\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(\mu)
.
We say that a theta linkage map is flag-like if its associated map of Verma modules is flag-like.

In fact, flag-like theta linkage maps are precisely the ones that become linear on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

A theta linkage map
\theta
is flag-like if and only if
\pi_{*}\theta
is a linear map. Moreover, in that case

\pi_{*}\theta
comes from a map of
M
-representations.

Proof.

Let
\theta
be given by a map
f:\lambda\to\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)
. Suppose first that
\phi
is flag-like
so that
f
factors through
\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(\mu)
.
Then by weight considerations the map

\pi_{*}f:W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)^{\vee}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}
must come from a map of
P
-representations

g:W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)^{\vee}\to W(-w_{0,M}\mu)^{\vee}
. Then
5.2.2
implies that

\pi_{*}\theta=\theta(\pi_{*}f)

is linear and comes from the map
g
. Conversely, suppose that
\pi_{*}\theta=\theta(\pi_{*}f)
is linear.
Then
\pi_{*}f
comes from a map
g
as before. By the diagram in
Lemma

5.2.1
we see that

\mu-\lambda
must be in the
\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}\Phi^{+}_{M}
, which implies that
f
factors through
\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(\mu)
.
∎

Proposition 5.3.3
.

Let
\phi:\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(\lambda)\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(\mu)
be a
B
-equivariant map, with associated differential operator

\theta:\mathcal{L}(-\mu)\to\mathcal{L}(-\lambda)
. Let
F:\mathcal{F}l\to\mathcal{F}l^{(p)}
be the relative Frobenius with respect to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.
Let also
\phi^{0}:\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B}(\lambda)\to\textnormal{Ver}^{0}_{P/B}(\mu)
be the map induced by
\phi
. Then

\textnormal{Ker}F^{*}F_{*}\theta=F_{B}\textnormal{Ker}(\phi^{0})^{\vee},

where we identify
F^{*}F_{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
with
\textnormal{Ver}^{0,\vee}_{P/B}(-\lambda)
using
e^{0}_{P/B}
in

Theorem

3.2.2
.
Moreover, let
\tilde{F}:P/B\to P/B
be the absolute Frobenius,
then
\phi
induces a
G
-equivariant map
\tilde{\theta}:\tilde{F}_{*}\mathcal{L}_{P/B}(-\mu)\to\tilde{F}_{*}\mathcal{L}_{P/B}(-\lambda)
.
Then we can write
\textnormal{Ker}\tilde{\theta}=F_{P/B}(V_{\phi})
for some
V_{\phi}\in\textnormal{Rep}(B)
. Then

\textnormal{Ker}F_{*}\theta=\sigma^{*}F_{B}(V_{\phi}),

where
\sigma:\mathcal{F}l^{(p)}\to\mathcal{F}l
is the base change morphism.

Proof.

The first part follows immediately from
Theorem

3.2.4
(1). The second part follows in the same way, by transporting
it from the flag variety via the Grothendieck–Messing map.
∎

Example 5.3.4
.

Take the example of
G=\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
with
P
the Siegel parabolic. In the notation of

4.2.4
let
\alpha=\alpha_{1}
.
Let
\phi:(-l^{\prime},-k^{\prime})\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P/B}(-l,-k)
be the map inducing the linkage map
for
\alpha\in\textnormal{Lie}(M)
. Then if
k-l=ap+b
with
0\leq b\leq p-1
we have

\textnormal{Ker}\phi^{0,\vee}=(ap,0)\otimes W(l+b,l),

with
\mathfrak{p}
acting naturally on
W(l+b,l)
and trivially on
(ap,0)
. By the
P/B

version of
Remark

3.3.7
this ensures that

V_{\phi}=(a,0)\otimes W(l+b,l)
.

5.3.3
together with the divisibility criteria for simple basic theta operators
gives a description of the kernel of a flag-like simple basic theta operator.

Corollary 5.3.5
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta\cap\textnormal{Lie}(M)
and
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
.
Let
F:\mathcal{F}l\to\mathcal{F}l^{(p)}
be the relative Frobenius with respect to
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.
Assume that
H_{\alpha}
has a simple zero. Write

\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=pa+b
for
1\leq b\leq p
.
Then

F_{*}\textnormal{Ker}(\theta_{\alpha}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda+h_{\alpha}+\alpha))=F_{*}\textnormal{Ker}(\theta_{\alpha}/H_{\alpha}:\mathcal{L}(\lambda-(p-b)h_{\alpha})\to\mathcal{L}(\lambda-(p-b)h_{\alpha}+\alpha)),

where the inclusion to
F_{*}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
is given by multiplication by
H^{p-b}_{\alpha}
, and the right-hand side
can be computed by
5.3.3
.

Proof.

Given a local section
f\in\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
in the kernel of
\theta_{\alpha}
we have to show that

H^{p-b}_{\alpha}\mid f
. We proceed by induction on
b
, with

b=p
being the base case. Then we use
Theorem

4.4.2
,
4.2.7
(2)
and the observation that

\langle h_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=\langle pw_{0,M}\sigma^{-1}\chi_{\alpha}-\chi_{\alpha},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=-1\bmod{p}
.
∎

We currently don’t have a good understanding of the kernel of a ”non flag-like”
simple basic theta operator, even just on global sections. Essentially we can only understand the
following case, which is
a direct generalization of the
\textnormal{GL}_{2}
case.

Example 5.3.6
.

Let
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
be the unitary Shimura variety of signature
(n-1,1)
as in

Example

2.3.2
with
p
split
in the quadratic imaginary field. Let
\alpha=\alpha_{n-1}\in\Delta\setminus\textnormal{Lie}(M)
.
Let
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and write
\omega(\underline{k_{1}},k_{2}):=\omega(k_{1},k_{1},\ldots,k_{1},k_{2})
.
Let
F
be the absolute Frobenius on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
. Assume for simplicity that
1\leq k_{1}\leq p
, then

\textnormal{Ker}(F_{*}\pi_{*}\theta_{\alpha}:F_{*}\omega(\underline{k_{1}},pk_{2})\to F_{*}\omega(\underline{k_{1}}+(p,p-1,\ldots,p-1),pk_{2}-1))=\omega(\underline{k_{1}-p+1},k_{2})

via
\omega(\underline{k_{1}-p+1},k_{2})\to F_{*}\omega(p\underline{(k_{1}-p+1)},pk_{2})\xrightarrow{H^{p-k_{1}}_{\alpha}}F_{*}\omega(\underline{k_{1}},pk_{2})
, where the first map induced by the adjunction

F^{*}F_{*}
. This follows by
Theorem

4.4.2
(2), and the observation that by the Leibniz rule,
on a local basis

\theta_{\alpha}
acting on
\mathcal{L}(p\underline{(k_{1}-p+1)},pk_{2})
can be identified with
\theta_{\alpha}
acting
on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}l}
. Therefore, we can check that

\pi_{*}\theta_{\alpha}
can be identified with
d:\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}\to\Omega^{1}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
,
e.g. using
5.2.2
,
whose kernel is precisely the functions that are
p
th powers.
The same kind of example will work for a unitary Shimura variety of any signature with
p
totally split
in the CM field,
or in the Siegel case.

6.
Duality

We prove a compatibility result between the functors in
Theorem

3.2.4
and Serre duality.

6.1.
Duality of differential operators

Fix
X/S
a smooth scheme whose fibers are equidimensional of dimension
d
. Let
\omega_{X}
be its
relative canonical bundle.

Definition 6.1.1
.

For a vector bundle
E
over
X
let
E^{*}=E^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{X}
. Let
f:P_{X}\otimes E\to V

be a (HPD) crystalline differential operator between two vector bundles.
By tensor-hom adjunction, and since
E,V
are reflexive, we get a map
V^{*}\otimes P_{X}\otimes E\to\omega_{X}
.
Again by tensor-hom adjunction we get a map

f^{*}:V^{*}\otimes P_{X}\to E^{*}
which is
\mathcal{O}
-linear for the
right
\mathcal{O}
-structure on
P_{X}
, so it defines
an element of
\textnormal{DiffOp}(V^{*},E^{*})
. We say that
f^{*}
is the dual differential operator of
f
.
The same definition extends to log differential operators. We note that
(f^{*})^{*}=f
canonically.

The goal of this subsection is to state the following compatibility with Serre duality.

Theorem 6.1.2
.

Let
S
an affine scheme and
let
\pi:X\to S
be a smooth proper scheme whose fibers are equidimensional of dimension
d
. Let
E,V
be vector bundles
on
X
, and
f:P\otimes E\to V
a PD differential operator. Then the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{RHom}(R\pi_{*}E,\mathcal{O}_{S})[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\pi_{*}E^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{RHom}(R\pi_{*}V,\mathcal{O}_{S})[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\pi_{*}V^{*},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{RHom}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

where the horizontal isomorphisms are given by Serre’s duality.
If
S
is the spectrum of a field, talking cohomology for
0\leq i\leq d
we get that the
following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}^{i}(X,E)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}^{d-i}(X,E^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}^{i}(X,V)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}^{d-i}(X,E^{*}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}^{i}(f)^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

The same statements hold for log differential operators for a pair
(X,D)
over
S
.

Proof.

The commutativity of the first diagram in the case of Grothendieck’s differential operators
is proved in

[
37
, §7.1]
.
There is a map

\textnormal{DiffOp}(E,V)\to\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\tilde{P}\otimes E,V)\to\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}(E,V)
,
where the first map to Grothendieck’s differential operators is compatible with duality.
Since the commutativity of the diagram
here only concerns
the image of this composition, it follows from that case.
To obtain the result on cohomology we take
\textnormal{H}^{i}
of the diagram above, and since
S
is a field
every complex in
D(\mathcal{O}_{S})
is a perfect complex with trivial differentials, so we get
the correct cohomology groups. The statement for log differential operators
is immediate since they map to usual differential operators.
∎

6.2.
Dual linkage maps

Let
Q\in\{P,B\}
with Levi
M
,
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
and
V_{i}\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(Q)
.
Given
\phi:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{1})\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2})
a
Q
-equivariant map, it induces a
G
-equivariant
differential operator
\psi:P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2}\to\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1}
by
3.1.19
.
Let
K_{G/Q}
denote the canonical bundle.
Then
\phi^{*}:P_{G/Q}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{1}\otimes K_{G/Q}\to\mathcal{V}_{2}\otimes K_{G/Q}
is still
G
-equivariant since the tensor-hom adjunction defining it
preserves
G
-equivariance. Therefore, it induces a map

\phi^{*}:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V^{\vee}_{2}\otimes W(2\rho_{M}-2\rho))\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V^{\vee}_{1}\otimes W(2\rho_{M}-2\rho))
,
where
\rho_{M}
is the half sum of positive roots of
M
, so that
K_{G/Q}=\mathcal{W}(2\rho-2\rho_{M})
.

Definition 6.2.1
.

Given a
Q
-equivariant map
\phi:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{1})\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2})
we define its dual as
\phi^{*}
constructed above.
Denote by
V^{*}:=V^{\vee}\otimes W(2\rho-2\rho_{M})
.

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
,
Q\in\{P,B\}
,
V_{i}\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(Q)
,
and
\phi:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{1})\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2})
a
Q
-equivariant map over
R
.
Then

\Phi_{Q}(\phi)^{*}=\Phi_{Q}(\phi^{*}),

where
\Phi_{Q}
is the functor of
Theorem

3.2.4
. On toroidal compactifications
we have

\Phi^{\textnormal{can}}_{Q}(\phi)^{*}=\Phi^{\textnormal{sub}}_{Q}(\phi^{*})

and the other way around.

Proof.

We can check everything at the level of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-points since it is a map of
vector bundles. Thus, for
x\in\mathcal{F}l_{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
, we use the description of
the canonical isomorphisms defining
\Phi_{Q}
in terms the Grothendieck–Messing maps
over
\mathcal{F}l^{\sharp}_{Q,x,R}
of
3.2.11
.
Since
\phi^{*}
by definition produces the dual differential operator
on
G/Q
we reduce to the following lemma. We say that a PD formal scheme
\text{Spf}(A)
(whose underlying
reduced is a point) over
R

is good if
A\cong R[[\frac{x^{n_{i}}_{i}}{n_{i}!},y_{j}]]
, where
(x_{i})
is the implicit ideal with
divided powers. Even if
A
is not smooth can define crystalline differential operators on
A
by
using
P_{A,\delta}
, which is a
A
-bimodule.
We can also define
\omega_{A,\delta}
and dual differential operators.

Lemma 6.2.3
.

Let
\pi:\mathfrak{X}_{1}=\textnormal{Spf}(R_{1})\to\mathfrak{X}_{2}=\textnormal{Spf}(R_{2})
be a map of affinoid good
PD
formal schemes over
R
,
such that it induces an isomorphism
\pi^{*}\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{2},\delta}\cong\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{1},\delta}
.
Let
E,V/R_{2}
be locally free modules, and let
f:P_{\mathfrak{X}_{2},\delta}\otimes E\to V
be
a differential operator. Consider the isomorphism
d\pi^{\vee}_{V}:\pi^{*}(P_{\mathfrak{X}_{2},\delta}\otimes V)\cong P_{\mathfrak{X}_{1},\delta}\otimes\pi^{*}V
of (
3.2.1
). Together
d\pi^{\vee}_{E}
and
\pi^{*}(f^{*})

define a differential operator
f_{1}:P_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\otimes\pi^{*}E\to\pi^{*}V
. Similarly,

d\pi^{\vee}_{V^{*}}
and
\pi^{*}f
define a differential operator
f_{2}:P_{\mathfrak{X}_{1},\delta}\otimes(\pi^{*}V)^{*}\cong P_{\mathfrak{X}_{1},\delta}\otimes\pi^{*}V^{*}\to\pi^{*}E^{*}\cong(\pi^{*}E)^{*}
. Then

f^{*}_{1}=f_{2}.

Proof.

This follows formally from the compatibility of tensor-hom adjunction with
\pi^{*}
, and the observation
that we can reduce to the case
E=V=\mathcal{O}
, by the way

d\pi^{\vee}_{V}
is defined.
∎

Finally, the statement on toroidal compactifications can be proved on the interior, being a map between vector bundles.

∎

Duality for
B
and
\mathcal{F}l
is not very interesting, as automorphic line bundles are self-dual, it does not change
the weight shift of
a theta linkage map, and the basic theta operators are self-dual.

Remark 6.2.4
.

In the case of
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
Yamauchi
[
75
, Prop 3.13]
defined
3
theta operators on
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
, one of which corresponds to

\pi_{*}\theta_{\beta}
. By
4.2.5
(1)
and the fact that the dual of
\nabla
on
\mathcal{V}
is

\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{d-1}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{d}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
,
we can check that the one denoted as
\theta^{\underline{k}}_{1}
is the dual differential operator of

\pi_{*}\theta_{\beta}
. In that paper they are only defined
for certain weights, since in general these dual maps will be only maps between the duals of automorphic
vector bundles.

6.3.
Serre duality on
\mathcal{O}_{P,R}

For the applications in
Section

8
it will be convenenient to extend the duality
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W\mapsto\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W^{*}
of
6.2.1

to
D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{P,R})
. It will be immediately clear that one needs to work over the derived category.
Let
R=W/p^{n}
, for
W=W(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

Let
C\in D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{P,R})
, then
C
is quasi-isomorphic to a complex whose terms are of the form
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W
for
W\in\textnormal{Mod}^{\textnormal{fg}}_{R}(P)
.

Proof.

By virtue of being finitely generated as a
U\mathfrak{g}
-module every object in
\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
admits a surjection from a Verma module as in the statement. Then the lemma follows from

[
11
, Thm 12.7]
.
∎

Definition 6.3.2
.

Let
V\in D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{P,R})
. Let
V
be quasi-isomorphic to
C=[\ldots\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W_{i}\xrightarrow{f_{i}}\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W_{i+1}\to\ldots]
as in
Lemma

6.3.1
.
Define
V^{*}
as
[\ldots\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W^{*}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{f^{*}_{i}}\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W^{*}_{i}\to\ldots]
.
Then
V^{*}
is independent of the choice of
C
.

Proof.

Suppose that
C_{1}=[\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W_{i}],C_{2}=[\textnormal{Ver}_{P}V_{i}]
are two quasi-isomorphic complexes of Verma modules. Then using
3.1.19
and the exactness of the admissible dual we get two quasi-isomorphic complexes

[P_{G/P}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{i}]\cong[P_{G/P}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{i}]
of
G
-equivariant sheaves on
G/P
. Then we take the Serre dual of both complexes to get a quasi-isomorphism

[P_{G/P}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{i}\otimes K_{G/P}]\cong[P_{G/P}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{i}\otimes K_{G/P}]
.
Using
Lemma

3.1.18
we get a quasi-isomorphism of complexes

[\textnormal{Ver}^{\vee}(W^{*}_{i})]\cong[\textnormal{Ver}^{\vee}(V^{*}_{i})]
. Applying duality on
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{P,R}
and using that the double dual is isomorphic to the identity
Lemma

3.1.9
(2),
we get that
C^{*}_{1}\cong C^{*}_{2}
,
since dual maps of Verma modules are defined via this process.
∎

As an example, given
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(G)
, it has a standard resolution by
the standard complex
C=[\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{p}\otimes V)]
. One can explicitly see that for
p
large enough with respect to
G
,
V^{*}=V^{\vee}[-d]
where
d
is the dimension of
G/P
.
However, Serre duality preserves a certain class of objects in
\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
.
We endow
D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{P,R})
with the natural
t
-structure, which enables to see
\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
as the subcategory of objects whose cohomology is concentrated in degree
0
.

Lemma 6.3.3
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
have a finite filtration by Verma modules. Then
[V]^{*}
lies in
\mathcal{O}_{P,R}
, and we simply denote it by
V^{*}
. Similarly,
if
C=[V_{1}\to V_{2}\to\ldots V_{n}]\in D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{P,R})
is a complex with each
V_{i}
having a finite filtration by Verma modules, then

C^{*}=[V^{*}_{n}\to V^{*}_{n-1}\to\ldots\to V^{*}_{1}].

Proof.

By induction it is enough to prove it when
0\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W_{1}\to V\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W_{2}\to 0
is an extension of two Verma modules. We get an exact triangle

[\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W^{*}_{2}]\to V^{*}\to[\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W^{*}_{1}]
, which shows that
V^{*}
has cohomology concentrated in degree
0
, so it lies in the heart of the
t
-structure.
The second statement follows from a general fact about complexes whose terms are acyclic for a derived functor.
∎

7.
The weight part of Serre’s conjecture: entailments

7.1.
Generalities about the weight part of Serre’s conjecture

We explain the setup of the weight part of Serre’s conjecture for Shimura varieties in some generality.
Let
\textnormal{Sh}/\mathcal{O}
be the integral model of a Hodge type Shimura variety at hyperspecial level,
with reductive model
G/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and reflex field
E_{0}/\mathbb{Q}
. There is an action of a spherical
Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
on coherent or étale cohomology of the Shimura variety.
Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite extension such that
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
splits over
F
, so that
E_{0}\subseteq F
. Let
\check{G}
be the dual group
of the split group
G_{F}
, as a group over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
We will work with the following assumption.

•

Given a mod
p
Hecke eigensystem
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}

appearing in the coherent or étale cohomology of the Shimura variety,
we can attach a semisimple mod
p
Galois representation
\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}:\textnormal{Gal}_{F}\to\check{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})

as in

[
17
, Conj 2.2.1]
.

Recall that
\mathcal{O}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}=E
is the completion of the reflex field
E_{0}
at some prime
\mathfrak{p}
above
p
.
For simplicity let us assume that we can take
F=E_{0}
and that
G^{\textnormal{der}}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is almost-simple.
This holds, for instance, for the Siegel threefold and unitary Shimura varieties where the CM field
is quadratic and the signature is not
(n,n)
. From now on we will assume that
Sh
is one of these,
and in the case of unitary Shimura varieties we will also assume that it is compact
5
5

5

This is mainly so that

2.8.2
(1) holds by the work of
[
76
]
.
.
Let
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
be the restriction of
\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}
to the decomposition group
of
\mathfrak{p}
. Then we define the set of modular Serre weights as

W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}):=\{F(\lambda):\textnormal{H}^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},F(\lambda))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\}.

We will often drop
\mathfrak{m}
from the notation and write
W(\overline{\rho})
.
In the case of
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbb{T}
be a maximal ideal occurring in the coherent cohomology of
the toroidal compactification
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}}
, e.g. occuring in
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(k,l))

for
k\geq l\geq 0
.
Then one can attach a semisimple Galois representation
\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}:\textnormal{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\textnormal{GSp}_{4}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})

by the construction of Galois representations for automorphic representations of regular weight
[
73
]
,
and the use of generalized Hasse invariants in
[
9
]
or
[
23
]
.
We say that
\mathfrak{m}
is non-Eisenstein if
\overline{r}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is irreducible
as a
\textnormal{GL}_{4}
-valued representation. For some compact unitary Shimura varieties,
as in Harris–Taylor Shimura varieties from
, Galois representations for characteristic
0

automorphic forms were constructed
in
[
29
]
and
[
69
]
. For mod
p
eigenforms, they are constructed via congruences as in the
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
case.

We briefly explain Herzig’s
recipe
[
32
]

[
31
]
for
W(\overline{\rho})
in the case that
\overline{\rho}
is semisimple
(in the
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
case this means semisimple as a
4
-dimensional representation),
as it will be relevant for the weight shifts of the operators.
For the two cases above we may assume that
E=\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}
is unramified.
For
t\geq 1
let
\omega_{t}:\textnormal{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{nt}}}\to\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}

be a niveau
tn
fundamental character. Note that in the two cases that we are considering
G
is self-dual.
Given
\overline{\rho}:\textnormal{Gal}_{E}\to\check{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
we can
write the semisimplification of
the restriction to inertia
\tau\coloneqq\overline{\rho}^{\text{ss}}_{\mid I_{\mathfrak{p}}}
as

\tau=\tau(\mu,w)\coloneqq(\overline{\mu}+p^{n}w^{-1}\overline{\mu}+\ldots+p^{n(t-1)}w^{1-t}\overline{\mu})\omega_{t}

where
w\in W
is such that

\check{w}\tau\sim\tau^{p^{n}}
,

t\geq 1
is the order of
w
,
\mu\in X^{*}(T)
, and
\overline{\mu}\in X_{*}(\check{T})
. We are fixing
the isomorphism
G=\check{G}
swapping
T
and
\check{T}
,
which on Weyl groups we denote by
w\to\check{w}
, and sending
\mu
to
\overline{\mu}
.
Then any semisimple
\tau:I_{\mathfrak{p}}\to G(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
that lifts
to
\textnormal{Gal}_{E}
(a tame inertial parameter)
can always be written as
\tau(\mu,w)
, and for generic
\tau
we can assume that
\mu\in X_{1}(T)
.
Herzig proposed a conjectural set of Serre weights
W^{?}(\overline{\rho})
when
\overline{\rho}
is semisimple
(restricting to generic weights),
based
on the compatibility with inertial mod
p
Langlands.
Further restricting to generic
weights, the set proposed by Herzig is
[
32
, Prop 6.28]

[
21
, Prop 10.1.13]

(7.1.1)

\displaystyle W^{?}(\overline{\rho})=\{F(\mu)\text{ such that }

\displaystyle\exists\;\mu^{\prime}\uparrow\mu\text{ with }\mu^{\prime}+\rho\text{ dominant and}

(7.1.2)

\displaystyle w\in W,\text{ such that }\overline{\rho}_{\mid I}=\tau(\mu^{\prime}+\rho,w)\}.

We say that
F(\mu)\in W^{?}(\overline{\rho})
is an
obvious weight
if we can take
\mu^{\prime}=\mu
above,
and a
shadow
weight
otherwise. The obvious weights take their name since for
\overline{\rho}
semisimple, given
any obvious weight in
W^{?}(\overline{\rho})
,
it admits an ”obvious” crystalline lift (by lifting the fundamental characters)
whose Hodge-Tate weights are associated to the obvious weight.
For general
\overline{\rho}
one expects that
W(\overline{\rho})\subseteq W^{?}(\overline{\rho}^{\text{ss}})
.
We immediately see that the combinatorics of the shadow weights are related to the combinatorics
of the theta linkage maps.
In fact, we will use theta linkage maps to prove the existence of certain shadow weights
in this section and the next one.
We recall our definition of generic weights in
2.2.1
, since we will make heavy use of it
in this section.

7.2.
A generic entailment for
\textnormal{GL}_{4}

By studying Breuil–Mezard cycles
[
53
]
can prove that there should not be
generic entailments for the group
\textnormal{GL}_{3}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, so the next
natural candidate for a generic entailment beyond
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is
\textnormal{GL}_{4}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
For this, we can work with a unitary group
G/\mathbb{Q}
with respect
to a quadratic imaginary
E/\mathbb{Q}
with
p
split in
E
. We can either work with signature
(3,1)
or
(2,2)
.

Notation 7.2.1
.

Consider
Sh
, the unitary Shimura variety of signature
(a,b)
for a quadratic imaginary
E/\mathbb{Q}
.
Then
G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}=\textnormal{GL}_{n}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Weights are of the form
(\lambda,c)
with
c
a character of

\mathbb{G}_{m}
. We will suppress the similitude character
c
from the notation from now on, since the representation
theory of
G_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
is equivalent to that of
\textnormal{GL}_{n}
.
For
\textnormal{GL}_{n}
we will use the notation for roots as in
4.2.4
, and take

\rho=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,0)
.

For
\textnormal{GL}_{4}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
we have
6

p
-restricted alcoves consisting of
(a,b,c,d)-\rho\in X^{*}(T)
satisfying

\displaystyle C_{0}:\quad

\displaystyle 0<a-b,\;b-c,\;c-d;\ a-d<p,

\displaystyle C_{1}:\quad

\displaystyle 0<b-c;\ p<a-d;\ a-c,\;b-d<p,

\displaystyle C_{2}:\quad

\displaystyle 0<c-d;\ p<a-c;\ a-b,\;b-d<p,

\displaystyle C_{3}:\quad

\displaystyle 0<a-b;\ p<b-d;\ c-d,\;a-c<p,

\displaystyle C_{4}:\quad

\displaystyle p<a-c,\;b-d;\ b-c<p;\ a-d<2p,

\displaystyle C_{5}:\quad

\displaystyle 2p<a-d;\ a-b,\;b-c,\;c-d<p,

\displaystyle C_{0^{\prime}}:\quad

\displaystyle 0<b-c,\;c-d;\ p<a-b;\ a-d<2p,

\displaystyle C_{0^{\prime\prime}}:\quad

\displaystyle 0<a-b,\;b-c;\ p<c-d;\ a-d<2p.

Here
C_{0^{\prime}}
and
C_{0^{\prime\prime}}
are not
p
-restricted but appear as constituents of
p
-restricted Weyl modules.
We can also describe the decomposition of dual Weyl modules into irreducible constituents in this case. We
only state the ones that we will use.

Proposition 7.2.2
.

[
35
]

Let
\lambda_{0}=(a,b,c,d)\in C_{0}
, and let
\lambda_{i}\in C_{i}
be the unique element of

W_{\textnormal{aff}}\cdot\lambda_{0}
in
C_{i}
. Then in the Grothendieck group of representations of

\textnormal{GL}_{4}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
we have the following relations.

•

V(\lambda_{4})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}=L(\lambda_{4})+L(\lambda_{3})+L(\lambda_{2})+L(\lambda_{1})+L(\lambda_{0})

•

V(\lambda_{5})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}=L(\lambda_{5})+L(\lambda_{4})+L(\lambda_{3})+L(\lambda_{2})+L(\lambda_{1})+L(\lambda_{0^{\prime}})+L(\lambda_{0^{\prime\prime}})

where
\lambda_{0^{\prime}}=(p+b-1,c-1,d-1,a-p+3)
and
\lambda_{0^{\prime\prime}}=(p+d-3,a+1,b+1,c-p+1)
.
In the Grothendieck group of
\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}\textnormal{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
we have that for generic
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}

\displaystyle F(\lambda_{0^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=F(b,c-1,d-1,a-p+3)+F(b-1,c,d-1,a-p+3)

\displaystyle+F(b-1,c-1,d,a-p+3)+F(b-1,c-1,d-1,a-p+4)

and

\displaystyle F(\lambda_{0^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=F(p+d-2,a-p+2,b-p+2,c-p+1)+F(p+d-2,a-p+1,b-p+2,c-p+2)

\displaystyle+F(p+d-2,a-p+2,b-p+1,c-p+2)+F(p+d-3,a-p+2,b-p+2,c-p+2)

Similarly, in the Grothendieck group of
\textnormal{Rep}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}U_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
we have that for generic
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}

\displaystyle F(\lambda_{0^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=F(b-1,c-1,d-1,a-p+2)+F(b-1,c-1,d-2,a-p+3)

\displaystyle+F(b-1,c-2,d-1,a-p+3)+F(b-2,c-1,d-1,a-p+3)

and

\displaystyle F(\lambda_{0^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=F(p+d-4,a-p,b-p,c-p+1)+F(p+d-4,a-p+1,b-p,c-p)

\displaystyle+F(p+d-4,a-p,b-p+1,c-p)+F(p+d-3,a-p,b-p,c-p)

Proof.

The decomposition of dual Weyl modules is from
[
35
]
. The decompositions for
F(\lambda_{0^{\prime}})

follow from Steinberg’s tensor product, the fact that
L(\lambda)=V(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in C_{0}
,
and the Littlewood-Richardson rule for tensor products of highest weight representations.
For
\textnormal{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
we use that
L(1,0,0,0)^{(p)}(\mathbb{F}_{p})=F(1,0,0,0)
and
L(1,1,1,0)^{(p)}(\mathbb{F}_{p})=F(1,1,1,0)
.
For
U_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
we use that

L(1,0,0,0)^{(p)}(\mathbb{F}_{p})=F(0,0,0,-1)
, and
L(1,1,1,0)^{(p)}(\mathbb{F}_{p})=F(0,-1,-1,-1)

since in that case Frobenius acts as

\sigma(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})=(-a_{4},-a_{3},-a_{2},-a_{1})
swapping
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{3}
.
∎

We have included only these two dual Weyl modules because
they are the only pair of
p
-restricted
\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}

with
\lambda_{i}\uparrow\lambda_{j}
such that
V(\lambda_{i})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
contains an irreducible factor which is not contained
in
V(\lambda_{j})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
, which is the key phenomenon we look for to produce entailments. The full list can be checked
in
[
32
, Prop 9.3]
(in the thesis version).
Also, crucially for us
\lambda_{4}\uparrow_{\delta}\lambda_{5}
, for
\delta
the longest root of
\textnormal{GL}_{4}
,
which is the relevant one in
Theorem

5.1.11
.
We make
Theorem

2.8.3
explicit in the
\textnormal{GL}_{4}
case. See
Example

2.3.2
for
the explicit description of the automorphic vector bundles.

Proposition 7.2.3
.

Let
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
be a compact unitary Shimura variety with
p
split in the quadratic imaginary field,
and
\lambda=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\in X^{*}(T)
.
Then

(1)

If
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
has signature
(2,2)
then if
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda))\neq 0
we have

p(a_{2}-a_{3})+(a_{1}-a_{4})\geq 0
. If
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
has signature
(3,1)
the condition is that

\lambda
is in the cone generated by the weights of the Hasse invariants and the cone

\{(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}):(b_{1}-b_{4})+p(b_{2}-b_{4})+p^{2}(b_{3}-b_{4})\}

[
25
, Thm 4.2.7/8]
.

(2)

Let
p\geq 7
.
If
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
has signature
(3,1)
then
\textnormal{H}^{1}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda))=0
whenever
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq a_{3}\geq 7
and

(a_{1}-a_{4}-2)+p(a_{4}-a_{3}+6)>0
. If
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
has signature
(2,2)
then we replace the last condition by

(a_{1}-a_{4}-2)+p(a_{3}-a_{2}+2)>0

[
76
]
.

To complete the proof of the entailment we need a way to relate coherent cohomology of
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}}

and mod
p
étale cohomology of
\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
We will work in the Harris–Taylor setting of
Example

2.3.2
with signature
(n-1,1)
, for simplicity.
Also from now on we assume that these satisfy the assumptions of

[
29
, §I.7]
for some auxiliary prime
l
. We will provide a different proof
from the one for
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
in
[
61
, Prop 5.1]
,
using that the Galois representation is contained in the étale cohomology.

Proposition 7.2.4
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
be a dominant weight. Let
E
be some imaginary quadratic number field, and

Sh
the associated compact Harris–Taylor Shimura variety of signature
(n,1)
of
Example

2.3.2
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbb{T}
be a non-Eisenstein generic
maximal ideal. Let
w\in W^{M}
, then

\textnormal{H}^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},V(\lambda)^{\vee}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{n-l(w)}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(w\cdot\lambda)^{\vee})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0.

Similarly,

(7.2.1)

\textnormal{H}^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0,

where
\eta
is the weight of
\Omega^{n}_{\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}}
.
Moreover,
\textnormal{H}^{*}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},V(\lambda)\otimes\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is concentrated
in degree
n
. Further, assuming
2.8.2
(1)
the reverse implications

(7.2.2)

\displaystyle\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0

(7.2.3)

\displaystyle\textnormal{H}^{n}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(-w_{0}\lambda)^{\vee})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0\implies\textnormal{H}^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0

holds for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
.

2.8.2
(1) holds when
p
is split in the CM field. For
n=2
(
7.2.2
)
holds for
\lambda=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in X_{1}(T)
satisfying
a_{1}>a_{2}+2
.

Proof.

The concentration of
étale cohomology in middle degree was proved in
[
13
]
,
in fact under a weaker genericity condition
than the one in
[
28
]
. First we prove the forward implication. By torsion freeness of étale cohomology
we can choose

\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
corresponding to an integral system of Hecke eigenvalues lifting

\mathfrak{m}
. Then under the restriction that
\mathfrak{m}
is non-Eisenstein,

r_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is a summand of

\textnormal{H}^{n}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},V(\lambda)^{\vee}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}})_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
.
This follows from
[
29
, Prop VII.1.8]
. Let
\pi^{\infty}
be a representation of
G(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})

appearing in the étale cohomology, whose eigensystem is

\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}
, and

\pi=\pi^{\infty}\otimes\pi_{\infty}
a choice of automorphic representation of
G(\mathbb{A})
.
For the cited result to apply we need to check that
the Jacquet-Langlands transform of the base change of
\pi
is cuspidal as a representation
of
\textnormal{GL}_{n+1}(\mathbb{A}_{E_{0}})
,
this follows since the Galois representations of Eisenstein forms are known to be reducible.
Moreover, by the étale-crystalline comparison theorem

\rho_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is crystalline with regular Hodge-Tate weights,
so each graded piece of
D_{\textnormal{crys}}(\rho_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}})
is one-dimensional.
By the rational étale-crystalline comparison theorem and the BGG decomposition the graded pieces of

D_{\textnormal{crys}}(\rho_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}})
are labelled by
W^{M}
and they are a summand of

\textnormal{H}^{n-l(w)}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\omega(w\cdot\lambda)^{\vee})_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
for each
w\in W^{M}
.
Therefore,
\textnormal{H}^{n-l(w)}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\omega(w\cdot\lambda)^{\vee})_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\neq 0

for each
w\in W^{M}
.
By rescaling an eigenform in
\textnormal{H}^{n-l(w)}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\omega(w\cdot\lambda)^{\vee})_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}

we can assume that it lies in

\textnormal{H}^{n-l(w)}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}},\omega(w\cdot\lambda)^{\vee})_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
,
and then we reduce mod
p
to obtain

\textnormal{H}^{n-l(w)}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(w\cdot\lambda)^{\vee})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
.
To prove (
7.2.1
), let
\lambda^{\vee}=-w_{0}\lambda
, then in characteristic
0
we have that
\omega(w\cdot\lambda^{\vee})^{\vee}=\omega(-w_{0,M}(w\cdot\lambda^{\vee}))

and
V(\lambda^{\vee})^{\vee}=V(\lambda)
.
For
w=w_{0,M}w_{0}\in W^{M}
of length
n
we can compute that the former is equal to
\omega(\lambda+\eta)
. Therefore, we obtain that

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\neq 0
.

Now we prove the first implication in (
7.2.2
).
The key is that by
Theorem

2.8.3
(1),
\textnormal{H}^{1}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
, so that
we can lift any eigenclass to
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}
, and this space is torsion-free.
Thus,
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}=\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\omega(w_{0,M}w_{0}\cdot\lambda^{\vee})^{\vee})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
.
Then we use the BGG in characteristic
0
and torsion-freeness of étale cohomology
to get that
\textnormal{H}^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},V(\lambda)_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. We get the statement
reducing modulo
p
. The genericity conditions for
n=2,3
are given in

7.2.3
(2). For the second implication in (
7.2.2
), the lifting of

\textnormal{H}^{n}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(-w_{0}\lambda)^{\vee})_{\mathfrak{m}}
to characteristic
0
is immediate, and the torsion-freeness
is given by the vanishing of
\textnormal{H}^{n-1}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(-w_{0}\lambda)^{\vee})_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong\textnormal{H}^{1}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(-w_{0}\lambda+\eta))^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{m^{\prime}}}
. Here we are using that Serre duality is
Hecke equivariant up to a twist
[
8
, Prop 4.2.9]
, and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is that twisted eigensystem,
which is still non-Eisenstein generic.

∎

With that we can exhibit a general method to construct generic weak entailments, which depends on the structure of the irreducible constituents of certain dual Weyl modules.
Then using
7.2.2
we will get a generic weak
entailment for
\textnormal{GL}_{4}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

Theorem 7.2.5
.

Let
\textnormal{Sh}/\mathcal{O}
be a compact unitary Shimura variety of signature
(n-1,1)
with
p
split
in the quadratic imaginary field. Assume that
2.8.2
(2) holds.
Let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be a generic non-Eisenstein maximal ideal
appearing in the cohomology of
Sh
. Let
\lambda,\mu\in X_{1}(T)
such that

\lambda\uparrow_{\delta}\mu
, where
\delta
is the longest root. Assume that there exists some

F(\chi)\in\textnormal{JH}[V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]
such that
F(\chi)\notin\textnormal{JH}[V(\mu)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]
.
Let
\{F(\mu_{i})\}
be the Jordan–Holder factors of
V(\mu)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
.
Then the statement

F(\chi)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(\mu_{i})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\textnormal{ for some }i

holds for generic
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
. Note that
F(\mu_{i})\neq F(\chi)
for all
i
.
The same result holds for
p
inert if
2.8.2
(1) holds.

Proof.

The method is the same as in
[
61
, Thm 5.2]
. By definition
F(\chi)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
implies that

\textnormal{H}^{n-1}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},F(\chi))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. By the concentration of étale cohomology in

7.2.4
and the assumption that
F(\chi)\in\textnormal{JH}[V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]
we obtain

\textnormal{H}^{n-1}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. By

7.2.4
this implies that

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\lambda+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. By
Theorem

5.1.11

and
Theorem

2.8.3
(1)
\theta_{\lambda\uparrow\mu}
is injective
on global sections for generic
\lambda
, so that

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}},\omega(\mu+\eta))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. Applying
7.2.4
in the other direction
yields
\textnormal{H}^{n-1}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},V(\mu)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. Finally, we conclude
from the the decomposition of
V(\mu)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
into irreducible components.
∎

Note that the theorem above is quite robust, and it should extend to more general settings, such as the Siegel case,
where
2.8.2
(2) is known. The only ingredient that we have not written in this generality is

7.2.4
.
In the
\textnormal{GL}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
case we get an unconditional result. Moreover, we also get (assuming the mild
2.8.2
(1))
a generic entailment for the non-split group
GU(4)
with respect to
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}
,
where much less is known in general.

Corollary 7.2.6
.

Let
\textnormal{Sh}/\mathcal{O}
be a compact unitary Shimura variety of signature
(3,1)

with
p
split in the quadratic imaginary field.
Let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be a generic non-Eisenstein maximal ideal.
Then the statement

F(\lambda_{0})\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(\mu)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})

for some
F(\mu)\in\textnormal{JH}[V(\lambda_{5})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]
holds for
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
generic.
Moreover,

F(\mu)\neq F(\lambda_{0})
.
The same result holds for
p
inert if
2.8.2
(1) holds.

Proof.

It follows from
Theorem

7.2.5
. We use as input that
2.8.2
(2) is known in this case by

Theorem

2.8.3
(2), and
7.2.2
for the Jordan–Holder factors of

V(\lambda_{4})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
and
V(\lambda_{5})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. We also use the observation that
\lambda_{4}\uparrow_{\delta}\lambda_{5}
.
∎

Remark 7.2.7
.

Using local arguments,
[
53
]
prove that if the Breuil–Mezard
conjecture is true, then
\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{0})}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{5})}
for generic
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
.
We give a heuristic argument that suggests that
7.2.6
implies that.
The idea is to use Kisin–Taylor–Wiles patching
[
12
]

on the injection
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda_{4}+\eta))\hookrightarrow\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(\lambda_{5}+\eta))

from
Theorem

5.1.11
. One would
still obtain an injection of patched modules
M_{\infty}(\lambda_{4})\hookrightarrow M_{\infty}(\lambda_{5})

over a thickening
R_{\infty}
of the local Galois deformation
R_{\overline{\rho}}
ring for
\overline{\rho}
.
One key ingredient in order to patch coherent cohomology (although this is not written in the literature,
some of the tools can be found in
[
30
]

[
2
]
)
is that both
\omega(\lambda_{i}+\eta)
have coherent cohomology concentrated in degree
0
by
Theorem

2.8.3
.
The heuristic is that by
7.2.4
, and assuming a form of Fontaine-Mazur’s conjecture,

\text{supp}_{R_{\overline{\rho}}}M_{\infty}(\lambda_{i})
should be (set-theoretically)

\mathcal{Z}_{V(\lambda_{i})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\overline{\rho})
.
Then the injection of patched modules would imply that

\mathcal{Z}_{V(\lambda_{4})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\overline{\rho})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{V(\lambda_{5})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}(\overline{\rho})

for every
\overline{\rho}
, so that using
7.2.2
we get that

\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{0})}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{5})}+\sum_{\mu}\mathcal{Z}_{F(\mu)}

where each
\mu
is in the lowest alcove, coming from
7.2.2
.
One can easily discard the possibility that
\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{0})}
is contained in any cycle of a different

C_{0}
weight
by Fontaine–Laffaille theory, so the above would suggest that

\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{0})}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda_{5})},

which agrees with the local predictions/results of
[
53
]
for
\textnormal{GL}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
Moreover, this is the only predicted generic entailment for
\textnormal{GL}_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
Assuming that
2.8.2
holds this heuristic generalizes.
Therefore, given a weak entailment proved as in
7.2.6
, it can help us guess
if there exists an entailment refining it. Indeed, in the
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
case this heuristic also correctly predicts the
entailment predicted by
[
53
]
. In that case, see
[
52
, §4]
for a similar reasoning.

7.3.
Non-generic entailments for
\textnormal{GL}_{3}

Even though there are no generic entailments for
\textnormal{GL}_{3,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
,
we can still obtain some non-generic entailments. We will say that an entailment is non-generic if it holds
for a family of weights which is not generic. Nevertheless, we will give explicit conditions on the weights, which make the
non-genericity clear.
There are two
p
-restricted alcoves: the lowest
alcove
C_{0}=\{(a,b,c):0\leq a-b,b-c;a-c<p-2\}
and the upper alcove

C_{1}=\{(a,b,c):a-b,b-c<p-1;a-c>p-2\}
. For
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
let
\lambda_{1}\in C_{1}
be its corresponding
reflection.

Lemma 7.3.1
.

For
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
,
V(\lambda_{0})=L(\lambda_{0})
. For
\lambda_{1}\in C_{1}
we have an exact sequence

0\to L(\lambda_{1})\to V(\lambda_{1})\to L(\lambda_{0})

If
\lambda\in X_{1}(T)
and is in the boundary of
C_{1}
then
V(\lambda)=L(\lambda)
.

We specify
Theorem

2.8.3
and
Theorem

5.1.11
to the
U(2,1)

p
split case.

Proposition 7.3.2
.

Let
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
be a compact unitary Shimura variety of signature
(2,1)
with
p
split in the quadratic
imaginary field. Then

(1)

If
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a,b,c))\neq 0
then
a\geq b
and
(a-c)+p(b-c)\geq 0
.

(2)

For
p\geq 5
we have
\textnormal{H}^{1}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a,b,c))=0
for
a\geq b\geq c+5
satisfying

p(b-c-4)+(a-c-2)>0
.

(3)

For
\lambda_{0}=(a,b,c)\in C_{0}
the linkage map

\theta_{\lambda_{0}\uparrow\lambda_{1}}:\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a+1,b+1,c-2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(p+c-1,b+1,a-p))_{\mathfrak{m}}

is injective for
\mathfrak{m}
non-Eisenstein.

Proof.

Part
(1)
follows from
[
22
, Thm 5.1.1, Fig 1]
. Part
(2)

is
[
76
, Thm 1.5/6]
. For
(3)
,
let
f
be an element of the kernel, by
Theorem

4.3.2

\theta_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}(f)=0
.
By
4.4.4
we know
H_{\alpha_{1}}\mid\theta_{\alpha_{2}}(f)
.
The resulting weight of
\theta_{\alpha_{2}}(f)/H_{\alpha_{1}}
would not be
M
-dominant, so
\theta_{\alpha_{2}}(f)=0
.
Restricting to
\overline{D}_{\alpha_{2}}
,
and using

Theorem

4.4.2
we get
H_{\alpha_{2}}\mid f
, since
p\nmid a-c+3
. Unless
a=b=c+p-4
, part
(1)

implies that
f=0
. Otherwise,
f/H_{\alpha_{2}}\in\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(c-2,c-2,c-2))
, which only contains
the trivial eigensystem.
∎

Theorem 7.3.3
.

Let
Sh
be a compact unitary Shimura variety of signature
(2,1)
with

p
split in the quadratic imaginary field.
Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a generic non-Eisenstein eigensystem
with associated local Galois representation
\overline{\rho}
.

(1)

Let
\lambda=(a,b,b)\in X_{1}(T)
satisfying
b\geq 5
.
Then
F(\lambda)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(a+p-1,b+p-1,b)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
.

(2)

Let
\lambda=(a,a,b)\in X_{1}(T)
. Then
F(\lambda)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(a+p-1,a,b)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
.

(3)

Let
p\geq 5
. Let
(a,b)
such that
0\leq a-b\leq p-3,b\geq 0

and
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a+1,b+1,b-1))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. Then

F(p+b-1,b,a-p+2)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
and
F(p+a,p+b-1,b)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
.

Proof.

We use that
L(\mu)=V(\mu)
for all
\mu
appearing in the proposition, by
Lemma

7.3.1
.
For
(1)
, by

7.2.4

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a+1,b+1,b-2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
.
Applying
H_{\alpha_{2}}

we get
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a+p,b+p,b-2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
, we conclude by

7.3.2
and

7.2.4
again. For
(2)

by the proof of
7.2.4

\textnormal{H}^{2}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a,b,a))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
.
There is a map

H_{\alpha_{2}}:\textnormal{H}^{2}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a,b,a+p-1))\to\textnormal{H}^{2}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a,b,a))
which is
Serre dual to an injection of global sections, so by the trivial case of
Theorem

6.1.2

it is surjective, so that

\textnormal{H}^{2}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a,b,a+p-1))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. We are using that we can take
H_{\alpha_{2}}

to be of weight
(0,0,1-p)
.
From (
7.2.2
) in
7.2.4
we
deduce that
\textnormal{H}^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\textnormal{Sh},V(a+p-1,a,b)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
.
For part
(3)
, we use that the linkage map
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(a+1,b+1,b-1))\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(\overline{\textnormal{Sh}},\omega(p+b,b+1,a-p))
and
\theta_{\alpha_{2}}
are injective .
These follow as in
7.3.2
(3),
using that
p\nmid a-b+2
. Then the result follows from

7.2.4
and that
L(p+b-1,b,a-p+2)=V(p+b-1,b,a-p+2)
.
∎

One could see part
(3)
as suggesting congruences between a form of non-regular Hodge-Tate weights

\{a+2,b+1,b+1\}
and one of regular Hodge-Tate weights
\{p-b+1,b+1,a-p+2\}
or

\{p+a+2,b+1,a-p+2\}
. However, in general it is not known if the Galois representation
of a form in
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sh}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\omega(a+1,b+1,b-1))
is crystalline of those Hodge-Tate weights.

Remark 7.3.4
.

The local analogue of parts
(1)
and
(2)
at the level of Breuil–Mezard cycles is proved
(without the extra genericity assumption in
(1)
) in upcoming work of Levin, Le, Le Hung and Morra.
They prove that if we define candidate Breuil–Mezard cycles by the property that

\mathcal{Z}_{F(\lambda)}
is its corresponding irreducible component except in the cases

\mathcal{Z}_{F(a+p-1,a,b)}=C_{F(a+p-1,a,b)}+C_{F(a,a,b)}
and
