\mathcal{Z}_{F(a+p-1,b+p-1,b)}=C_{F(a+p-1,b+p-1,b)}+C_{F(a,b,b)}
;
then these cycles
satisfy the Breuil–Mezard
conjecture for Hodge-Tate weights
\{2,1,0\}
and tame types.
Therefore, one can reach almost all possible entailments geometrically.

In general, for
\textnormal{GL}_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
one expects the following ”obvious non-generic entailments”: for
1\leq k\leq n-1

F(\lambda)\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\implies F(\lambda+(p-1,p-1,\ldots,p-1,0,\ldots,0))\in W(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})

where there are
k

(p-1)
s and
\lambda
is of the form
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k+2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
. Using Hasse invariants
and duality for unitary Shimura varieties of different signatures
we can reach those weight increases at the level of coherent cohomology. However, in general
we won’t have
F(\lambda+(p-1,p-1,\ldots,p-1,0,\ldots,0))=V(\lambda+(p-1,p-1,\ldots,p-1,0,\ldots,0))
,
so we can’t deduce the result at the level of Serre weights.

7.4.
A remark on the injectivity of theta linkage maps

The reader might wonder in what generality are theta linkage maps (generically) injective. Let us briefly explain the conjectural picture
in the case of
\textnormal{GSp}_{4}
, which is the simplest case where we don’t understand this for theta linkage maps within the
p
-restricted region (and coherent cohomology in degree
0
).
There are
4

p
-restricted
\rho
-shifted alcoves
C_{i}
for
i=0,1,2,3
, with
C_{0}
being the lowest alcove. Let
\lambda_{0}\in C_{0}
and let
\lambda_{i}\in C_{i}
be its affine Weyl reflections.
Then, we have
3
theta linkage maps in this region:
\theta_{\lambda_{0}\uparrow\lambda_{1}},\theta_{\lambda_{1}\uparrow\lambda_{2}}
, and
\theta_{\lambda_{2}\uparrow\lambda_{3}}
.
For
\theta_{\lambda_{1}\uparrow\lambda_{2}}
,
Theorem

5.1.11
says that it is injective on global sections for generic
\lambda_{0}
.

(1)

We expect, although we cannot prove,
\theta_{\lambda_{0}\uparrow\lambda_{1}}
to be injective on global sections for generic
\lambda_{0}
. This is based on the conjectural generic middle degree concentration for certain spaces
of de Rham cohomology, which we study in forthcoming work
[
63
]
. If
\theta_{\lambda_{0}\uparrow\lambda_{1}}
were not injective its kernel would contribute to degree
2
de Rham cohomology with coefficients
in
L(\lambda_{1})
.

(2)

We know that
\theta_{\lambda_{2}\uparrow\lambda_{3}}
is not injective on global sections, even for generic
\lambda_{0}
. We give two arguments. One is that if that was true, then using the general machinery to produce entailments in

Theorem

7.2.5
we would get a weak entailment between
F(\lambda_{1})
and
F(\lambda_{2})
or
F(\lambda_{3})
, using that in the Grothendieck group

[V(\lambda_{i})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}]=[F(\lambda_{i})]+[F(\lambda_{i-1})]
for
i=2,3
. However, from the local results of
[
53
]
on Breuil–Mezard cycles we know this shouldn’t happen.
Less conjecturally, one can prove that the image of
\theta_{\lambda_{1}\uparrow\lambda_{2}}
on global sections is contained in the kernel of
\theta_{\lambda_{2}\uparrow\lambda_{3}}

after possibly
choosing another non-zero map of Verma modules inducing an operator of the same weight increase
as
\theta_{\lambda_{2}\uparrow\lambda_{3}}
. This is the case because over
\mathcal{F}l

the composition of
\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda_{1}+\eta)\to\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda_{2}+\eta)\to\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda_{3}+\eta)

and
\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda_{1}+\eta)\to\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}s_{\alpha}\cdot\lambda_{1}+\eta)\to\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}\lambda_{3}+\eta)
agree,
but
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}(w_{0,M}s_{\alpha}\cdot\lambda_{1}+\eta)=0
. This commutativity can be seen by
p
-translating a piece of
the BGG complex over
\mathcal{F}l
for a certain
\lambda\in C_{0}
, see
[
62
, Rmk 7.2.0.5]
.
Alternatively, it would follow if we knew that the Hom space of Verma modules is
1
-dimensional.

(3)

One could also ask the question about the behaviour of theta linkage maps in higher cohomology. We currently don’t understand much about these. The two main problems is that higher coherent cohomology is
not understood at all in general, and that restricting to strata becomes a less viable strategy for higher coherent cohomology.
At least in some cases the
conjectures in
[
63
]
can help guide us in this regard.

8.
A de Rham realization functor

For the purposes of our companion paper
[
63
]
on computing de Rham cohomology of
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}^{\textnormal{tor}}
, we describe a functor that computes de Rham cohomology
and whose domain is
D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{P,\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})
. This will allow us to compute generalized BGG decompositions in

[
63
]
.

8.1.
The realization functor

Let
Q\in\{P,B\}
, and let
\mathcal{F}l_{P}=\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
,
\mathcal{F}l_{B}=\mathcal{F}l
. Even though we have mostly focused on Verma modules
it is natural to work in the larger category
\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
of
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules.
As a corollary of
3.1.14
(4) and
Lemma

3.2.1
we get the following.

Proposition 8.1.1
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
and

V\in\mathcal{O}_{Q,R}
. Then
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}=F_{Q}(V^{\vee})
is naturally equipped with a
HPD stratification over
\textnormal{Sh}_{R}
. It extends to a log HPD stratification on
\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}_{R}
.

Let
X/\mathcal{O}
be a smooth proper scheme. Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
and let
X_{n}:=X_{R}
. We will need some important facts about crystals,
see
[
4
]
. We recall that
\mathcal{O}=W(k)
.

Proposition 8.1.2
.

(1)

The abelian category of
\mathcal{O}_{X_{n}}
-modules equipped with a HPD stratification with respect to
R
and

\mathcal{O}_{X_{n}}
-linear maps that respect the HPD stratifications as morphisms
is equivalent
to the category of crystals on
(X_{n}/R)_{\textnormal{crys}}
, the crystalline topos of
X_{n}
with respect to
R
.

(2)

There is an exact functor
L
from the category of
\mathcal{O}_{X_{n}}
-modules (with HPD differential operators with respect
to
R

as morphisms) to the category of crystals on
(X_{n}/R)_{\textnormal{crys}}
. Under the equivalence of
(1)
it is given by
E\mapsto P_{X_{n}}\otimes E

with the HPD stratification of
3.1.14
(3).

(3)

Consider the derived pushforward
Ru_{*}:(X_{n}/R)_{\text{crys}}\to\textnormal{Sh}(X_{n,\text{zar}})

to the topos of Zariski sheaves on
X_{n}
. It satisfies

Ru_{*}L(E)=E
for
E
a
\mathcal{O}_{X_{n}}
-module. Further, if
f:E_{1}\to E_{2}
is a
HPD differential operator with respect to
R
,
then
Ru_{*}[L(E_{1})\xrightarrow{L(f)}L(E_{2})]=[E_{1}\xrightarrow{f}E_{2}]
.

(4)

All the statements above extend to the setup of log-HPD stratifications and log-crystals as in

[
60
, §4]
.

Putting the two propositions together we get the realization functor which we will use to compute de Rham
cohomology of
\overline{\textnormal{Sh}}
.

Theorem 8.1.3
.

Let
R=\mathcal{O}/p^{n}
and
V\in\textnormal{Rep}_{R}(G)
. For a scheme
X/S
let
\mathcal{C}_{X/S}

be the category of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules with
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-linear maps. We can define an exact functor

\Psi_{Q,R}:=Ru_{*}\circ F_{Q}\circ(-)^{\vee}:D^{b}(\mathcal{O}_{Q,R})\to D^{b}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}l_{Q}/R}),

where we are using
8.1.2
(1) and
8.1.1
to make sense of it.
If
C^{\bullet}
is a complex of

(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules which is quasi-isomorphic to the complex
[V]
concentrated in degree
0
,

\Psi_{Q,R}(C^{\bullet})
is quasi-isomorphic to the de Rham complex
\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{F}l_{Q}}
.
Moreover, if each
term
C^{-k}
is an extension of Verma modules
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{i,k})
, then
\Psi_{Q}(C^{\bullet})^{k}
is an
extension of
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{i,k}
, and whenever a map between the graded pieces is defined it is given
by the functor in
Theorem

3.2.4
. The functor
\Psi_{Q,R}
extends to functors
\Psi^{\textnormal{can}}_{Q,R}
and

\Psi^{\textnormal{sub}}_{Q,R}
on toroidal compactifications, satisfying the same properties.

Proof.

By
8.1.1

F_{Q}\circ(-)^{\vee}
sends
C^{\bullet}
to a complex of
crystals on
(\textnormal{Sh}_{R}/R)_{\textnormal{crys}}
. Since it is an exact functor it descends to the respective bounded derived categories, so

F_{Q}(C^{\bullet,\vee})
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{V^{\vee}}
considered as a crystal,
thus it computes
its de Rham cohomology. Also,
\mathcal{V^{\vee}}
is quasi-isomorphic to the linearized de Rham complex

L(\mathcal{V^{\vee}}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{F}l_{Q}})

[
4
, Thm 6.12]
. After applying
Ru_{*}

and
8.1.2
(3) and we get the quasi-isomorphism
in
D(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}l/R})
. For the last statement, we have that

\Psi_{Q,R}
sends
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V)
to the crystal
L(\mathcal{V}^{\vee})
, by
Theorem

3.2.2
(7).
Thus, if
M
is an extension of
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{1})
and
\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}(V_{2})
, then
F_{Q}(M^{\vee})
is an extension of

L(V^{\vee}_{2})
and
L(V^{\vee}_{1})
. After applying

Ru_{*}
and using by
8.1.2
(3) again, it becomes an extension of
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2}

and
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1}
. Finally, for a map
\phi:\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}V_{1}\to\textnormal{Ver}_{Q}V_{2}
, under the isomorphism from

Theorem

3.2.2

F_{Q}(\phi^{\vee}):L(\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{2})\to L(\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{1})

is also identified with
L(\Phi_{P}(\phi))
, by construction of
\Phi_{P}
. Therefore, we conclude by
8.1.2

again.
∎

8.2.
The lowest alcove BGG complex

As an illustration of the realization functor, we reprove the lowest alcove integral BGG decomposition of

[
48
]
and
[
64
]
. Some of our technical inputs are different from theirs, and as opposed to
[
48
]

we exhibit a quasi-isomorphism of the whole de Rham complex, not just of the graded pieces of its Hodge filtration.
Let
\lambda\in C_{0}
. Then the dual Weyl module
V(\lambda)
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
is irreducible.
Consider the standard complex
\text{Std}_{P}(V(\lambda))
for
V(\lambda)\in\textnormal{Rep}_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}(P)

0\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(\bigwedge^{d}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}V(\lambda))\to\ldots\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}((\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{p})\otimes V(\lambda))\to\textnormal{Ver}_{P}V(\lambda)\to 0,

defined as in
[
64
, 2.2]
, where
d
is the dimension of

\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{p}
. It is also
the dual of the de Rham complex of
F_{G/P}(V(\lambda)^{\vee})
over
G/P
, under the equivalence of

Lemma

3.1.18
.
They are complexes of
(U\mathfrak{g},P)
-modules,
exact except at the term
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}V(\lambda)
, which has homology
V(\lambda)
.
Let
W(a)\subset W
be the elements of length
a
. We will need a small lemma, let

W_{\text{ext}}=pX^{*}(T)\rtimes W
be the extended affine Weyl group, containing

W_{\text{aff}}=p\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{+}\rtimes W
. They both act by the dot action on
X^{*}(T)

as in
Section

2.1
. Let
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}
be the

G
-invariant elements under the adjoint action. It is a commutative algebra, and there is
a natural map of algebras
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})^{G}\to(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}
.

Lemma 8.2.1
.

Assume that
p
is greater than the Coxeter number of
G
. Let
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
, and let

\chi_{\lambda}
be the character by which
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}
acts on

\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\lambda)_{\check{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}
. Then

\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)_{\chi_{\lambda}}
is non-zero, and equal to
\textnormal{Ver}_{B}(\mu)
, if and only if

\mu\in W_{\textnormal{ext}}\cdot\lambda
. The same statement holds for

\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(L_{M}(\mu))_{\chi_{\lambda}}
.

Proof.

Under the assumption on
p
we have that
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}
is isomorphic to
S(\mathfrak{h})^{W}
,
where
S(\mathfrak{h})

is the symmetric algebra of the Cartan subalgebra, and the action of
W
is given by the dot action

[
59
]
.
Note that any element of
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}
also acts as
\chi_{\mu}
on
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}(L_{M}(\mu))_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
,
since
L_{M}(\mu)
is cyclic as a
P
-module.
Therefore,
the localization of the Verma modules by
\chi_{\lambda}
are non-zero if
and only if
\chi_{\lambda}=\chi_{\mu}
.
By the Harish-Chandra isomorphism above we have that
\chi_{\lambda}=\chi_{\mu}
if and only if
\lambda\in W\cdot\mu+pX^{*}(T)
, i.e.

\lambda\in W_{\text{ext}}\cdot\mu
.
∎

In all applications we will have
\lambda-\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\Phi^{+}
, so we can replace
the extended affine Weyl group by the affine Weyl group. Also, note that the assumption on
p

is needed for the lowest alcove to be non-empty, so we will often not mention it.
With that we prove the BGG resolution for
\lambda\in C_{0}
in the category of
(U\mathfrak{g},Q)
-modules.
Recall that a weight
\mu\in X^{*}(T)
is
p
-small
if
|\langle\mu+\rho,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle|<p
for all roots
\gamma
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}=W(k^{\prime})
such that
G
splits over
k^{\prime}
.

Theorem 8.2.2
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
in
C_{0}
.
There exists a complex of
(U\mathfrak{g},P)_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}}
-modules
\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda}
, quasi-isomorphically
embedded as a summand
\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda}\subset\textnormal{Std}_{P}(V(\lambda))_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}}
, such that

\textnormal{BGG}^{k}_{P,\lambda}=\oplus_{w\in W(k)\cap W^{M}}\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(w\cdot\lambda)

and the differentials go in the direction
\textnormal{BGG}^{d}_{P,\lambda}\to\textnormal{BGG}^{d-1}_{P,\lambda}
.

Proof.

Let
\chi_{\lambda}
be the character by which
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}

acts on
V(\lambda)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
, since it is an irreducible representation. Then define

\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda}\coloneqq\textnormal{Std}_{Q}(V(\lambda))_{\chi_{\lambda}}

as the isotypic component of
\chi_{\lambda}
. It is a summand of
\textnormal{Std}_{P}(V(\lambda))

since the latter is a direct sum over all the characters of
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}
. The homology
of
\textnormal{Std}_{P}(V(\lambda))
is
V(\lambda)
concentrated in one degree, which is killed by every other
character
\chi\neq\chi_{\lambda}

of
(U\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})^{G}
. Therefore,

\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\textnormal{Std}_{P}(V(\lambda))
.
The rest of
the proposition for
\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda}
is proved in
[
64
, Thm D]
and
[
48
, Thm 5.2]
.
We work with slightly more general groups, but the key input that we need is
Lemma

8.2.1
.
∎

For
\lambda\in C_{0}
we have the identity
V(\lambda)^{\vee}=V(-w_{0}\lambda)
, and

W(\lambda)^{\vee}=W(-w_{0,M}\lambda)
.
Denote
\lambda^{\vee}=-w_{0}\lambda
.
Let
\textnormal{dR}_{\mathcal{F}l}(\lambda)
be the de Rham complex of

\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
. Recall that
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}=W(k^{\prime})
for some
k^{\prime}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that
G
splits over
k^{\prime}
.
Define
\textnormal{BGG}_{\textnormal{Sh}}(\lambda)
as a complex on
\textnormal{Sh}_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}}
obtained
by applying the functor
\Phi_{P}
of

Theorem

3.2.4
to
\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda^{\vee}}
. Note that this is well-defined since
\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda^{\vee}}
is a complex whose terms are direct sums of Verma modules, not
just an element of the derived category.
By
Theorem

8.2.2
, it comes equipped with maps
\textnormal{BGG}_{\textnormal{Sh}}(\lambda)\to\textnormal{dR}_{\textnormal{Sh}}(\lambda)
. This is because
\Psi_{P,W(k^{\prime})/p^{n}}
of the standard complex for
V(\lambda)^{\vee}
is

\textnormal{dR}_{\textnormal{Sh}}(\lambda)_{W(k^{\prime})/p^{n}}
.
Similarly, let
\textnormal{BGG}^{?}_{\textnormal{Sh}}(\lambda)
and
\textnormal{dR}^{?}_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}}(\lambda)
for
?\in\{\textnormal{can},\textnormal{sub}\}

be their (sub)canonical extensions to
\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}
. Both the de Rham and BGG complexes are equipped with
a natural Hodge filtration, as in
[
48
, Def 3.10]
.

Theorem 8.2.3
.

Let
\lambda\in C_{0}
and
?\in\{\textnormal{can},\textnormal{sub}\}
. Then
the map

\textnormal{BGG}^{?}_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}}(\lambda)\to\textnormal{dR}^{?}_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}}(\lambda)

is a quasi-isomorphism of filtered complexes over
\textnormal{Sh}_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}}
. Moreover,
it admits a section.

Proof.

The fact that the base change of the map above to each
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}/p^{n}
is a quasi-isomorphism follows from
Theorem

8.1.3
and
Theorem

8.2.2
,
by the compatibility of the functors
\Phi_{P}
and
\Psi_{P}
. Since both are complexes of vector bundles
it implies that the map over
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
The fact that they are summands follows from functoriality of
Theorem

8.1.3
.
The compatibility with the Hodge filtrations follows in the same way, since as defined on each

D(\mathcal{O}_{Q,\tilde{\mathcal{O}}/p^{n}})

both Hodge filtrations are quasi-isomorphic. Explicitly, for a Verma module
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(\chi)
with
W(\chi)\in\textnormal{Rep}(P)
irreducible,
its
n
-th Hodge filtration is
\textnormal{Ver}_{P}W(\chi)
itself if
\langle\chi,\mu\rangle\leq n
, and otherwise it is zero.
This defines a filtration on
\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda,R}
. On the standard complex we define its Hodge filtration
as the one induced
by the Hodge filtration on
V(\lambda)\otimes\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{p}
.
From its definition the quasi-isomorphism
\textnormal{BGG}_{P,\lambda,\tilde{\mathcal{O}}/p^{n}}\cong\textnormal{Std}_{P}V(\lambda)
preserves these filtrations.
Finally, by the fact that
\Psi_{P,R}\textnormal{Ver}_{P}V=\mathcal{V}^{\vee}[0]
we see that the associated filtration
on
\textnormal{dR}_{\textnormal{Sh}^{\textnormal{tor}}}(\lambda)_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}/p^{n}}=\Psi_{P,\tilde{\mathcal{O}}/p^{n}}\textnormal{Std}_{P}V(\lambda)^{\vee}

is the Hodge filtration induced by Griffiths transversality.
∎
